The Dashwood family had long called Sussex home, residing at Norland Park, a sprawling estate situated squarely within their ancestral lands. For generations, they had maintained a reputation of such high standing that they commanded the warmest regard of everyone in the neighborhood. The late proprietor of Norland was a bachelor who lived to a remarkable age, spending his later years in the devoted company of his sister, who served as both his companion and housekeeper. However, her passing a decade prior had fundamentally altered his domestic life. To fill the void she left, he welcomed into his household the family of his nephew, Mr. Henry Dashwood—the rightful heir to Norland and the beneficiary of his will. Surrounded by his nephew, niece, and grandchildren, the elderly gentleman found his days filled with genuine contentment. The unwavering attentiveness of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Dashwood to his needs, born of kindness rather than mere duty, provided him with profound comfort, while the lively spirits of the children brought a special joy to his existence.

Mr. Henry Dashwood's lineage included one son from a previous marriage and three daughters from his current union. The son, a steady and respectable youth, had already been generously provided for by his mother's substantial fortune, receiving half upon reaching maturity and further augmenting his wealth through his own recent marriage. Consequently, the inheritance of Norland held significantly less weight for him than it did for his sisters. Their financial prospects, aside from whatever portion their father might inherit, were meager; their mother possessed nothing, and their father held only seven thousand pounds personally. Moreover, the remainder of his first wife's fortune was legally secured to her son, leaving him with only a life interest in that capital.

When the old gentleman finally passed away, the reading of his will delivered a mixture of relief and crushing disappointment. Though he was neither unjust nor ungrateful enough to disinherit his nephew entirely, he attached conditions that effectively halved the value of the bequest. Mr. Dashwood had coveted the estate primarily for the sake of his wife and daughters, yet the will stipulated that the property pass directly to his son and grandson—a toddler of four—to prevent any encroachment on the boy's security. This arrangement stripped Mr. Dashwood of the power to provide for those dearest to him, prohibiting any charges against the estate or the sale of its valuable timberlands. The entire property was thus locked away for the benefit of the child, who, during occasional visits to Norland, had managed to charm his uncle with the irresistible allure typical of toddlers aged two or three: broken speech, stubborn demands, mischievous antics, and endless noise. These traits ultimately outweighed the years of devotion shown to him by his aunt and cousins.

Despite these constraints, Mr. Dashwood intended no cruelty. As a token of his affection for the three girls, he bequeathed them one thousand pounds apiece. Initially, Mr. Dashwood's despair was acute, but his naturally cheerful and optimistic temperament allowed him to hope that, by living frugally, he could accumulate a significant sum from the estate's existing resources and potential improvements. Tragically, this fortune proved fleeting. He survived his uncle for barely twelve months, dying just as his health began to fail. Upon his deathbed, summoning his son, Mr. John Dashwood, he urged him with all the urgency his failing strength permitted to ensure the welfare of his mother-in-law and sisters.

Mr. John Dashwood lacked the deep emotional resonance felt by the rest of the family, yet the gravity of his father's plea moved him. He promised to use every means at his disposal to secure their comfort. Satisfied by this assurance, Mr. Dashwood passed peacefully, leaving behind only ten thousand pounds—including the legacies—for his widow and daughters. Meanwhile, Mr. John Dashwood retreated into quiet contemplation regarding the extent of his ability to assist them. Not inherently malicious, though perhaps somewhat cold-hearted and self-regarding, he was a man of propriety and respectability. Had he married a woman of greater warmth, he might have become more endearing; instead, he wedded a woman who was a stark mirror of his own limitations—narrow-minded and equally selfish.

Mrs. John Dashwood had harbored no particular affection for her husband's family until this crisis revealed her true character. While Mr. John had envisioned presenting his widowed mother and sisters with three thousand pounds—a sum he believed he could easily spare given his projected annual income of four thousand plus his share of his mother's fortune—he found his resolve crumbling under the reality of the situation. Yet, before his father's funeral concluded, Mrs. John Dashwood arrived at Norland with her child and retinue, making no prior announcement of her intent. Though legally entitled to enter the house now that it belonged to her husband, her abrupt appearance was a breach of etiquette that would have caused offense to anyone with ordinary feelings. For Mrs. Dashwood, however, possessing a romanticized sense of honor and a fierce, albeit misguided, generosity, such rudeness was an insurmountable affront.

Though never a favorite among her in-laws, Mrs. John Dashwood had previously lacked the opportunity to demonstrate her lack of consideration. Her behavior stung Mrs. Dashwood profoundly, sparking a desire to abandon the house forever. Only the gentle persuasion of her eldest daughter, Elinor, tempered this impulse. Elinor, despite being only nineteen, possessed a sharp intellect and calm judgment that guided her mother wisely. Possessing a noble heart and strong affections, Elinor learned to govern her emotions—a lesson her mother had yet to master, and which her youngest sister, Marianne, explicitly rejected.

Marianne matched Elinor in intelligence and capability but differed radically in temperament. Eager in all things, her sorrows and joys knew no bounds. Generous, charming, and captivating, she lacked prudence entirely, bearing a striking resemblance to her mother. Elinor observed her sister's overwhelming sensitivity with concern, whereas Mrs. Dashwood cherished it. Together, mother and daughter indulged their grief, voluntarily renewing their anguish and refusing any form of consolation. They immersed themselves in their sorrow, finding new depths of misery in every reflection, vowing never to accept comfort again.

Elinor, though deeply afflicted, retained the strength to endure. She consulted with her brother, greeted his wife with dignity, and strove to encourage her mother toward similar restraint. The third sister, Margaret, was a cheerful and well-meaning girl of thirteen, though she had absorbed much of Marianne's romanticism without acquiring her sister's sense, suggesting she would not match her older siblings' maturity in the years ahead.

Mrs. John Dashwood now settled into her role as the mistress of Norland Park, relegating her mother and her sisters-in-law to the status of mere visitors. Yet, despite this shift in hierarchy, she treated them with a quiet, surface-level civility, while her husband offered them a kindness reserved for anyone other than himself, his wife, or their infant son. He earnestly urged them to regard Norland as their permanent home. Since no alternative seemed more suitable to Mrs. Dashwood than staying put until she could find a residence nearby, she graciously accepted his invitation. Remaining in a place saturated with memories of past joy was precisely what soothed her spirit. In moments of mirth, her temperament was as buoyant as any, brimming with that sanguine anticipation of happiness that often *is* happiness itself. Conversely, in times of sorrow, she was swept away by her imagination just as easily, finding herself as distant from consolation as she was from alloy in her pleasures.

However, Mrs. John Dashwood harbored deep reservations regarding her husband's intention to aid his sisters. To withdraw three thousand pounds from the inheritance of their beloved little boy would, in her view, render him destitute to a terrifying degree. She implored him to reconsider. How could he reconcile robbing his sole offspring of such a substantial sum? Furthermore, what conceivable claim did the Miss Dashwoods hold against his generosity? They were related to him only by half-blood, a connection she deemed virtually non-existent. It was common knowledge that no affection was presumed to exist between children born of different marriages; thus, why should he ruin himself and their poor little Harry by squandering all his wealth on his "half-sisters"?

"It was my father's final request," her husband replied solemnly, "that I should assist his widow and daughters."

"He was likely speaking in a fog," she countered sharply. "Ten to one he was light-headed at the time. Had he possessed his faculties, he could not possibly have imagined asking you to give away half your fortune from your own child."

"He made no specific stipulation for a sum, my dear Fanny," he explained gently. "He merely asked, in general terms, that I assist them and improve their circumstances as far as my ability allowed. Perhaps it would have been wiser had he left the matter entirely to my discretion; he could hardly have anticipated that I would neglect them. Nevertheless, since he secured my promise, I felt bound to fulfill it. Something must be arranged for them once they depart Norland and establish a new household."

"Very well, then," she conceded reluctantly, "let something be done. But let us agree that 'something' does not necessitate three thousand pounds. Consider, my dear, that once money is parted with, it returns to no one. Your sisters will inevitably marry, and the funds will vanish forever. Unless..."

"Why, certainly," interrupted her husband, with grave deliberation, "the outcome would differ vastly if the sum remained with our poor little boy. There may come a time when Harry regrets having surrendered such a large portion. Should he eventually raise a numerous family, for instance, it would prove a remarkably convenient addition to their resources."

"To be sure, it would."

"Perhaps, then, it would serve all parties better if we reduced the sum by half. Five hundred pounds would constitute a prodigious windfall for their fortunes!"

"Oh! Beyond anything great!" she exclaimed. "What brother on earth would offer half so much to his sisters, even if they were truly his sisters? And considering they are only half-related—"

"But you possess such a generous spirit!" he interjected warmly.

"I would not wish to act meanly," he continued. "On such matters, one prefers to err on the side of excess rather than deficiency. Surely no one believes I have fallen short; indeed, even they could hardly expect more."

"There is no predicting what *they* might expect," the lady remarked coolly. "Yet, we are not concerned with their hopes, but with what you can realistically afford."

"Certainly. And I believe I can afford to grant them five hundred pounds apiece. As it stands, without my contribution, they will each inherit approximately three thousand pounds upon their mother's passing—a very respectable fortune for any young woman."

"To be sure," she agreed. "In fact, it strikes me that they require no augmentation whatsoever. With ten thousand pounds divided among them, they will assuredly fare well if married, or live comfortably together on the interest if unmarried."

"That is accurate," Mr. Dashwood admitted. "Therefore, I fail to see why it would be more prudent to provide for their mother during her lifetime rather than for the daughters directly—I refer specifically to an annuity. A hundred pounds a year would render them all perfectly content."

His wife paused, weighing the proposal. "Admittedly," she said slowly, "it is preferable to parting with fifteen hundred pounds outright. However, if Mrs. Dashwood survives fifteen years, we shall be thoroughly ensnared."

"Fifteen years! My dear Fanny, her life is unlikely to extend even half that duration."

"Certainly not; yet, observe, people invariably seem immortal when an annuity is involved. She is robust, healthy, and barely forty. An annuity is a binding commitment; it recurs annually, offering no escape. You underestimate the gravity of such an arrangement. I have witnessed firsthand the turmoil annuities can cause. My mother was burdened by similar obligations to three elderly servants via my father's will. It was exhausting. Payments were due twice yearly, requiring constant travel to deliver the funds. Then came the deception: one servant would be reported dead, only to resurface later. My mother grew utterly weary of it. She lamented that her income was no longer her own, shackled by perpetual claims. It was particularly cruel, she noted, because otherwise, the funds would have been entirely at her disposal without restriction. Consequently, I harbor such an aversion to annuities that I would not accept one under any circumstance."

"It is undeniably distressing," Mr. Dashwood concurred, "to face such annual drains on one's income. As your mother rightly observed, one's fortune ceases to be truly one's own. Being tethered to the rigid schedule of payments, devoid of flexibility, strips away one's independence."

"Exactly. Moreover, there is rarely gratitude to be gained. They assume security; you perform merely what is expected, eliciting no real thankfulness. If I were you, I would exercise complete discretion. I would not bind myself to a yearly allowance. It may become inconvenient in certain years to spare a hundred, or even fifty pounds from our own necessities."

"I believe you are correct, my love," he yielded. "It is superior to provide occasional gifts rather than a fixed annuity. Were they assured of a steady income, they would simply elevate their standard of living, gaining no actual financial advantage by year's end. A sporadic gift of fifty pounds prevents immediate distress and, I trust, sufficiently honors my father's wish."

"To be sure," she affirmed. "Frankly, I am convinced your father never envisioned you providing significant sums. The assistance he contemplated was likely limited to reasonable neighborly duties: securing a modest cottage, aiding with moving, and sending seasonal provisions like fish and game. I wager my life he intended nothing further; it would indeed be bizarre and unreasonable if he did. Consider, my dear Mr. Dashwood, how comfortably your mother-in-law and her daughters can subsist on the interest of seven thousand pounds, plus the five hundred pounds generated by the individual legacies of the girls (fifty pounds each). Altogether, they command five hundred pounds annually. What earthly necessity could four women have beyond that? They will live frugally. No carriages, no horses, perhaps few servants, no social engagements, and consequently, no expenses. Imagine their comfort! Five hundred a year! I cannot fathom how they would spend half of it. To suggest you give more is absurd. They will be far more capable of providing for *you*."

"Upon my word," Mr. Dashwood declared, "I believe you are entirely correct. My father surely meant nothing more than you describe. I now fully comprehend his intent, and I shall strictly fulfill my engagement through the acts of kindness you have outlined. Once your mother relocates, I shall gladly assist in accommodating her to the best of my ability. Additionally, a modest gift of furniture may prove welcome."

"Certainly," Mrs. John Dashwood replied. "However, one crucial detail must be addressed. When your parents moved to Norland, although the Stanhill furniture was sold, all the china, silverware, and linens were preserved and left to your mother. Thus, her new home will be nearly fully furnished immediately upon her arrival."

"That is a significant factor," he acknowledged. "A valuable legacy indeed! Though, some of the silverware would have been a delightful addition to our own collection here."

"Yes; and the breakfast service alone is twice as exquisite as ours. Far too ornate, in my humble opinion, for any establishment they can ever afford. Nevertheless, such is the case. Your father thought solely of *them*. I must add, however, that you owe neither special gratitude nor strict adherence to his wishes. We both know that if he had possessed the means, he would have bequeathed almost everything in the world to them."

This argument proved irrefutable. It supplied the decisive resolve lacking previously, leading him to conclude that it would be not only unnecessary but potentially indecorous to provide the widow and her children with more than the neighborly gestures suggested by his wife.

Mrs. Dashwood stayed at Norland Manor for several months. She did not linger out of reluctance to leave; rather, she waited until the sharp sting of grief dulled enough for her to bear the sight of familiar places without being overwhelmed by fresh waves of sorrow. Once her spirits began to lift and her mind sought relief from the paralyzing weight of memory, she grew impatient to depart. She was eager to find a new home nearby, for moving far away from the place where her late husband had breathed his last was unthinkable.

Yet, despite her earnest search, she could find no dwelling that satisfied everyone. There was no house that perfectly balanced her desire for comfort and ease with her eldest daughter's prudent caution. Several properties Mrs. Dashwood admired were deemed too grand for their reduced income by Marianne, while others her mother preferred lacked the necessary charm.

Throughout this uncertainty, Mrs. Dashwood held onto a specific hope: the solemn promise made by her husband regarding a legacy left to them by his son, John. Though she had long suspected that seven thousand pounds—a sum Mr. Dashwood believed generous—was insufficient to maintain true affluence for three women, she clung to the idea that it would suffice for their survival. More importantly, she cherished the belief that John Dashwood cared deeply for his siblings' welfare. His attentive behavior toward his widowed mother and sisters had gradually dispelled her earlier contempt for his wife, Mrs. John Dashwood. While Mrs. John's character remained flawed in Mrs. Dashwood's eyes, the prospect of living near her children offered a solace that outweighed social friction.

A particularly fortifying circumstance emerged: a growing attachment between Elinor, the eldest daughter, and Edward Ferrars. Introduced shortly after his sister Fanny moved to Norland, Edward had become a constant presence in the household. He was a gentleman of pleasing demeanor, though not strikingly handsome. His manners were initially reserved, requiring familiarity to reveal the warmth beneath. However, once his natural shyness receded, he displayed an open, affectionate nature. His understanding was sound, refined by a solid education, and his heart proved steady and kind.

To Mrs. Dashwood, these qualities were paramount. Unlike many mothers who might encourage the match solely for financial security—or discourage it due to the precarious nature of Edward's inheritance, which rested entirely on his mother's will—Mrs. Dashwood was guided purely by affection. She saw no barrier in their differing fortunes if their hearts aligned. Indeed, she found it inconceivable that anyone who truly knew Elinor's merit would fail to appreciate her.

Edward's lack of ambition further endeared him to her. While his mother and sister dreamed of seeing him rise to prominence—perhaps entering Parliament or associating with the elite—Edward possessed no such aspirations. He found joy only in domestic tranquility and private life. His younger brother, Henry, showed more promise in worldly affairs, but Edward contentedly remained rooted in simplicity.

Initially, Mrs. Dashwood paid little heed to Edward amidst her profound mourning, noticing only his quiet unobtrusiveness. It was Elinor who drew her mother's full attention to him one day, highlighting the stark contrast between Edward and his sister. "It is enough," Elinor remarked, "to say that he is unlike Fanny. That alone implies everything amiable."

"I love him already," Mrs. Dashwood declared warmly.

"You will like him when you know him better," Elinor suggested gently.

"Like him!" her mother smiled, shaking her head. "I feel no sentiment short of love."

"You may merely esteem him."

"I have never known how to separate esteem from love," Mrs. Dashwood replied.

Determined to know him fully, Mrs. Dashwood cultivated an acquaintance with Edward. Her engaging manner quickly melted his reserve, allowing her to discern his genuine worth. Even his quiet demeanor, which previously seemed lacking in youthful vigor, became endearing once she understood the depth of his affection. Upon perceiving clear signs of his devotion to Elinor, she viewed their union as inevitable, eagerly anticipating the day they would marry.

"In a few months, my dear Marianne," she told her youngest daughter, "Elinor will likely be settled for life. We shall miss her dearly, but she will be happy. You will gain a brother—a real, affectionate brother. I hold the highest opinion of Edward's heart."

Marianne, however, listened with a furrowed brow. "Perhaps," she admitted, "I view it with some surprise. Edward is undeniably amiable, and I love him tenderly. Yet... he lacks something. His figure is not striking; it lacks the grace I expect in a man destined to captivate my sister. His eyes lack that spark, that fire which announces both virtue and intelligence."

She paused, her voice trembling slightly. "Furthermore, I fear he possesses no real taste. Music barely attracts him. While he praises Elinor's drawings, it is the admiration of a lover, not a connoisseur. He knows nothing of art, yet attends her diligently. To satisfy me, a partner must share my soul. The same books, the same music must charm us both. I cannot be happy with a man whose tastes do not mirror my own. He must enter into every feeling I possess."

Recalling a recent evening, Marianne continued, "How spiritless and tame was Edward's manner when he read to us last night! I felt for my sister acutely. Yet she bore it with such composure, seemingly unaware of the dullness. I could hardly sit still. To hear those beautiful lines, often driving me wild, delivered with such impenetrable calmness and indifference!"

"He would certainly have done more justice to simple, elegant prose," Mrs. Dashwood interjected. "But you insisted on giving him Cowper."

"Nay, Mama, if he cannot be animated by Cowper! But we must allow for differences in taste. Elinor does not share my intense feelings, so she may overlook it and remain happy. But had I loved him, hearing him read with such insensibility would have broken my heart. Mama, the more I learn of the world, the more convinced I am that I shall never find a man worthy of my love. I require so much! He must possess all of Edward's virtues, yet his person and manners must adorn his goodness with every possible charm."

Mrs. Dashwood placed a comforting hand on her daughter's shoulder. "Remember, my love, you are not yet seventeen. It is too early to despair of such happiness. Why should your destiny differ from mine? In one respect only, Marianne, may your fate be different from yours."

"What a pity it is, Elinor," said Marianne, "that Edward should have no taste for drawing."

"No taste for drawing!" replied Elinor. "Why should you think so? He does not draw himself, indeed, but he takes great pleasure in viewing the works of others, and I assure you he is by no means deficient in natural taste, though he lacks the opportunity to refine it. If he had ever been able to study, I believe he would have become quite proficient. He simply trusts his own judgment so little in these matters that he is invariably reluctant to offer an opinion on any painting; yet, he possesses an innate propriety and simplicity of taste that generally guides him perfectly aright."

Marianne, fearing she might offend, fell silent on the subject. However, the sort of approval Elinor claimed Edward felt toward other artists' work was worlds away from the rapturous delight that, in Marianne's view, constituted true taste. Still, though she smiled inwardly at her sister's error, she honored Marianne for the blind devotion to Edward that caused it.

"I hope, Marianne," continued Elinor, "you do not consider him lacking in general taste. Indeed, I daresay you cannot, for your behavior toward him is perfectly cordial. Were that not the case, I am certain you could never treat him with civility."

Marianne hardly knew what to reply. She would not wound her sister's feelings on any account, yet stating what she did not truly believe was impossible. Finally, she answered: "Do not be offended, Elinor, if my praise of him falls short of your assessment of his merits. I have not had as many opportunities to gauge the finer nuances of his mind, his inclinations, and his tastes as you have; but I hold the highest opinion in the world of his goodness and sense. I consider him everything that is worthy and amiable."

"I am sure," replied Elinor, with a smile, "that his dearest friends could not be dissatisfied with such commendation. I do not see how you could express yourself more warmly."

Marianne was delighted to find her sister so easily pleased.

"As for his sense and his goodness," continued Elinor, "no one can possibly be in doubt regarding them who has spent enough time engaging him in open conversation. The excellence of his understanding and his principles can be hidden only by that shyness which too often renders him silent. You know him well enough to appreciate his solid worth. As for those 'finer nuances,' as you term them, you have, due to peculiar circumstances, remained more ignorant of them than I have. He and I have occasionally spent considerable time together, whereas you have been entirely absorbed, on the most affectionate grounds, by my mother. I have observed him closely, studied his sentiments, and listened to his opinions on subjects of literature and taste. Upon the whole, I venture to declare that his mind is well-informed, his enjoyment of books profound, his imagination lively, his observations just and correct, and his taste delicate and pure. His abilities in every respect grow more impressive with familiarity, just as his manners and appearance do. At first glance, his address is certainly not striking, and his features can hardly be called handsome until one perceives the uncommon goodness of his eye expression and the general sweetness of his countenance. Now that I know him so well, I think him truly handsome—or at least, almost so. What say you, Marianne?"

"I shall very soon think him handsome, Elinor, if I do not already. When you tell me to love him as a brother, I shall no more see imperfection in his face than I now do in his heart."

Elinor started at this declaration and regretted the warmth she had inadvertently shown in speaking of him. She felt that Edward held a very high place in her estimation. She believed the regard to be mutual, yet she required greater certainty before finding Marianne's conviction of their attachment agreeable. She knew that with Marianne and her mother, what was wished for one moment was believed the next; for them, to wish was to hope, and to hope was to expect.

She endeavored to explain the reality of the situation to her sister. "I do not attempt to deny," she said, "that I think very highly of him—that I greatly esteem, and that I like him."

Here, Marianne burst forth with indignation. "Esteem him! Like him! Cold-hearted Elinor! Oh! Worse than cold-hearted! Ashamed of being otherwise! Use those words again, and I will leave the room this very instant."

Elinor could not help laughing. "Excuse me," she said. "Be assured that I meant no offense to you by speaking, in such a quiet manner, of my own feelings. Believe them to be stronger than I have declared; believe them, in short, to be such as his merit, and the suspicion—and perhaps the hope—of his affection for me may warrant, without imprudence or folly. But go no further than this in your belief. I am by no means assured of his regard for me. There are moments when the extent of it seems doubtful; and until his sentiments are fully known, you cannot wonder at my wishing to avoid encouraging my own partiality by believing or calling it more than it is. In my heart, I feel little—scarcely any—doubt of his preference. But there are other points to consider besides his inclination. He is very far from being independent. What his mother truly is we cannot know; but from Fanny's occasional mentions of her conduct and opinions, we have never been inclined to think her amiable. And I am very much mistaken if Edward is not himself aware that there would be many difficulties in his path if he were to wish to marry a woman who lacked either a great fortune or high rank."

Marianne was astonished to discover how much the imaginations of her mother and herself had outrun the truth.

"And you really are not engaged to him!" she exclaimed. "Yet it certainly soon will happen. But two advantages will arise from this delay. *I* shall not lose you so soon, and Edward will have greater opportunity to improve that natural taste for your favorite pursuit, which must be indispensable to your future happiness. Oh! If he should be so far stimulated by your genius as to learn to draw himself, how delightful it would be!"

Elinor had given her true opinion to her sister. She could not consider Marianne's partiality for Edward in such a prosperous state as Marianne believed. There were, at times, a lack of spirits about him which, if it did not indicate indifference, spoke of something almost as unpromising. A doubt of her regard, assuming he felt it, need not trouble him more than mere anxiety. It would not likely produce that deep dejection of mind that frequently attended him. A more reasonable cause might be found in his dependent situation, which forbade the indulgence of his affection. She knew that his mother neither conducted herself in a way to make his current home comfortable nor to give him any assurance that he might build a home for himself without strictly adhering to her views for his advancement. With such knowledge, it was impossible for Elinor to feel at ease. She was far from relying on the outcome of his preference for her, which her mother and sister still considered certain. Nay, the longer they remained together, the more doubtful the nature of his regard appeared; and sometimes, for a few painful minutes, she believed it to be nothing more than friendship.

But whatever its true limits might be, it was enough, once perceived by his sister, to make her uneasy, and at the same time—which was even more common—to make her uncivil. Seizing the first opportunity, she affronted her mother-in-law on the matter, speaking to her so expressively of her son's great expectations, of Mrs. Ferrars's resolution that both her sons should marry well, and of the dangers facing any young woman who attempted to *draw him in*, that Mrs. Dashwood could neither pretend to be unaware nor strive to remain calm. She gave an answer that marked her contempt and instantly left the room, resolving that, regardless of the inconvenience or expense of such a sudden move, her beloved Elinor should not be exposed another week to such insinuations.

In this state of spirits, a letter arrived from the post, containing a proposal particularly well-timed. It was an offer of a small house on very favorable terms, belonging to a relative of her own, a gentleman of consequence and property in Devonshire. The letter came directly from him and was written in the true spirit of friendly accommodation. He understood she was in need of a dwelling; although the house he now offered was merely a cottage, he assured her that everything necessary would be done to it if the location pleased her. He earnestly urged her, along with her daughters, to visit Barton Park, his own residence, from where she could judge for herself whether Barton Cottage, since the houses were in the same parish, could, through alterations, be made comfortable for her. He seemed genuinely anxious to accommodate them, and the entire letter was written in such a friendly style that it could not fail to please his cousin, especially at a moment when she was suffering under the cold and unfeeling behavior of her closer relations.

She needed no time for deliberation or inquiry. Her resolution was formed as she read. The situation of Barton, in a county so far distant from Sussex as Devonshire, which but a few hours prior would have been a sufficient objection to outweigh every possible advantage, became its first recommendation. To quit the neighborhood of Norland was no longer an evil; it was an object of desire

As soon as her reply was sent, Mrs. Dashwood delighted in sharing the news with her son-in-law and daughter-in-law: she had secured a new residence and would not trouble them for long until her new home was ready. They listened with astonishment. Mrs. John Dashwood remained silent, while her husband politely expressed hope that she would not settle too far from Norland. With great satisfaction, she replied that she was moving to Devonshire.

Upon hearing this, Edward turned quickly toward her. His voice, filled with surprise and deep concern, needed no translation for her ears as he asked, "Devonshire! Are you truly going there? That is so distant! And to which part?" She explained the location: just four miles north of Exeter. "It is merely a cottage," she continued, "yet I hope to welcome many friends there. A couple of rooms can easily be added, and if my friends find no hardship traveling so far to visit me, I am certain I shall find none in hosting them." She ended with a warm invitation for both Mr. and Mrs. John Dashwood to come stay at Barton, extending one even more tender specifically to Edward. Although her recent talk with her daughter-in-law had resolved her to leave Norland as soon as necessary, it did not alter her feelings regarding the matter that mattered most. Separating Edward and Elinor was never her intention; instead, she wanted to demonstrate to Mrs. John Dashwood, through this pointed invitation to her brother, how completely she ignored her disapproval of the marriage.

Mr. John Dashwood repeatedly told his mother how deeply sorry he was that she had chosen a house so remote that it prevented him from assisting with the move of her belongings. He genuinely felt a pang of conscience about the situation, for the very effort he had promised his father to undertake was now rendered impossible by these arrangements. Their furniture was transported entirely by water, consisting mainly of household linens, silverware, china, books, and a fine piano belonging to Marianne. As the packages departed, Mrs. John Dashwood sighed; she could not suppress the thought that it was unfair for Mrs. Dashwood's modest income to allow her to keep such elegant items compared to their own wealth.

Mrs. Dashwood rented the cottage for a year; it was fully furnished, allowing her immediate occupancy. There was no friction between the parties regarding the terms. She simply awaited the clearing out of Norland and the organization of her new household before departing for the west. This process, undertaken with remarkable speed given her intense interest, was completed swiftly. The carriage her husband had left her had been sold shortly after his passing, and seeing an opportunity to dispose of it, she agreed to sell it as well, following the earnest counsel of her eldest daughter. Had she acted solely on her own desires for her children's comfort, she would have retained it, but Elinor's prudence prevailed. Furthermore, Elinor wisely restricted their staff to three people: two maids and a man, whom they quickly hired from among those who had previously served at Norland.

The man and one maid were dispatched immediately to Devonshire to prepare the house for their mistress's arrival. Since Lady Middleton was entirely unfamiliar to Mrs. Dashwood, she preferred traveling straight to the cottage rather than stopping as a guest at Barton Park. Relying unquestioningly on Sir John's description of the property, she felt no urge to inspect it personally until she stepped inside as its owner. Her eagerness to leave Norland was undiminished by the visible relief shown by her daughter-in-law at the prospect of the move—a relief that was only weakly masked behind a chilly suggestion that Mrs. Dashwood delay her departure.

Now was the moment when her son-in-law's promise to his father could rightfully be honored. Having neglected to fulfill it upon first taking up residence at the estate, their imminent departure seemed the perfect occasion to make good on it. However, Mrs. Dashwood soon abandoned any hope of receiving such aid. From the general tenor of his conversations, she became convinced that his assistance would extend no further than providing maintenance for six months at Norland. He spoke constantly of rising household expenses and the endless demands placed on the purse of any man of standing, suggesting he stood in need of funds himself rather than having designs on giving them away.

Within a few weeks of receiving Sir John Middleton's first letter, everything was arranged for their new life, enabling Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters to begin their journey. Many tears were shed during their final farewells to a place so dearly loved. "Dear, dear Norland!" cried Marianne as she wandered alone before the house on the last evening of their stay. "When shall I cease to regret you? When shall I learn to feel at home elsewhere? Oh, happy house, could you know the suffering I endure now viewing you from this spot, from where I may never look upon you again! And you, ye familiar trees! Yet you will remain unchanged. No leaf will wither because we depart, nor any branch grow still though we can no longer watch them. You will persist, unaware of the joy or sorrow you inspire, indifferent to the changes in those who once walked beneath your shade! But who will remain to cherish you?"

The initial leg of their journey was cast in such a shadow of gloom that it felt interminable and dreary. Yet, as they neared their destination, the anticipation of inhabiting a new land began to lift their spirits. Upon entering Barton Valley, the landscape offered a balm to their sorrow; it was a verdant, fertile expanse, lush with trees and rich grazing lands. After winding through the valley for over a mile, they finally arrived at their home.

Barton Cottage possessed a modest charm. Its entire frontage consisted of a small, grassy courtyard, accessed by a tidy wicket gate. Though the structure itself was compact and cozy, it lacked the rustic character of a true cottage. It was too orderly—the roof was tiled rather than thatched, the window shutters remained unpainted, and the walls were bare of climbing honeysuckle. A narrow corridor cut straight through the center of the house, leading directly to the garden behind. Flanking the entrance were two sitting rooms, each roughly sixteen feet square, with the offices and staircase situated beyond. The remaining space comprised four bedrooms and two attic rooms. Built relatively recently and kept in excellent condition, the house stood in stark contrast to the grandeur of Norland. It was undeniably smaller and poorer—but as they stepped inside, the tears summoned by painful memories quickly evaporated. The cheerful greetings of the servants warmed their hearts, and driven by mutual affection, both sisters resolved to project an air of happiness.

It was early September, and the weather was splendid. Seeing their new surroundings bathed in sunshine instantly fostered a favorable impression, a psychological boost that proved invaluable in helping them accept their fate. The location was picturesque: high hills rose immediately behind the cottage, flanked on either side by open downs and cultivated woodlands. The village of Barton sprawled across one of these slopes, offering a delightful vista from the cottage's windows. The view ahead was even more expansive, commanding the entire valley and stretching toward distant horizons. While the surrounding hills enclosed the valley in that direction, the land branched out again further downstream, cutting between two particularly steep peaks.

Mrs. Dashwood was largely pleased with the house's size and furnishings. Although her accustomed lifestyle meant many refinements were necessary, she found the act of improvement deeply satisfying. Moreover, she possessed sufficient liquid assets to furnish the apartments with greater elegance should the need arise. "As for the house itself," she remarked, "it is certainly too small for our family, but we shall manage comfortably for now, especially since it is far too late in the year for major renovations. Perhaps come spring, if my finances allow—and I daresay they will—we can consider expansion. These parlors are both too cramped for the gatherings of friends I hope to host frequently. I am thinking of merging the central passage with one of the rooms, perhaps incorporating part of the other, leaving the remainder as a proper entrance. Add a new drawing-room, a bedroom, and an attic above, and we shall have a very snug little cottage. I would also like to beautify the stairs, though one cannot expect perfection everywhere. Still, widening them should prove straightforward. Come spring, I shall assess exactly where I stand financially, and we can plan our enhancements accordingly."

For the interim, until these alterations could be funded from the meager savings of a five-hundred-pound-a-year income—a sum accumulated by a woman who had never previously known thrift—they wisely chose contentment. Each sister busied herself organizing her personal affairs, arranging books and cherished belongings to transform the empty spaces into a home. Marianne carefully unpacked and positioned her pianoforte, while Elinor pinned her sketches to the walls of their sitting room.

These domestic pursuits were abruptly interrupted shortly after breakfast the following morning by the arrival of their landlord. He came to welcome them to Barton and to pledge every convenience available from his own estate to supplement any deficiencies in theirs. Sir John Middleton was a handsome man of about forty. Though he had once visited Stanhill, the intervening years had faded from the memory of his younger cousins. His countenance radiated good humor, and his mannerisms matched the warm tone of his correspondence. Their presence clearly delighted him; their comfort appeared to be a genuine concern. He spoke passionately of his desire for close social ties with his family and urged them so warmly to dine daily at Barton Park until they were fully settled that, despite his persistence bordering on impertinence, they could hardly take offense. His kindness extended beyond words. Within an hour of his departure, a large basket brimming with garden produce and fruit arrived from the park, followed later that same day by a gift of game. Furthermore, he insisted on personally delivering and collecting their mail, refusing to relinquish the pleasure of sending them his daily newspaper.

Lady Middleton conveyed a courteous message via Sir John, indicating her intent to pay her respects as soon as she knew her visit would cause no inconvenience. Responding with equal politeness, an invitation was issued, and the next day she was formally introduced to the sisters. Naturally, the Dashwoods were eager to meet the woman whose influence on their comfort in Barton would be paramount. Her elegant appearance exceeded their expectations. Lady Middleton was barely twenty-seven; her face was beautiful, her figure tall and imposing, and her bearing graceful. Her manners possessed all the refinement her husband lacked. However, they might have benefited from a touch of his frankness and warmth. Indeed, her visit eventually tempered their initial awe, revealing that while impeccably bred, she was reserved, cool, and unable to contribute anything beyond the most mundane inquiries or observations.

Conversation was unnecessary, for Sir John was endlessly talkative. Lady Middleton had wisely brought along their eldest son, a charming little boy of about six, ensuring that whenever the ladies faced a lull, there was always a topic: his name, his age, his beauty, and endless questions answered by his mother while he clung to her skirts, holding his head low. This shyness surprised his mother greatly, who noted that he could make quite a racket at home. On formal visits, a child is essential to facilitate conversation. In this instance, ten minutes were spent debating whether the boy resembled his father or mother, and in what specific ways, for opinions naturally varied, and everyone marveled at the differing assessments. Soon, the Dashwoods would have ample opportunity to discuss the rest of the Middleton children, as Sir John refused to leave the house without securing their promise to dine at the park the following day.

Barton Park stood roughly half a mile from the cottage. Though the ladies had crossed its grounds earlier while traversing the valley, the house remained hidden from their view back home, concealed by the projecting ridge of a nearby hill. It was a structure of considerable size and grandeur, inhabited by the Middletons, who maintained a lifestyle marked by both generous hospitality and refined elegance. This dual display served distinct purposes: the former catered to Sir John's desires, while the latter satisfied his wife. Their household was rarely empty; they entertained guests constantly and hosted a wider variety of social gatherings than any other family in the district. Such activity was essential to their contentment. Despite their stark differences in temperament and demeanor, they shared a profound similarity: a complete lack of intellectual or artistic cultivation that restricted their pursuits outside of social obligations to a very narrow scope. Sir John was a devoted sportsman, while Lady Middleton was solely defined by her role as a mother. He spent his days hunting and shooting; she devoted hers to doting on her children. These were their only true passions. Lady Middleton held the advantage of being able to indulge her offspring year-round, whereas Sir John's independent amusements were available only seasonally. Nevertheless, a relentless schedule of engagements—both local and distant—compensated for their natural and educational shortcomings. These activities sustained Sir John's high spirits and provided ample opportunity for Lady Middleton to practice her impeccable manners.

Lady Middleton took immense pride in the refinement of her dining table and the perfection of her domestic arrangements; indeed, this vanity constituted her greatest joy during any gathering. Conversely, Sir John derived a far more genuine satisfaction from society itself. He delighted in filling his home with as many young people as possible, finding the louder the noise, the greater his pleasure. He was a boon to the youth of the neighborhood: in summer, he organized endless outdoor feasts of cold ham and chicken, and in winter, his private balls were frequent enough to satisfy any young lady not yet overwhelmed by the voracious appetites typical of age fifteen. The arrival of a new family in the countryside was always a cause for celebration with him. Consequently, he was utterly charmed by the inhabitants he had recently secured for his cottage at Barton. The Dashwood sisters were young, beautiful, and possessed a natural simplicity. To Sir John, this combination was sufficient to win his approval; for a pretty girl, lacking pretense was the ultimate asset in making her spirit as captivating as her appearance. His kindly disposition made him eager to accommodate those whose circumstances, compared to his own, might seem unfortunate. Thus, in showing kindness to his "cousins," he experienced the authentic fulfillment of a benevolent heart. Furthermore, by hosting a family consisting entirely of women in his cottage, he enjoyed the specific satisfaction of a sportsman: a man who values only those of his own sex who share his love for the chase is seldom inclined to encourage their tastes by inviting them to reside within his own estate.

Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters were greeted at the door by Sir John, who welcomed them to Barton Park with sincere warmth. As he escorted them to the drawing-room, he reiterated the regret he had expressed the previous day regarding his inability to arrange a gathering of lively young gentlemen. He explained that aside from himself, there would be only one other gentleman present—a close friend staying at the park who was neither notably young nor particularly vivacious. He hoped they would forgive the smallness of the party, assuring them that such an occurrence would never repeat itself. He had visited several households that morning hoping to expand their numbers, but the moonlit night meant everyone was already fully engaged. Fortunately, Lady Middleton's mother had arrived at Barton less than an hour prior. Described as a cheerful and agreeable woman, Sir John hoped the young ladies would not find the evening as dreary as they might anticipate. Both the sisters and their mother were perfectly content with having two complete strangers among their party and desired no additions.

Mrs. Jennings, Lady Middleton's mother, was a good-natured, jolly, plump, elderly woman who spoke incessantly, appeared perpetually happy, and leaned heavily toward vulgarity. Full of jokes and laughter, she managed to utter numerous witty remarks concerning lovers and husbands before dinner concluded. She joked that they must not have left their hearts behind them in Sussex, pretending to detect blushing cheeks regardless of whether they actually felt shy. Marianne found these comments irritating on her sister's behalf, turning her gaze toward Elinor to observe how she endured such banality, with an intensity that caused Elinor far more distress than the triviality of Mrs. Jennings's teasing warranted.

Colonel Brandon, Sir John's friend, seemed equally ill-suited to friendship as Lady Middleton was to matrimony, or Mrs. Jennings to maternal duty. He was silent and grave. Yet, despite being deemed by Marianne and Margaret an absolute old bachelor (though he was merely past fifty-five), his appearance was not displeasing. While his face lacked conventional handsomeness, his countenance was intelligent, and his bearing was distinctly gentlemanly. Nothing in the assembled group recommended them as ideal companions for the Dashwoods; however, the cold insipidity of Lady Middleton was so uniquely repellent that, in comparison, the gravity of Colonel Brandon—even the boisterous mirth of Sir John and his mother-in-law—appeared engaging.

Lady Middleton seemed capable of enjoyment only after dinner, once her four noisy children entered the room. They tugged at her dress, tore at her clothing, and silenced every form of conversation save that which concerned themselves. Later that evening, upon discovering Marianne's musical ability, she was invited to perform. The piano was unlocked, and everyone prepared to be enchanted. At their request, Marianne, who sang beautifully, performed the majority of the songs Lady Middleton had introduced into the family upon her marriage. Perhaps these melodies had lain untouched on the keyboard ever since, for her ladyship had abandoned music entirely upon marrying, despite accounts from her own mother claiming she played exceptionally well and her personal admission that she loved it. Marianne's performance received enthusiastic applause. Sir John shouted his admiration at the conclusion of every song and conversed loudly with others throughout the duration of each piece. Lady Middleton frequently chided him, marveling at how anyone could divert their attention from music for even a moment, and insisted Marianne sing a specific tune she had just heard completed.

Of the entire party, Colonel Brandon alone listened to her without falling into rapture. He offered her only the courtesy of attentive silence. On this occasion, Marianne felt a sense of respect for him that she believed the others had reasonably forfeited through their shameless lack of taste. His appreciation for music, though it did not reach the ecstatic heights required to truly sympathize with her own feelings, was commendable when contrasted against the horrifying insensitivity of the rest. She was sensible enough to acknowledge that a man of fifty-five might well have outgrown all acute sensitivity and every exquisite capacity for enjoyment. She was entirely willing to grant every allowance for the Colonel's advanced years that humanity demanded.

Mrs. Jennings was a widow possessing an ample income, having seen both of her daughters happily settled into respectable marriages. With no family left to manage, her sole ambition turned outward: to marry off everyone else. She pursued this goal with zealous energy, seizing every opportunity to engineer unions among the youth within her circle. Possessing a keen instinct for detecting romantic attachment, she delighted in stirring the blushes and inflaming the vanities of young ladies by hinting at their secret affections for specific gentlemen. This talent allowed her to arrive at a definitive conclusion regarding Colonel Brandon and Miss Marianne Dashwood shortly after settling at Barton. Indeed, she suspected his feelings from their very first meeting, noting his rapt attention while she sang, and confirmed it when the Middletons hosted dinner at the cottage and he listened to her once more. It was inevitable, she decided; he was wealthy, and she was beautiful—a match made in heaven.

Mrs. Jennings had long desired to secure a husband for Colonel Brandon, ever since Sir John introduced him to her society, and she held the same fervent wish for every pretty girl under her roof. The personal benefit to herself was substantial, providing endless material for jests. At the park, she mocked the colonel; at the cottage, she teased Marianne. While the colonel likely found her ribbing harmless, Marianne initially struggled to comprehend it. Once the target became clear, she oscillated between laughing at the sheer absurdity and censuring the impertinence, viewing the comments as cruel reflections on the colonel's advanced years and his status as a lonely bachelor.

Mrs. Dashwood, unable to accept that a man merely five years younger than herself could appear so antiquated to her daughter's youthful imagination, stepped in to defend him. "But surely, Mama, you cannot deny the absurdity of the accusation, even if you don't mean it maliciously," she argued gently. "Colonel Brandon is indeed younger than Mrs. Jennings, but he is old enough to be *my* father. If he ever possessed the courage to fall in love, he must have long ago outgrown such sentiments. It is preposterous! When will a man finally be safe from such wit if age and infirmity offer no protection?"

"Infirmity!" exclaimed Elinor. "Do you truly consider Colonel Brandon infirm? I can imagine his age seems much older to you than to my mother, but you cannot fool yourself about his physical capabilities."

"Did you not hear him complaining of rheumatism?" Marianne countered. "And isn't that the most common ailment of declining years?"

"My dearest child," Mrs. Dashwood laughed, "if we proceed at this rate, you shall live in perpetual fear of *my* own decay. It must seem miraculous to you that I have survived to the advanced age of forty."

"Mama, you are not giving me credit," Elinor replied calmly. "I know Colonel Brandon is not so aged that his friends need worry about losing him naturally. He could easily live another twenty years. However, thirty-five has little place in matrimony."

"Perhaps," Elinor continued smoothly, "thirty-five and seventeen should not be paired either. But if, by some chance, a woman remains unmarried at twenty-seven, I see no objection to Colonel Brandon marrying her."

"A woman of twenty-seven," Marianne mused after a pause, "can never hope to feel or inspire affection again. If her home is uncomfortable or her fortune small, she might endure the duties of a nurse merely for the security of a wife. Thus, in his marrying such a woman, there would be nothing improper. It would simply be a contract of convenience, satisfying the world. In my eyes, however, it would not be a marriage at all—it would be a mere commercial exchange where each party seeks profit at the other's expense."

"It is impossible," Elinor responded, "to convince you that a woman of twenty-seven could feel for a man of thirty-five anything approaching true love, making him a desirable companion. Yet, I must object to your sentencing Colonel Brandon and his future wife to a lifetime of confinement in a sickroom solely because he complained yesterday—on a cold, damp day—of a slight ache in his shoulder."

"But he spoke of flannel waistcoats!" Marianne insisted. "To me, a flannel waistcoat is forever linked with aches, cramps, rheumatism, and every affliction that plagues the elderly and weak."

"If he had been suffering from a violent fever, you would not despise him nearly so much. Confess, Marianne—is there not something fascinating to you in the flushed cheek, the hollow eye, and the rapid pulse of someone running a fever?"

Shortly thereafter, as Elinor prepared to leave the room, Marianne confided a different anxiety. "Mama, I have a dread concerning illness that I cannot hide. I am certain Edward Ferrars is unwell. We have been here nearly two weeks, yet he has not visited. Only genuine indisposition could explain such an extraordinary delay. What else could keep him at Norland?"

"Had you expected him sooner?" Mrs. Dashwood asked.

"I had no notion," Marianne admitted. "On the contrary, whenever I mentioned his coming to Barton, he seemed to show a lack of enthusiasm. Does Elinor expect him already?"

"I have never hinted at it to her, but perhaps she assumes it."

"I doubt you are correct," Mrs. Dashwood replied. "When I spoke yesterday of installing a new grate in the spare bedroom, Elinor remarked there was no rush, as the room would likely remain unused for some time."

"How peculiar this is! What could account for it? Their behavior toward one another has been utterly inexplicable! How cold and composed were their final farewells! How listless their conversation during their last evening together! In Edward's goodbye, there was no distinction between Elinor and myself; it was merely the warm wishes of a loving brother. Twice I deliberately left them alone earlier that morning, and each time he followed me out of the room with uncanny precision. And Elinor, when departing Norland and saying goodbye to Edward, did not cry as I did. Her self-control remains unshaken. When does she ever appear dejected or melancholy? When does she seek solitude or display restlessness amidst society?"

The Dashwood sisters had finally found their footing at Barton Cottage, settling into a life of tolerable comfort. The house, its gardens, and the rolling landscape surrounding them had ceased to be strangers; instead, they had become familiar friends. The simple pleasures that once held Norland Manor dear were now pursued with a joy far deeper than the estate itself could ever provide during the shadow of their father's absence.

Sir John Middleton, accustomed to a quiet domestic routine, visited daily for the first two weeks. He could not hide his astonishment at discovering the Dashwoods perpetually occupied. While he urged them repeatedly to mingle more freely with the neighborhood, promising his carriage was always at their disposal, Mrs. Dashwood's spirited independence proved insurmountable. She resolved firmly to decline visits to any household beyond walking distance. Such families were scarce, and even fewer were accessible.

Yet, nature offered ample distraction. About a mile and a half from the cottage, nestled in the narrow, winding valley of Allenham, lay an ancient, dignified mansion. Its resemblance to Norland sparked the girls' imaginations, fueling a desire to learn more about its inhabitants. Inquiry revealed that the owner, an elderly lady of impeccable character, was too frail to venture outdoors, confined entirely to her home.

Surrounding them, the countryside teemed with breathtaking paths. From nearly every window of the cottage, the high downs beckoned, offering exquisite breezes that served as a perfect refuge when the lower valleys became muddy and gloomy. It was toward one of these sun-drenched heights that Marianne and Margaret ventured one memorable morning. Drawn by patches of sunlight breaking through a cloudy sky, they sought relief from the oppressive rain that had persisted for two days.

Elinor and their mother remained indoors, engrossed in books and drawing, despite Marianne's confident prediction that the clouds would vanish. The two sisters ascended the downs with buoyant spirits, celebrating every glimpse of blue sky. When the invigorating gusts of a southerly wind brushed against their faces, they felt a pang of pity for those kept inside.

"Is there any happiness in the world," Marianne declared, "greater than this?"

"Then let us stay here for at least two hours," Margaret agreed.

They pressed onward against the wind, laughing with delight for another twenty minutes before the heavens turned. Clouds merged overhead, unleashing a driving rain that drenched them completely. Chagrined yet resigned, they turned back, knowing no shelter awaited until they returned home. However, urgency transformed their retreat into a race. With the steep slope leading straight to the garden gate, they sprinted downward.

Marianne initially gained the lead, but a misstep sent her crashing to the earth. Margaret, unable to halt her momentum to help, was swept forward, reaching the bottom safely. Just as Marianne fell, a gentleman carrying a gun passed nearby, accompanied by two pointing dogs. Hearing the commotion, he dropped his weapon and rushed to her aid.

Marianne had managed to raise herself, but her foot was twisted, leaving her barely able to stand. The gentleman, recognizing her distress, offered his help. Seeing her modesty hesitate where necessity demanded action, he lifted her effortlessly into his arms and carried her down the hill. Passing through the open garden gate—which Margaret had left ajar—he bore her directly into the house, where she was just arriving. He did not release her until he had seated her comfortably in a parlor chair.

Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor rose in stunned silence, their gazes fixed upon the stranger with a mixture of wonder and secret admiration. His appearance was undeniably striking. He explained his arrival with such frankness and grace that his inherent handsomeness seemed enhanced by his voice and demeanor. Even if he had been older, uglier, or coarser, Mrs. Dashwood's gratitude would have been secure; yet, the combination of youth, beauty, and elegance added a profound resonance to his gesture.

She thanked him profusely and, with her characteristic sweetness, invited him to sit. He politely declined, citing his wet and disheveled state. Curiosity piqued, Mrs. Dashwood asked for his name.

"I am Willoughby," he replied smoothly, noting that he resided at Allenham and hoped to pay his respects the following day. The invitation was gladly accepted, and he departed into the pouring rain, leaving behind a trail of fascination.

His masculine beauty and exceptional grace immediately became the subject of universal acclaim. The laughter elicited by his gallantry toward Marianne rang out with renewed vigor due to his physical allure. Though Marianne had seen less of him than the others—her face flushed crimson when he lifted her, robbing her of the chance to study his features fully—she joined the chorus of admiration with characteristic enthusiasm. In her mind, his figure matched the heroes of her favorite tales. The lack of formal ceremony in carrying her indoors impressed her greatly; every detail about him seemed intriguing. His name was agreeable, his location favored, and she quickly concluded that nothing suited a man's attire better than a shooting jacket. Pain from her sprained ankle faded under the weight of her delighted imagination.

When Sir John visited later that afternoon, eager to hear of the incident, he was promptly asked if he knew a Mr. Willoughby at Allenham.

"Willoughby!" Sir John exclaimed. "Is *he* in the country? Excellent news! I shall ride over tomorrow and invite him to dine on Thursday."

"You know him, then?" Mrs. Dashwood asked.

"Know him? Of course! He comes down here annually."

"And what sort of a young man is he?"

"As good a fellow as ever lived," Sir John assured them. "A decent shot, and perhaps the boldest rider in England."

"That is all you can say?" Marianne interjected indignantly. "What of his manners? His talents? His genius?"

Sir John looked slightly perplexed. "Upon my soul, I know little of those things. But he is pleasant, good-humored, and possesses the finest black pointer I've ever seen. Did he bring her out today?"

Marianne could not answer regarding the dog's color any more than Sir John could describe Willoughby's inner qualities.

"But who is he?" Elinor queried. "Where does he come from? Does he live at Allenham?"

On this point, Sir John was more informed. Mr. Willoughby owned no local property; he stayed only to visit the elderly lady at Allenham Court, to whom he was related and from whom he expected to inherit.

"Yes, yes, he is certainly worth pursuing," Sir John added cheerfully. "Besides, he owns a small estate in Somersetshire. If I were you, I wouldn't give him up to my younger sister, regardless of this hill-climbing business. Miss Marianne cannot expect to monopolize all the eligible men. Brandon will be jealous if she isn't careful."

"I doubt," Mrs. Dashwood smiled gently, "that Mr. Willoughby will be inconvenienced by either of my daughters attempting to 'catch' him. It is not a pursuit they were raised for. Men are quite safe with us, whatever their wealth. Still, hearing he is a respectable young man makes me glad our acquaintance is inevitable."

"He is as good a sort of fellow as ever lived," Sir John reiterated. "Last Christmas, at a small dance at the park, he danced from eight until four without sitting down."

"Indeed?" Marianne cried, her eyes sparkling. "With elegance? With spirit?"

"Yes; and he was back on horseback by eight to hunt."

"That is precisely what I admire," Marianne declared. "Whatever his pursuits, his eagerness should know no bounds, leaving him no sense of fatigue."

"Aye, aye, I see exactly how this will go," Sir John chuckled. "You'll be setting your cap at him now, and never thinking of poor Brandon."

"That is an expression, Sir John," Marianne retorted warmly, "which I particularly detest. I abhor every cliché used to feign wit; phrases like 'setting one's cap at a man' or 'making a conquest' are the most odious. Their tendency is crude and unrefined. Were they ever clever, time has long since eroded their ingenuity."

Sir John struggled to grasp the rebuke but laughed heartily anyway. "Well, you'll make plenty of conquests, one way or another. Poor Brandon! He is already smitten, and he is very well worth your attention, despite all this tumbling and spraining."

The following morning, Willoughby arrived at the cottage with a gracefulness that Margaret, in her description of him, termed "elegance" rather than mere precision. He paid his respects early, driven by a desire to inquire personally into the welfare of those he had aided. Mrs. Dashwood welcomed him with a warmth born of both her own gratitude and Sir John's glowing endorsement; indeed, the entire visit served to reassure him of the family's charm, mutual affection, and domestic harmony. Yet, regarding their physical allure, he needed no second glance to be convinced.

Elinor possessed a delicate complexion, regular features, and a remarkably pleasing figure. Marianne, however, surpassed her sister entirely. Though her stature lacked the strict correctness of Elinor's, her height lent her form a striking presence. Her face was so radiant that calling her "beautiful" felt like a lesser offense than usual. Her skin, though tanned, glowed with an unusual brilliance due to its transparency. Every feature was excellent; her smile was sweet and inviting, while her deep, dark eyes burned with a vitality, spirit, and eagerness that was impossible not to admire.

Initially, Marianne kept her gaze lowered, embarrassed by the memory of his rescue. But as this initial shyness faded, replaced by a recognition of his frankness and vivacity—and especially when he confessed his passionate love for music and dancing—she bestowed upon him a look of approval that instantly claimed the majority of his conversation. Any mention of favorite pastimes immediately unlocked her tongue; she spoke with neither reserve nor hesitation. Soon, they discovered a profound alignment in their tastes for dance and song. When Marianne turned her inquiry toward literature, her enthusiasm was so infectious that any man of twenty-five would have been hard-pressed not to agree with her judgments, regardless of prior opinions. Their minds mirrored one another perfectly; whether agreeing on a passage or debating a point, Willoughby yielded to the force of her arguments and the sparkle in her eyes. Before his departure, they conversed with the intimacy of old friends.

"Well, Marianne," Elinor remarked as soon as he left, "for a single morning, I believe you have accomplished quite enough. You have already gauged Mr. Willoughby's views on nearly every important subject. You know his take on Cowper and Scott, are sure he appreciates Pope appropriately, and have confirmed his admiration for the beauties of poetry. But how long can such rapid progress sustain an acquaintance? You will soon exhaust every favorite topic. A future meeting will merely reveal his thoughts on picturesque scenery and remarriage, leaving you with little else to discuss."

"Elinor!" Marianne exclaimed, indignant. "Is this fair? Is this just? Are my ideas so limited?" She quickly realized her error. "Ah, I see! I have been too comfortable, too happy, too open. I have violated every convention of decorum; I have been sincere where I should have been reserved, spirited where I should have been dull, and honest where I should have been deceitful. Had I spoken only of the weather and the roads, limiting myself to speech once every ten minutes, this rebuke would have been avoided."

"My dear," their mother interjected gently, "do not take offense at Elinor's jest. I would scold her myself if she wished to dampen the joy of your conversation with our new friend."

Marianne's anger melted instantly. On his part, Willoughby offered every sign of delight in their growing friendship, eager to deepen the bond. He visited daily. Initially, checking on Marianne's health was his pretext, but as her welcome grew warmer each day, the need for such an excuse vanished entirely, especially since she had fully recovered and was no longer confined to the house.

Willoughby proved to be a youth of considerable ability, quick imagination, lively spirits, and open, affectionate manners. He was ideally suited to capture Marianne's heart. Beyond these qualities, he possessed a natural ardor of mind that was ignited and amplified by Marianne's own example, making him irresistible to her. Their society became her greatest delight. They read, talked, and sang together; his musical talent was significant, and he approached reading with a sensitivity and spirit that Edward sadly lacked. In Mrs. Dashwood's eyes, he was flawless. Elinor, however, found fault only with a tendency shared by her sister: speaking too freely of his opinions without regard for the listener or the situation. By hastily judging others, prioritizing the attention of those he loved over general politeness, and lightly dismissing social conventions, he displayed a lack of caution that Elinor could not endorse, despite Marianne's vigorous defense.

Marianne began to realize that her despair at sixteen—a fear that no man could ever meet her ideal of perfection—had been rash and unjustified. Willoughby embodied everything her youthful imagination had conjured in that tragic hour and in all subsequent dreams. His behavior confirmed his desires were as intense as his talents were strong. Even Mrs. Dashwood, who had initially harbored no romantic hopes for her daughters' futures based on financial prospects, found herself hoping and expecting a match within a week, secretly congratulating herself on securing two such sons-in-law: Edward and Willoughby.

Meanwhile, Colonel Brandon's affection for Marianne, previously noted only by acquaintances, finally drew Elinor's full attention. With everyone's focus diverted to the more fortunate rival, the mockery Brandon had endured earlier faded as his genuine feelings emerged. Elinor reluctantly accepted that the sentiments Mrs. Jennings had attributed to him were real, and that while Willoughby's attraction stemmed from a similarity of temperament, Brandon's regard thrived precisely because of their differences. She viewed the situation with concern: what hope did a quiet man of fifty-three have against a vibrant one of twenty? Unable to wish him success, she fervently prayed for his indifference. Yet, she liked him. Despite his gravity and reserve, she saw in him an object of interest. His serious manner was mild, and his reserve seemed born of sorrow rather than innate gloom. Having heard hints from Sir John of past injuries and disappointments, she regarded him with respect and pity, perhaps feeling even more sympathy because he was overlooked by Willoughby and Marianne, who, biased against his lack of liveliness and youth, seemed determined to undervalue his worth.

One day, while discussing him, Willoughby remarked, "Brandon is exactly the sort of man everyone praises yet ignores; whom all are pleased to see, yet no one bothers to speak to."

"That is precisely my view of him," Marianne agreed eagerly.

"Do not boast of it," Elinor chided. "It is unfair to both of you. He is highly esteemed by the entire household at the Park, and I never fail to make an effort to converse with him whenever I see him."

"He is patronized by *you*," Willoughby retorted, "which is certainly to his credit. But as for the esteem of the others, it is an insult in itself. Who would endure the indignity of being approved by women like Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings if they could command the indifference of anyone else?"

"But perhaps the disdain of men like yourselves makes up for the regard of Lady Middleton and her mother. If their praise is criticism, then your criticism might be praise, for they are no less discerning than you are prejudiced and unjust."

"In defense of your protégé, you can even be impertinent," Willoughby teased.

"My protégé, as you call him, is a sensible man," Marianne countered. "And sense always holds appeal for me. Yes, even in a man between thirty and forty. He has traveled widely, read extensively, and possesses a thoughtful mind. I have found him capable of providing vast information on many subjects, answering my questions with readiness, good breeding, and good nature."

"To put it plainly," Marianne sneered, "he has simply told you that the climate in the East Indies is hot and the mosquitoes are annoying."

"He *would* have told me so, I suspect, had I asked, but fortunately, I was already informed on those points."

"Perhaps," Willoughby suggested, "his observations extended to the existence of nabobs, gold mohurs, and palanquins."

"I dare say his observations went far beyond your candour. But why do you dislike him?"

"I do not dislike him," Willoughby insisted. "On the contrary, I consider him a very respectable man, universally praised yet ignored; a man with more wealth than he spends and more leisure than he knows how to use, sporting two new coats annually."

"And furthermore," Marianne added scornfully, "he lacks genius, taste, and spirit. His understanding shines with no brilliance, his feelings possess no ardor, and his voice lacks expression."

"You judge his flaws so broadly," Elinor observed calmly, "relying so heavily on your own imagination that my own praise seems cold and uninspired by comparison. I can only state that he is a sensible man, well-bred, well-informed, of gentle address, and, I believe, possessing an amiable heart."

"Miss Dashwood," Willoughby cried out, "you are treating me unkindly now. You attempt to disarm me with logic and convince me against my will. But it will not work. You shall find me as stubborn as you are cunning. I have three unanswerable reasons for disliking Colonel Brandon: he threatened me with rain when I desired sunshine; he criticized the arrangement of my carriage interior; and he refuses to purchase my brown mare. However, if knowing that I believe his character otherwise irreproachable brings you satisfaction,

Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters had never anticipated upon arriving in Devonshire that their quiet retreat would so quickly transform into a whirlwind of social obligations. Instead of finding solitude, they were immediately swept up in a tide of engagements, constant invitations, and ceaseless visits that left them with precious little time for reflection or serious pursuits. Once Marianne recovered from her initial disappointment, Sir John's long-planned schemes for amusement were set in motion with vigor. Private balls erupted within the park grounds, and outings on the water became a weekly ritual, weather permitting.

Willoughby was invariably included in these gatherings. The relaxed atmosphere of these events fostered an intimacy between him and the Dashwood sisters that grew rapidly. It offered him perfect opportunities to observe Marianne's virtues, to express his fervent admiration, and to receive unmistakable signs of her affection in return. Elinor, unsurprised by the depth of their connection, simply hoped it were displayed with greater discretion. On rare occasions, she gently urged Marianne toward self-restraint, but Marianne recoiled at the suggestion. To her, concealing genuine feeling was not just unnecessary but a shameful submission of reason to petty convention. Willoughby shared this sentiment entirely. Their behavior became a living testament to their beliefs: whenever he was near, Marianne saw no one else. Everything he did was flawless; everything he said was witty. If card games ended the evening, he would cheat himself and everyone else solely to ensure she held the winning hand. If dancing took precedence, they were partners for half the night, and when forced apart, they stood close together, ignoring all other company.

Such conduct inevitably drew laughter, yet ridicule failed to shame or deter them.
