Mrs. Dashwood embraced their passion with such warmth that she felt no impulse to curb it, viewing it merely as the natural expression of a strong heart in a youthful spirit. For Marianne, this was a season of profound joy. Her devotion to Willoughby was absolute, and the lingering attachment she carried for Norland began to soften under the enchanting influence of her new surroundings.

Elinor's happiness, however, was far more muted. Her peace of mind was unsettled, and the amusements offered her no solace for the life she had left behind. Neither Lady Middleton nor Mrs. Jennings could fill the void left by her old friends. Although Mrs. Jennings talked incessantly and treated Elinor with boundless kindness, her stories were repetitive, and her knowledge of family history seemed endless. Lady Middleton, while quieter, possessed a reserve that was merely a lack of wit rather than true dignity; she remained distant and unengaging toward both her husband and mother. Elinor soon realized that Lady Middleton's silence was a shield against conversation, not a sign of grace. There was nothing new in her speech, and her spirits remained perpetually flat. Though she tolerated the parties organized by her husband if they maintained a certain style and her eldest children were present, she derived no genuine pleasure from them. Her presence added little to the room's ambiance, often serving only as a reminder of her concern for her "troublesome" sons.

In contrast to the superficiality of her new acquaintances, Elinor found a singular figure worthy of her respect and friendship: Colonel Brandon. While Willoughby's attention was entirely consumed by Marianne, leaving no room for anyone else, Colonel Brandon found unexpected comfort in Elinor's company. He possessed the intellect to command her respect and the gentleness to inspire her affection. Elinor's sympathy for him deepened as she suspected he bore the scars of a thwarted love. One evening, while the others danced and the pair sat quietly nearby, the truth nearly slipped from his lips. With a faint smile, he remarked, "Your sister, I understand, does not approve of second attachments."

"No," Elinor replied softly, "her opinions are purely romantic."

"Or, perhaps more accurately," he continued, "she believes them impossible to exist."

"I believe she does," Elinor admitted. "Though I wonder how she manages to hold such views without considering her own father, who married twice. Perhaps a few years will bring her back to the sensible foundation of common observation, making her stance easier to defend."

"That is likely," Brandon agreed, adding, "Yet there is something so charming in the prejudices of youth that one regrets seeing them yield to mundane realities."

"I cannot agree with you there," Elinor countered firmly. "The dangers inherent in such feelings outweigh all the allure of enthusiasm and ignorance. Her system tends to dismiss propriety entirely. What she needs most is a deeper understanding of the world."

Brandon paused before asking, "Does she make exceptions? Is a second attachment equally unforgivable regardless of circumstance?"

"Elinor shook her head. 'I am unaware of the nuances of her principles, but I have never heard her excuse a single instance.'"

"This cannot be true forever," Brandon insisted. "A complete change of heart... No, do not wish for that. When youthful idealism crumbles, it is often replaced by opinions that are far too common and perilous. I speak from experience. I once knew a woman whose temperament and judgment mirrored your sister perfectly. But due to changed circumstances..." He stopped abruptly, realizing he had revealed too much. His sudden silence and troubled expression confirmed Elinor's fears. The resemblance was undeniable, and the story of lost love hung heavy in the air. Elinor tried to suppress the revelation, but Marianne, had she been present, would have instantly constructed the tragic narrative in her mind, confirming every detail of a disaster born of misplaced affection.

The following morning, as Elinor and Marianne strolled together through the garden, Marianne broke the silence with a revelation that left her sister momentarily stunned. Despite Elinor's prior knowledge of Marianne's impulsiveness and lack of foresight, the sheer extravagance of this new development caught her completely off guard. With radiant enthusiasm, Marianne announced that Willoughby had gifted her a horse—a magnificent animal he had personally bred on his Somersetshire estate, perfectly suited to carry a woman.

Marianne had accepted the gift without a second thought, failing to consider the logistical nightmare it presented. Her mother had explicitly ruled against keeping horses; altering that decision would require purchasing a mount for the servant, hiring an additional staff member to tend to it, and constructing a stable to house them. Yet, Marianne spoke of the arrangement in raptures. "He intends to send his groom to Somersetshire immediately for it," she exclaimed. "Once it arrives, we shall ride every single day. You must share its use with me, Elinor! Just imagine the joy of galloping across those downs!"

Elinor found herself desperately reluctant to shatter this vision of happiness with harsh realities. She reasoned that the cost of an extra servant would be negligible; surely her mother would never object. Any ordinary horse would suffice for the man-servant, who could likely find one at the park, and even a simple shed would serve as a stable. However, Elinor dared to question the propriety of accepting such a substantial gift from a man she barely knew.

"You are mistaken, Elinor," Marianne retorted, warming to her defense. "Do not suppose I know Willoughby poorly. While I have not known him long, my acquaintance with him surpasses that of anyone else in the world save yourself and Mother. Intimacy is not determined by time or opportunity, but solely by disposition. Seven years might fail to bring two people close, while seven days can be more than enough for others. I would feel far greater impropriety in accepting a horse from my brother John, whom I have known for years yet understand little of, than from Willoughby, whose character I have thoroughly judged."

Seeing that pressing this point further would only inflame Marianne's sensitive nature, Elinor wisely chose to drop the argument. Instead, she appealed to Marianne's deep love for her mother, painting a vivid picture of the inconvenience and burden such an expansion of the household would impose upon their kind-hearted parent. Slowly, Marianne yielded. She promised not to tempt her mother into imprudence by mentioning the offer and vowed to decline Willoughby's gift the next time she saw him. True to her word, when Willoughby visited the cottage later that day, Elinor heard Marianne express her regret in a hushed tone regarding her inability to accept the present. She explained the reasons clearly, rendering any further persuasion on Willoughby's part futile.

His distress was palpable. After voicing his sorrow earnestly, he leaned closer and whispered, "Yet, Marianne, the horse remains yours, even if you cannot use it now. I shall keep it merely until you are ready to claim it. When you depart Barton to establish your own permanent home, 'Queen Mab' awaits you."

Miss Dashwood, listening intently, seized upon every nuance of his speech—the intimate use of her sister's first name, the directness of his tone, and the unmistakable familiarity in his manner. In that instant, she perceived a bond so profound and explicit that it signaled a mutual engagement. There was no shock in realizing they were betrothed; rather, she marveled that two individuals of such open natures could allow their union to remain hidden until discovered by chance.

Later, Margaret added weight to Elinor's suspicions. Having spent the previous evening alone in the drawing-room with Willoughby and Marianne, Margaret had witnessed events that confirmed her elder sister's fears. With a grave expression, she approached Elinor. "Oh, Elinor!" she cried. "I have a secret concerning Marianne. I am certain she will marry Mr. Willoughby very soon."

"You have suggested this daily since they first met on High-church Down," Elinor replied dryly. "We hadn't known each other a week before you were convinced she wore his portrait around her neck, only to discover it was merely a miniature of our great-uncle."

"But this is entirely different!" Margaret insisted. "They will wed soon because he possesses a lock of her hair."

"Be careful, Margaret. It might simply be the hair of some great-uncle of *his*."

"No, truly, Elinor, it is Marianne's. I am nearly certain, for I saw him cut it off. Last night, after tea, when you and Mother left the room, they were whispering furiously. He seemed to beg something of her, then snatched her scissors, severed a long lock from her tumbling hair, kissed it reverently, folded it in white paper, and tucked it safely into his pocket-book."

Given the gravity of Margaret's account and its alignment with what Elinor had observed firsthand, she could not deny the truth of it. However, Margaret's insight often lacked tact. One evening at the Park, when Mrs. Jennings persistently demanded the name of Elinor's favorite young man—a query that had long amused the group—Margaret responded by glancing at her sister and asking, "May I not tell, Elinor?"

The comment drew laughter from everyone, including Elinor, though the effort to smile was painful. She suspected Margaret had selected a suitor whose identity she could not comfortably discuss as a public jest. Marianne, feeling genuine sympathy for her friend, inadvertently exacerbated the situation by flushing angrily and telling Margaret, "Remember that regardless of your conjectures, you have no right to repeat them."

"I never made any conjectures," Margaret countered cheerfully. "It was you who told me yourself."

This only fueled the merriment, prompting Mrs. Jennings to press further. "Pray, Miss Margaret, reveal everything. What is the gentleman's name? Where does he reside?"

"I cannot tell, ma'am. But I know precisely who he is and where he lives."

"Yes, yes, we can guess. At his own home in Norland, undoubtedly. He is the parish curate, I presume."

"No, *he* is not," Marianne interjected sharply. "He holds no profession whatsoever."

"Well, then, he must be recently deceased, Marianne, for I am sure such a man existed once, and his name begins with an F."

At this critical juncture, Elinor breathed a sigh of relief when Lady Middleton remarked, "It is raining very heavily." Though Elinor suspected the interruption stemmed more from Lady Middleton's distaste for the frivolous banter enjoyed by her husband and mother than from any consideration for Elinor's discomfort, the change of subject proved timely. Colonel Brandon, ever attentive to the feelings of others, eagerly joined in discussing the weather, successfully steering the conversation away from the awkward topic.

Willoughby then rose, opening the pianoforte and inviting Marianne to play, allowing the tension to dissipate amidst music. Yet, Elinor remained unsettled by the implications of the afternoon. That evening, plans were made for a grand excursion the following day to view a magnificent estate located twelve miles from Barton. The property belonged to a brother-in-law of Colonel Brandon; access was strictly controlled by the absent owner, whose instructions required the intervention of Colonel Brandon to arrange a viewing.

Sir John, a frequent visitor who had organized trips there twice annually for the past decade, enthusiastically praised the grounds as exceptionally beautiful. The itinerary included sailing on a large lake, picnicking with cold provisions, and traveling exclusively in open carriages, adhering to the traditional style of a leisure outing. However, the timing raised concerns among some guests, given the relentless rainfall over the previous fortnight. Consequently, Mrs. Dashwood, already suffering from a cold, was gently persuaded by Elinor to remain behind at home.

The planned outing to Whitwell unfolded far differently than Elinor had anticipated. She had braced herself for rain, exhaustion, and perhaps even fright, yet the outcome proved even less fortunate: they did not go at all.

By ten o'clock, the entire party gathered at the park for breakfast. Despite the previous night's rainfall, the morning showed promise; clouds were breaking apart, allowing the sun to shine intermittently. Spirits were high, tempers excellent, and everyone was eager to embrace joy, ready to endure any hardship rather than miss the opportunity. However, the arrival of letters shattered this mood. Among the correspondence was one addressed to Colonel Brandon. Upon receiving it, he glanced at the sender, his complexion darkened instantly, and he departed the room without a word.

"What is troubling Colonel Brandon?" Sir John asked. "Nobody knows," someone replied. "I trust he hasn't received bad news," Lady Middleton interjected. "Something extraordinary must have occurred to make him abandon my breakfast table so abruptly."

Five minutes later, he returned. "No bad news, I hope," Mrs. Jennings said warmly as he entered. "None at all, ma'am, thank you."

"Was it from Avignon? I pray it doesn't concern your sister's health."

"No, ma'am. It arrived from London and is purely a matter of business."

"But how could a mere business letter disturb you so greatly? Come, Colonel, we deserve the truth."

"My dear madam," Lady Middleton chided gently, "please consider your words."

"Perhaps it announces that your cousin Fanny is married?" Mrs. Jennings guessed, ignoring her daughter's reproach.

"Not at all."

"Well, then, I know exactly who sent it. And I hope she is well."

"Who do you mean, ma'am?" he asked, flushing slightly.

"Oh! You know precisely who I mean."

"I am deeply sorry, ma'am," he addressed Lady Middleton, "to receive this letter today, for it concerns business requiring my immediate attention in town."

"In town!" Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. "What possible business could keep you in town during this season?"

"My personal loss is significant," he continued, "in leaving such a delightful gathering. Yet, I am even more distressed because I fear my presence is essential to secure admission to Whitwell."

The announcement struck the group like a blow.

"If you simply write a note to the housekeeper, Mr. Brandon," Marianne suggested eagerly, "wouldn't that suffice?"

He shook his head firmly.

"We must proceed," Sir John declared. "It cannot be postponed now that we are so close. You cannot travel to town until tomorrow, Brandon—that is final."

"I wish it were so simple," he replied. "However, I lack the power to delay my journey by even a single day."

"If you would only tell us the nature of your business," Mrs. Jennings pressed, "perhaps we could determine if it might wait."

"You wouldn't be delayed by six hours," Willoughby remarked smoothly, "if you postponed your trip until our return."

"I cannot afford to lose *one* hour."

Elinor caught Willoughby whispering to Marianne, "Some individuals simply cannot tolerate a pleasure party. Brandon is one of them. He likely feared catching a chill and concocted this excuse to escape. I'd wager fifty guineas the letter was written by himself."

"I have no doubt of it," Marianne responded.

"There is no convincing you to change your mind, Brandon, as I've known you since childhood," Sir John noted. "Nevertheless, I trust you'll reconsider. Think of it: the two Miss Careys traveled from Newton, the three Miss Dashwoods walked up from the cottage, and Mr. Willoughby rose two hours earlier than usual just to join us at Whitwell."

Colonel Brandon reiterated his sorrow at disappointing the group but insisted his departure was unavoidable.

"Well, when will you return?"

"We shall hopefully see you at Barton," her ladyship added, "as soon as you can conveniently leave town. Consequently, we must postpone the Whitwell excursion until your return."

"You are very kind. Yet, given the uncertainty of my return date, I dare not make any promises."

"Oh! He must and shall return," Sir John cried. "If he isn't here by the end of the week, I shall go fetch him myself."

"Ay, do that, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings chimed in. "Perhaps then you'll discover the true nature of his business."

"I have no desire to pry into another man's affairs. I suspect it involves something he feels ashamed of."

As Colonel Brandon's horses were announced, Sir John asked, "Are you traveling to town on horseback?"

"No. Only to Honiton. From there, I shall take the post coach."

"Well, since you are resolved, I wish you a safe journey. Though, truly, you ought to change your mind."

"I assure you, it is beyond my power."

With these words, he took his leave of the assembly. Turning to the Dashwood sisters, he asked, "Shall I ever have the pleasure of seeing you and your sisters in town this winter?"

"I fear not at all."

"Then I must bid you farewell for a duration longer than I care to accept." To Marianne, he offered only a bow and silence.

"Come, Colonel," Mrs. Jennings urged before he departed, "do tell us what errand drives you away."

He wished them a good morning and exited, accompanied by Sir John. Once alone, the polite restraint dissolved, and universal complaints erupted. Everyone lamented the disappointment repeatedly.

"I can guess the reason for his business," Mrs. Jennings declared triumphantly. "Do you?" nearly everyone asked.

"Yes; it concerns Miss Williams, I am certain."

"And who is Miss Williams?" Marianne queried.

"What! Don't you know Miss Williams? Surely you've heard of her before. She is a relative of the Colonel's, my dear—a very close one. We won't specify how close, lest we shock the young ladies." Lowering her voice, she leaned toward Elinor and whispered, "She is his illegitimate daughter."

"Indeed!"

"Oh, yes! And she resembles him as closely as anyone could stare. I daresay the Colonel will leave her his entire fortune."

When Sir John returned, he enthusiastically joined the collective regret over the ruined plans, though he concluded by suggesting that since they were all assembled, they must find some way to enjoy themselves. After brief deliberation, they agreed that while true happiness awaited them at Whitwell, they could achieve a reasonable state of cheerfulness by touring the countryside. Carriages were summoned; Willoughby's led the way, and Marianne appeared happier than ever as she stepped inside. He drove swiftly through the park, and they vanished from sight quickly. No further mention of them arose until their late return, occurring only after the rest of the party had come back. Both seemed thrilled with their excursion, mentioning vaguely that they had stayed within the lanes while others ventured onto the downs.

A dance was scheduled for the evening, and everyone vowed to remain merrily occupied throughout the day. Additional members of the Carey family joined for dinner, resulting in a table seating nearly twenty guests, a prospect Sir John viewed with great satisfaction. Willoughby took his customary seat between the two eldest Miss Dashwoods. Mrs. Jennings sat to Elinor's right, and shortly after they were seated, she leaned forward, speaking loudly enough for both Willoughby and Marianne to hear, "I have uncovered your secret despite all your cunning. I know exactly where you spent the morning."

Marianne flushed and replied hurriedly, "Where, pray?"

"Didn't you know," Willoughby interjected, "that we rode out in my curricle?"

"Yes, yes, Mr. Impudence, I knew that perfectly well. My determination was to discover *where* you had gone. I hope you approve of your residence, Miss Marianne. It is quite spacious, I understand; and when I visit next, I hope you have furnished it anew, for it desperately needed updating when I visited six years ago."

Marianne turned away, deeply embarrassed. Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily. Meanwhile, Elinor realized that in her resolve to ascertain their whereabouts, she had inadvertently instructed her maid to inquire with Mr. Willoughby's groom. Through this channel, she learned they had gone to Allenham, spending considerable time strolling the gardens and inspecting the entire house.

Elinor struggled to believe this account plausible; it seemed highly improbable that Willoughby would propose entering the house, or that Marianne would consent, while Mrs. Smith resided there—with whom Marianne shared no acquaintance whatsoever.

Immediately upon leaving the dining room, Elinor questioned her maid, only to be astonished when every detail Mrs. Jennings recounted proved entirely accurate. Marianne became furious at Elinor's skepticism.

"Why did you imagine, Elinor, that we didn't go there, or that we didn't view the house? Isn't that something you have often wished to do yourself?"

"Yes, Marianne, but I would not enter while Mrs. Smith was present, especially with no companion other than Mr. Willoughby."

"Mr. Willoughby, however, is the sole individual entitled to show that house; moreover, since he arrived in an open carriage, it was impossible for anyone else to accompany us. I never experienced a more delightful morning in my life."

"I fear," Elinor replied, "that the enjoyment of an activity does not necessarily prove

The abrupt end to Colonel Brandon's stay at the Park, coupled with his remarkable success in hiding the true reason for his departure, kept Mrs. Jennings in a state of bewildered speculation for several days. Being a woman of intense curiosity regarding the movements of everyone she knew, she found it nearly impossible to stop wondering. With barely a moment's pause between thoughts, she convinced herself that something terrible must have occurred. She mentally cataloged every conceivable misfortune that could have befallen him, determined that he deserved none of them less.

"Something deeply sorrowful must be the cause, I am certain," she declared. "I saw it written plainly on his face. Poor soul! I fear his financial situation has turned dire. That estate at Delaford was never worth much more than two thousand pounds a year, and his brother left behind debts that were quite overwhelming. I truly believe he was summoned back to deal with monetary troubles; surely nothing else could explain it. I wonder if that is indeed the case. I would give anything to know the truth. Perhaps it concerns Miss Williams—and, by the way, I suspect it does, simply because he looked so guilty when I brought her name up. Maybe she is ill in London; nothing is more likely, for I've always suspected she is somewhat frail. I'd bet my life it's about Miss Williams. Though, honestly, it isn't very probable he is in financial straits *now*, considering he is a man of prudence and must have settled those affairs long ago. What on earth could it be? Perhaps his sister is worse at Avignon and has called for him. His hurried departure certainly suggests that. Very well, I wish him relief from all his troubles, and a suitable wife to boot!" Thus pondered and thus spoke Mrs. Jennings, her theories shifting with every new idea, all appearing equally plausible as they formed.

Elinor, while genuinely concerned for Colonel Brandon's well-being, could not share the depth of astonishment regarding his sudden exit that her mother desired. In her view, the incident did not warrant such prolonged amazement or endless guessing. Furthermore, her curiosity was directed elsewhere: it was consumed by the extraordinary silence maintained by her sister and Mr. Willoughby concerning the matter. They must realize how intensely this topic affected everyone present. As the silence persisted, each passing day rendered it stranger and more inconsistent with the natures of both young people. Elinor could not fathom why they would not openly admit to their mother and themselves what their consistent behavior toward one another clearly indicated had happened.

She could understand that marriage might not be immediately feasible; although Willoughby possessed independence, there was no evidence he was wealthy. His estate had been valued by Sir John at roughly six or seven hundred pounds annually, yet he lived beyond his means, frequently complaining of his poverty. However, this peculiar secrecy regarding their engagement—which revealed nothing hidden—was difficult to reconcile with their usual openness. It was so contrary to their general character that a shadow of doubt occasionally crossed Elinor's mind as to whether they were truly engaged. This uncertainty alone prevented her from asking Marianne directly.

Nothing could demonstrate greater devotion to the family than Willoughby's conduct. Toward Marianne, he displayed every tender distinction a lover's heart could offer; toward the rest of the household, he showed the warm attentiveness of a beloved son and brother. The cottage appeared to be his true home; he spent far more time there than at Allenham. Even when no specific engagement drew them to the Park, his morning walks invariably ended there, where he remained throughout the day beside Marianne, with his favorite pointer dog resting at her feet.

One evening, approximately a week after Colonel Brandon had left the countryside, Willoughby's heart seemed unusually open to the affections surrounding him. When Mrs. Dashwood casually mentioned her intention to improve the cottage in the spring, he vigorously opposed any alterations to a place that affection had already perfected in his eyes.

"What!" he exclaimed. "Improve this dear cottage! No. That I will never consent to. Not a single stone must be added to its walls, nor an inch to its size, if my feelings are respected."

"Do not be alarmed," Miss Dashwood reassured him. "No such plans will be executed; for my mother simply lacks the funds to undertake them."

"I am heartily glad of that," he cried. "May she always remain poor, if she can utilize her wealth no better than this."

"Thank you, Willoughby. But rest assured, I would not sacrifice a single sentiment of local attachment held by you—or anyone I love—for all the improvements in the world. Be confident that whatever surplus remains when I settle my accounts in the spring, I would prefer to leave it idle rather than spend it in a way so painful to you. Yet, are you truly so attached to this place that you perceive no flaws in it?"

"I am," he replied. "To me, it is flawless. Indeed, I regard it as the only architectural form wherein happiness can be attained. Were I rich enough, I would tear down Combe immediately and rebuild it exactly according to the plan of this cottage."

"With dark, narrow staircases and a kitchen full of smoke, I presume," Elinor remarked dryly.

"Yes," he shouted in the same fervent tone, "with every detail included—in no single convenience or discomfort should the slightest change be noticeable. Only then, under such a roof, might I perhaps find myself as happy at Combe as I have been at Barton."

"I flatter myself," Elinor responded, "that even with superior rooms and wider staircases, you will eventually find your own home just as perfect as you do this one."

"There are certainly aspects," Willoughby admitted, "that might greatly increase my fondness for it; but this place holds one claim on my affection that no other location can possibly match."

Mrs. Dashwood watched with delight at Marianne, whose beautiful eyes were fixed so expressively upon Willoughby that it was plain she fully comprehended him.

"How often," he added, lowering his voice slightly, "did I wish, when I was at Allenham last year, that Barton Cottage were occupied! I never passed within sight of it without admiring its setting and mourning that no one lived there. How little did I anticipate that the very first news I would receive from Mrs. Smith upon returning to the country would be that Barton Cottage had been rented! I felt an instant satisfaction and deep interest in the event, which nothing short of a premonition of the happiness I would derive from it can explain. Was it not so, Marianne?"

He then resumed his louder tone, addressing Mrs. Dashwood: "And yet, you would ruin this house? You would strip it of its simplicity through imagined enhancements! And this dear parlor, where our acquaintance first commenced and where countless joyful hours have since been shared among us, you would degrade to the status of a mere entryway, allowing everyone to rush through the room that has hitherto offered more genuine comfort and ease than any spacious apartment in the grandest mansion could provide."

Mrs. Dashwood once again promised him that no such alterations would be attempted.

"You are a good woman," he replied warmly. "Your assurance brings me peace. Extend it a bit further, and it will bring me joy. Promise me not only that your house will remain unchanged, but that I shall forever find you and your family as unaltered as your dwelling; and that you will always regard me with the kindness that has made everything connected to you so precious to me."

The promise was granted readily, and Willoughby's demeanor throughout the entire evening unmistakably conveyed both his affection and his profound happiness.

"Shall we see you tomorrow for dinner?" Mrs. Dashwood asked as he prepared to depart.

"I do not request to come in the morning, for we must walk to the Park to pay our respects to Lady Middleton," he noted.

He agreed to join them by four o'clock.

The following day, Mrs. Dashwood paid her promised visit to Lady Middleton, accompanied by two of her daughters. Marianne, however, declined to join them, citing a trivial engagement that kept her at home. Her mother, believing that Willoughby had promised to call upon her during their absence, was entirely content with her staying behind. Upon returning from the park, they discovered Willoughby's curricle and servant waiting at the cottage door. Mrs. Dashwood, convinced her earlier intuition was correct, felt a surge of satisfaction. Yet, as they crossed the threshold, reality presented a scene no foresight had prepared her for.

Hardly had they stepped into the passage when Marianne emerged from the drawing-room, visibly distraught, her handkerchief pressed tightly to her eyes. Without offering a word, she hurried upstairs. Alarmed by her daughter's sudden flight, Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed immediately into the room Marianne had just vacated. Inside, they found only Willoughby, leaning against the fireplace with his back turned toward them. At their entrance, he turned around, his face betraying the same profound emotion that had overwhelmed Marianne.

"Is anything the matter with her?" cried Mrs. Dashwood, stepping forward with concern. "Is she ill?"

"I hope not," Willoughby replied, attempting to maintain a cheerful demeanor. With a strained smile, he quickly added, "In fact, it is I who may fairly expect to fall ill—for I am currently suffering under a grievous disappointment!"

"A disappointment?"

"Yes," he confirmed. "I am unable to fulfill my engagement with you. Mrs. Smith has exercised the privileges of wealth today upon a poor dependent cousin by sending me on urgent business to London. I have just received my dispatches and bid farewell to Allenham; and, seeking a measure of exhilaration, I have come to bid you farewell as well."

"To London! And are you leaving this morning?"

"Almost at this very moment."

"This is exceedingly unfortunate. However, Mrs. Smith must be gratified, and I trust her business will not detain you from us for long."

He flushed slightly as he answered, "You are very kind, but I have no intention of returning to Devonshire immediately. My visits to Mrs. Smith are rarely repeated within a year."

"And is Mrs. Smith your sole companion? Is Allenham the only household in the neighborhood where you are welcome? Pray, Willoughby, can you truly wait for an invitation here?"

His color deepened further. Casting his gaze downward, he offered only, "You are too good."

Mrs. Dashwood exchanged a surprised glance with Elinor, who felt a similar shock. A heavy silence fell upon the group until Mrs. Dashwood broke it.

"I need only to add, my dear Willoughby, that Barton Cottage shall always remain your sanctuary. I shall not urge you to return here immediately, for only you can judge whether such haste might please Mrs. Smith. On this matter, I shall neither question your judgment nor doubt your inclination."

"My current engagements," Willoughby stammered, looking increasingly confused, "are of such a nature that... I dare not flatter myself..." He trailed off.

Mrs. Dashwood stood stunned, unable to speak, as another awkward pause ensued. Finally, Willoughby broke the tension with a faint, weary smile. "It is folly to linger thus. I shall no longer torment myself by remaining among friends whose company I can no longer enjoy."

With that, he hastily took his leave of everyone and departed. They watched him mount his carriage, which vanished from sight within a minute. Overwhelmed with emotion, Mrs. Dashwood found no words and retreated to the adjoining room to process the alarm and grief caused by his abrupt departure.

Elinor's unease matched her mother's, bordering on anxiety and distrust. She replayed the events with growing worry. Willoughby's behavior in bidding farewell—his embarrassment, his forced cheerfulness, and most notably, his refusal to accept her mother's invitation—was profoundly disturbing. Such reluctance was utterly unlike a lover, and even more unlike the man she knew. In one breath, she feared he had never harbored serious intentions; in the next, she imagined a terrible quarrel between him and her sister. Marianne's anguish upon leaving the room suggested a serious dispute, yet given the depth of her love for him, a quarrel seemed almost inconceivable. Regardless of the specific cause, her sister's affliction was undeniable. Elinor reflected with tender pity on the violent sorrow Marianne was enduring, fearing it was not merely a release of pain but a self-imposed duty she was feeding and encouraging.

About half an hour later, her mother returned. Though her eyes were red-rimmed, her expression remained composed.

"Our dear Willoughby is now several miles from Barton, Elinor," she said, settling down to her work. "And with how heavy a heart does he travel?"

"It is all so strange," Elinor replied. "So sudden! It seems to have happened in the blink of an eye. Last night he was with us so happy, so cheerful, so affectionate. Now, after barely ten minutes' notice—he is gone, with no intention of returning! Something more than what he confessed must have occurred. He did not speak or behave like himself. You must have noticed the change as clearly as I. What could it be? Could they have quarreled? That is the only explanation for his such evident unwillingness to stay here."

"It was not a lack of inclination, Elinor; I perceived that clearly. He lacked the *power* to accept. I have thought it through thoroughly, and I can explain every aspect that initially seemed so peculiar to both of us."

"You can, indeed?"

"Yes. I have arrived at a conclusion that satisfies me completely—you, Elinor, who delight in doubting whenever certainty eludes you, may find it unsatisfactory. But you shall not talk me out of my conviction. I am persuaded that Mrs. Smith suspects Willoughby's regard for Marianne, disapproves of it—perhaps because she holds other designs for him—and is eager to remove him. Consequently, the 'business' sent to London is likely an invention designed to dismiss him. This is what I believe transpired. Furthermore, Willoughby knows she disapproves; he dares not yet confess his engagement to her. Bound by his dependent position, he feels compelled to acquiesce to her schemes and abscond from Devonshire temporarily.

"You will argue, I know, that this may or may not be true. But I invite no contradiction unless you can offer a more satisfactory interpretation. And now, Elinor, what is your verdict?"

"None, for you have anticipated my answer."

"Then you would have said it might or might not have happened. Oh, Elinor, how incomprehensible your feelings are! You prefer to assume evil on credit rather than good. You seek misery for Marianne and guilt for poor Willoughby instead of finding an apology for the latter. You are resolved to deem him blameworthy simply because he departed with less affection than his usual conduct displayed. Is no allowance to be made for inadvertence, or for spirits crushed by recent disappointment? Are no probabilities to be accepted merely because they lack absolute certainty? Is nothing owed to the man we all hold in such high esteem, and for whom there is no reason to harbor ill will? Must we ignore motives that are inherently sound yet necessarily secret for the time being? And ultimately, what exactly do you suspect him of?"

"I can hardly articulate it myself. But suspicion of something unpleasant is the inevitable result of such a drastic alteration in his demeanor. There is great truth, however, in what you have just argued regarding the allowances we owe him, and it is my wish to remain candid in judging others. Willoughby undoubtedly has sufficient reasons for his conduct, and I will hope that he does. Yet, it would have been more characteristic of Willoughby to acknowledge them forthwith. While secrecy may be prudent, I cannot help but wonder at its practice in this instance."

"Do not blame him for deviating from his character where necessity demands it. But surely you admit the justice of my defense? I am happy, and he is acquitted."

"Not entirely. Concealing their engagement—from Mrs. Smith, if they are indeed engaged—is understandable, and it is highly expedient for Willoughby to avoid Devonshire for the present. But this offers no excuse for hiding it from us."

"From us! My dear child, do you accuse Willoughby and Marianne of concealment? This is remarkable, considering your own eyes have reproached them daily for their incaution."

"I require no proof of their affection," Elinor insisted firmly. "But regarding their engagement, I do."

"I am perfectly satisfied of both."

"Yet not a single word has been spoken on the subject by either of them."

"I have required no words where actions have spoken so plainly. Has not his behavior toward Marianne and toward all of us for the past fortnight declared that he loves her and considers her his future wife? Did he not feel for us the attachment of the closest kin? Have we not perfectly understood one another? Has not my consent been sought daily through his looks, his manner, his attentive and affectionate respect? My Elinor, is it possible to doubt their engagement? How could such a thought occur to you? How can it be supposed that Willoughby, convinced as he must be of your sister's love, would leave her, potentially for months, without informing her of his affection? That they would part without exchanging a mutual confession

Marianne would have considered herself utterly inexcusable had she managed to sleep a single hour during the first night following her separation from Willoughby. Indeed, she would have been too ashamed to face her family the next morning had she not risen from her bed in a state of exhaustion greater than when she had lain down. Yet, the very emotions that rendered such composure a disgrace left her entirely free from the risk of incurring it. She remained awake throughout the entire night, weeping for the majority of it. She rose with a pounding headache, incapable of speech and refusing all nourishment, thereby inflicting constant pain upon her mother and sisters while sternly rejecting every attempt at consolation. Her capacity for sensitivity was formidable!

Once breakfast concluded, she ventured outdoors alone, wandering aimlessly through the village of Allenham. There, she indulged in memories of past joy while mourning the present reversal, spending the bulk of the morning in tears. The evening followed suit, marked by an equally unrestrained surrender to feeling. She revisited every favorite song she had once performed for Willoughby, every melody where their voices had harmonized most frequently. She sat before the instrument, staring intently at every line of music he had transcribed for her, until her heart grew so heavy that no further depth of sorrow could possibly be achieved; yet, this sustenance of grief was applied anew every single day. She devoted hours to the pianoforte, alternating between singing and sobbing, her voice often silenced completely by her tears. Similarly, in literature as well as music, she actively sought out the misery guaranteed by the stark contrast between the past and the present. She read nothing but works they had once enjoyed together.

Such violent affliction, however, could not endure indefinitely; within a few days, it subsided into a calmer, lingering melancholy. Nevertheless, her daily return to these pursuits—her solitary strolls and silent contemplations—still provoked occasional outbursts of sorrow as vivid as ever. Not a letter arrived from Willoughby, nor did any seem anticipated by Marianne. Her mother found the silence surprising, while Elinor grew increasingly uneasy. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood possessed the ability to conjure explanations whenever required, satisfying her own conscience at least. "Remember, Elinor," she remarked, "how frequently Sir John retrieves our mail personally and delivers it to the postbox ourselves. We have already agreed that secrecy may be necessary, and we must admit that it could not be preserved if their correspondence were to pass through Sir John's hands."

Elinor could not dispute the validity of this reasoning and attempted to locate a motive within it sufficient to explain their silence. However, there existed one method so direct, so simple, and in her view so prudent for discerning the true state of affairs—and instantly dispelling all mystery—that she could not refrain from suggesting it to her mother. "Why do you not simply ask Marianne," she proposed, "whether she is or is not engaged to Willoughby? From you, her mother—who is so kind and so indulgent—the question could cause no offense. It would naturally stem from your affection for her. She was wont to be entirely unreserved, and toward you, above all others."

"I would never dream of asking such a question," Mrs. Dashwood replied firmly. "Suppose, for argument's sake, that they are not engaged; what anguish would not such an inquiry inflict! At any rate, it would be most ungenerous. I should never deserve her confidence again after forcing from her a confession of what is currently meant to remain unacknowledged by anyone. I know Marianne's heart; I know she adores me, and I shall certainly be the first to whom the affair is revealed when circumstances render disclosure appropriate. I would not attempt to compel the confidence of anyone, let alone a child, for a sense of duty would prevent denying whatever direction her wishes might take."

Elinor deemed this display of generosity excessive, given her sister's youth, and pressed the matter further, but in vain. Common sense, common caution, and common prudence were all submerged beneath Mrs. Dashwood's romantic delicacy.

For several days, Willoughby's name was not mentioned before Marianne by any member of her family. Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, however, were not so fastidious; their witty remarks only added sting to many a painful hour. Then, one evening, Mrs. Dashwood, casually picking up a volume of Shakespeare, exclaimed, "We have never finished *Hamlet*, Marianne; our dear Willoughby departed before we could complete it. Let us set it aside, so that when he returns..."

"But it may be months, perhaps, before *that* happens," she continued hesitantly.

"Months!" Marianne cried, with profound astonishment.

"No—not many weeks," Mrs. Dashwood corrected, regretting her words immediately. Yet, it brought Elinor genuine pleasure, as the response elicited from Marianne expressed such unwavering confidence in Willoughby and clear knowledge of his intentions.

One morning, approximately a week after Willoughby's departure, Marianne was persuaded to join her sisters in their customary walk, rather than wandering off alone. Until now, she had meticulously avoided every companion during her excursions. If her sisters planned to traverse the downs, she would stealthily veer toward the lanes; if they discussed the valley, she would climb the hills with equal speed, ensuring she could never be found when the others set forth. Eventually, however, she was secured by Elinor's efforts, who strongly disapproved of such continuous isolation.

They proceeded along the road through the valley, moving mostly in silence, for Marianne's mind could not be restrained, and Elinor, content with having gained ground on one point, chose not to press further. Beyond the valley's entrance, where the landscape, though still fertile, appeared less wild and more open, a long stretch of the road they had traveled upon their initial arrival at Barton lay ahead. Upon reaching this vantage point, they paused to survey the surroundings, examining a prospect that formed the distant horizon from a spot they had never previously encountered in any of their walks.

Among the various objects in the scene, they quickly spotted an animated figure: a man on horseback riding toward them. Within minutes, they recognized him as a gentleman, and a moment later, Marianne erupted in rapture. "It is he! It is indeed—I know it is!" she cried, hastening to meet him.

"Eh, Marianne, I believe you are mistaken," Elinor interjected sharply. "It is not Willoughby. The person lacks his height, and possesses none of his bearing."

"He has it! He has it!" Marianne insisted vehemently. "His air, his coat, his horse. I knew exactly how swiftly he would arrive."

She walked eagerly forward as she spoke, while Elinor, wishing to shield Marianne from scrutiny since she was nearly certain it was not Willoughby, quickened her pace to keep abreast of her. Soon, they were within thirty yards of the approaching gentleman. Marianne glanced again; her heart sank within her. Abruptly turning, she hurried backward, only to be detained by the raised voices of both her sisters. A third voice, almost as familiar as Willoughby's, joined theirs in pleading for her to stay, causing her to turn in surprise to behold Edward Ferrars.

He was the only individual in the world who could, at that precise moment, be forgiven for failing to be Willoughby; the only one capable of earning a smile from her. Consequently, she banished her tears to offer a smile to *him*, and in her sister's apparent happiness, momentarily forgot her own disappointment. Dismounting and handing his horse to his servant, he walked back with them toward Barton, where he had purposefully come to pay them a visit.

He was welcomed by everyone with great cordiality, particularly by Marianne, who displayed a warmth of regard in receiving him that surpassed even Elinor's own. For Marianne, indeed, the encounter between Edward and her sister merely extended that inexplicable coldness she had often observed at Norland regarding their mutual behavior. On Edward's part, specifically, there was a distinct lack of everything a lover ought to exhibit or utter on such an occasion. He appeared confused, seemingly scarcely aware of the pleasure of seeing them, looking neither ecstatic nor cheerful. He spoke little, offering only responses forced from him by questioning, and distinguished Elinor with no mark of affection whatsoever.

Marianne watched and listened with growing astonishment. She began almost to feel a dislike for Edward; and as every feeling must eventually conclude for her, her thoughts returned to Willoughby, whose manners presented a contrast strikingly different from those of her supposed rival.

After a brief silence following the initial surprise and inquiries of their meeting, Marianne asked Edward if he had come directly from London.

"No," he replied, "I have been in Devonshire for two weeks."

"Two weeks!" she repeated, astonished that he had remained in the same county as Elinor for so long without seeing her beforehand. He appeared somewhat distressed as he added that he had been staying with friends near Plymouth.

"Have you been recently in Sussex?" Elinor inquired.

"I visited Norland about a month ago," Edward stated.

"And how does dear, dear Norland appear?" Marianne exclaimed warmly.

"Dear, dear Norland," Elinor responded dryly, "probably looks much as it always does at this time of year. The woods and paths are heavily covered with dead leaves."

"Oh!" cried Marianne, "with what transporting sensation have I formerly witnessed

Mrs. Dashwood's surprise at Edward's arrival lasted only a fleeting moment; in her view, his return to Barton was the most natural occurrence imaginable. Yet, while her initial wonder quickly faded, her joy and warm regard remained deep and enduring. She greeted him with such genuine kindness that any lingering shyness, coldness, or reserve on his part dissolved instantly under the weight of her hospitality. Even before he crossed the threshold, these barriers had begun to crumble, completely overwhelmed by Mrs. Dashwood's captivating warmth. Indeed, it is impossible for a man to harbor true affection for either of her daughters without eventually extending that feeling to their mother. Consequently, Elinor found great satisfaction in watching Edward gradually regain his usual composure. His affections seemed to reawaken toward them all, and his concern for their welfare became evident once more.

However, despite his attentiveness, his praise of their home, and his admiration of the view, it was clear that Edward was not truly in high spirits. The entire family sensed this shift, and Mrs. Dashwood, interpreting his gloom as a result of his mother's stinginess, sat down to dinner with a heart indignant against all selfish parents.

"What are Mrs. Ferrars's plans for you just now, Edward?" she asked after the meal, as they gathered around the fire. "Are you still destined to be a great orator, whether you wish it or not?"

"No," Edward replied. "I trust my mother is finally convinced that I possess neither the talent nor the inclination for a public life!"

"But how, then, is your reputation to be secured? For surely you must achieve fame to satisfy your family. With no desire for expense, no affection for strangers, no profession, and no assurance, establishing a name might prove difficult indeed."

"I shall make no attempt," Edward said firmly. "I have no wish to be distinguished, and I have every reason to hope I never shall. Thank heaven, I cannot be forced into genius or eloquence."

"You lack ambition, I am well aware," Mrs. Dashwood noted. "Your desires are entirely moderate."

"As moderate as those of the rest of the world, I believe," Edward countered. "I wish, as everyone else does, to be perfectly happy; but, like everyone else, it must be in my own way. Greatness will not secure that for me."

"Strange that it wouldn't!" cried Marianne. "What possible connection do wealth or grandeur have with happiness?"

"Grandeur has but little to do with it," Elinor interjected gently, "but wealth has much."

"Elinor, for shame!" Marianne exclaimed. "Money can only provide happiness where nothing else exists. Beyond a comfortable competency, it offers no real satisfaction regarding one's own inner state."

"Perhaps," Elinor smiled, "we shall arrive at the same conclusion. *Your* competency and *my* wealth are remarkably similar, I daresay. Without them, as the world currently stands, we would both agree that every form of external comfort is missing. Your ideals are simply nobler than mine. Come, what exactly constitutes your competency?"

"Between eighteen hundred and two thousand pounds a year; not more than that."

Elinor laughed softly. "*Two* thousand a year! *One* is my wealth! I knew exactly how this conversation would end."

"And yet, two thousand a year is a very modest income," Marianne declared. "A family cannot reasonably maintain itself on less. I assure you, I am not extravagant in my demands. A proper household staff, perhaps two carriages, and hunting horses cannot be sustained on anything smaller."

Elinor smiled again, hearing her sister describe their future expenses at Combe Magna with such precision.

"Hunting horses!" Edward repeated. "But why must you have them? Not everyone hunts."

Marianne blushed as she replied, "But most people do."

"I wish," said Margaret, suddenly striking upon a novel idea, "that someone would grant us all a large fortune individually!"

"Oh, that they would!" Marianne cried, her eyes sparkling with animation and her cheeks glowing with the delight of such imagined bliss.

"We are all unanimous in that wish, I suppose," Elinor remarked, "despite the acknowledged insufficiency of wealth."

"Oh dear!" Margaret sighed happily. "How joyful I should be! I wonder what I would do with it!"

Marianne appeared to have no doubts whatsoever on that score.

"I should be quite puzzled to spend such a vast fortune myself," Mrs. Dashwood admitted, "if my children were all to be wealthy without my assistance."

"You must begin your improvements on this house," Elinor suggested. "Then your difficulties will soon vanish."

"What magnificent orders would travel from this family to London in such an event!" Edward mused. "What a glorious day for booksellers, music merchants, and print shops! You, Miss Dashwood, would issue a general commission for every new print of merit to be sent to you. As for Marianne, knowing her magnanimous soul, there would not be enough music in all of London to content her. And books! Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she would purchase them all over and over again. I believe she would buy up every single copy to prevent them from falling into unworthy hands; she would acquire every volume that teaches her how to admire an ancient, twisted tree. Would you not, Marianne? Forgive me if I am being slightly saucy. I was merely wishing to demonstrate that I have not forgotten our old debates."

"I love to be reminded of the past, Edward," Marianne responded warmly. "Whether it be melancholy or cheerful, I cherish recalling it. You will never offend me by speaking of former times. You are quite correct in guessing how I would spend my money—at least some of it. My loose cash would certainly be invested in expanding my collection of music and books."

"And the bulk of your fortune?" Edward pressed playfully. "Would that be laid out in annuities on the authors or their heirs?"

"No, Edward, I would have something else to do with it."

"Perhaps, then, you would bestow it as a reward on the person who penned the most able defense of your favorite maxim—that no one can ever fall in love more than once in their lifetime? Your opinion on that point remains unchanged, I presume?"

"Undoubtedly," Marianne affirmed. "At my age, opinions are tolerably fixed. It is unlikely that I shall now see or hear anything capable of altering them."

"Marianne is as steadfast as ever, you see," Elinor observed. "She has not changed at all."

"She has only grown a little more grave than she was," Edward added.

"Nay, Edward," Marianne said, turning to him. "_You_ need not reproach me. You are not very gay yourself."

"Why should you think so!" he replied, letting out a sigh. "But gaiety has never been a part of my character."

"Nor do I consider it a part of Marianne's," Elinor stated calmly. "I would hardly call her a lively girl. She is very earnest, very eager in everything she undertakes. She sometimes talks a great deal and always with animation, but she is rarely truly merry."

"I believe you are right," Edward conceded. "And yet, I have always considered her a lively girl."

"I have frequently caught myself making such errors," Elinor confessed. "I have totally misunderstood characters in various ways, imagining people to be far more gay or grave, or ingenious or stupid than they truly are. I can hardly explain why or how this deception originates. Sometimes we are guided by what they say of themselves, and very often by what others say of them, without taking the time to deliberate and judge properly."

"But I thought it was right, Elinor," Marianne insisted, "to be guided wholly by the opinions of others. I believed our judgments were given merely to serve those of our neighbors. That has always been your doctrine, I am sure."

"No, Marianne, never. My doctrine has never sought the subjugation of the understanding. All I have ever attempted to influence is behavior. You must not confuse my meaning. I admit, I have often wished you to treat our acquaintances with greater attention; but when have I advised you to adopt their sentiments or conform to their judgment in serious matters?"

"You have not been able to bring your sister over to your plan of general civility," Edward turned to Elinor. "Do you gain no ground?"

"On the contrary," Elinor replied, looking expressively at Marianne.

"My judgment," Edward continued, addressing Elinor, "is entirely on your side of the question; but I fear my practice aligns much more closely with my sister's. I never wish to offend, yet I am so foolishly shy that I often appear negligent when I am merely held back by my natural awkwardness. I have frequently thought that nature must have intended me for low company, for I am so ill at ease among strangers of gentility!"

"Marianne has no shyness to excuse any inattention on her part," Elinor pointed out.

"She knows her own worth too well for false shame," Edward retorted. "Shyness is merely the effect of a sense of inferiority in some way. If I could convince myself that my manners were perfectly easy and graceful, I would not be shy."

"But you would still be reserved," Marianne added sharply. "And that is worse."

Edward started visibly. "Reserved! Am I reserved, Marianne?"

"Yes

Elinor watched her brother-in-law with deep concern, noting the heavy cloud that had settled over his spirits. His recent visit offered her only a fleeting comfort, especially since his own apparent enjoyment seemed so muted. It was painfully clear that he was unhappy. She desperately wished it were just as obvious that he still held for her the same affection she had once believed she inspired; yet, up until now, the persistence of his favor had remained dangerously uncertain. Furthermore, his reserved demeanor toward her constantly contradicted any hint of animation he might have shown moments prior.

The following morning, Edward joined Elinor and Marianne in the breakfast room before the rest of the household had risen. Marianne, ever eager to foster their happiness, quickly excused herself to leave them alone. However, barely halfway up the stairs, she heard the parlor door open. Turning around, she was startled to see Edward emerging. "I'm off to the village to check on my horses," he explained, "since you aren't ready for breakfast yet. I'll be right back."

When Edward returned, he spoke with renewed enthusiasm about the surrounding countryside. During his walk to the village, he had viewed various parts of the valley from advantageous angles, and the village itself, situated much higher than their cottage, provided a sweeping panorama that delighted him immensely. This topic naturally captured Marianne's attention. She began to describe her own awe of the scenery and questioned him in detail about the specific features that had struck him most.

Edward, however, cut her short. "You mustn't inquire too closely, Marianne," he warned. "Remember, I possess no knowledge of the picturesque. If we delve into specifics, I shall surely offend you with my ignorance and lack of taste. I tend to call hills 'steep' when they ought to be described as 'bold'; I label surfaces 'strange and uncouth' when they should be 'irregular and rugged'; and I note distant objects as merely 'out of sight,' whereas they should appear indistinct through the soft haze of a misty atmosphere. Please accept whatever admiration I can honestly offer. To me, it is simply a very fine country—the hills are steep, the woods brim with excellent timber, and the valley looks cozy and secure, dotted with rich meadows and neat farmhouses. It perfectly matches my definition of a beautiful place because it combines aesthetic appeal with practicality. And I daresay it is picturesque too, simply because you admire it. I can easily imagine it teeming with rocks, promontories, grey moss, and brushwood, though none of that registers with me. I know nothing of the picturesque."

"I fear it is indeed too true," Marianne admitted. "But why boast of such limitations?"

"Perhaps," Elinor interjected gently, "to avoid one form of pretension, Edward has fallen into another. Believing that many people feign a deeper appreciation for nature's beauties than they truly feel, and disgusted by such hypocrisy, he adopts an attitude of greater indifference and less discernment than he actually possesses. He is fastidious and has cultivated his own affectation."

"That is entirely accurate," Marianne agreed. "Admiration of landscape has devolved into mere jargon. Everyone pretends to feel and attempts to describe things with the refined taste of the man who first defined 'picturesque beauty.' I detest jargon of every sort, and often I keep my feelings to myself because I cannot find a language to express them that isn't worn, hackneyed, and devoid of meaning."

"I am convinced," Edward stated firmly, "that you genuinely feel all the delight in a fine prospect that you claim to feel. In return, I ask your sister to admit that I feel no more than I profess. I enjoy a fine view, but not according to picturesque principles. I dislike crooked, twisted, or blasted trees; I prefer them tall, straight, and flourishing. I have no fondness for ruined, tattered cottages. I am not enamored with nettles, thistles, or heather blossoms. A snug farmhouse brings me far more pleasure than a watchtower, and a group of tidy, happy villagers pleases me infinitely more than the finest bandits in the world."

Marianne stared at Edward with astonishment, while looking at her sister with profound pity. Elinor, meanwhile, only smiled. They did not pursue the subject further, and Marianne fell into a thoughtful silence until a new object caught her eye. Sitting beside Edward, she took her tea from Mrs. Dashwood. As Edward reached across to pass the cup, his hand brushed hers, revealing a ring on one of his fingers. At its center lay a small plait of hair, making it conspicuously visible.

"I've never seen you wearing a ring before, Edward!" Marianne exclaimed. "Is that Fanny's hair? I recall her promising to give you some. Though I suppose I expected her hair to be darker."

Marianne spoke impulsively, voicing exactly what she felt. Yet, upon seeing the pain her thoughtlessness caused Edward, her own regret was matched only by his distress. He flushed deeply, cast a quick, meaningful glance at Elinor, and replied, "Yes, it is my sister's hair. The setting tends to alter the shade slightly, you know."

Elinor had met his gaze and appeared equally conscious. Instantly, she realized the hair belonged to her. The only divergence between their thoughts was that where Marianne assumed it was a voluntary gift from her sister, Elinor knew it must have been obtained through some theft or deception unknown to her. Nevertheless, she was not in the mood to consider it an insult. Pretending to ignore the incident, she immediately changed the subject, but inwardly she resolved to seize every opportunity to examine the hair and confirm beyond doubt that its color matched her own exactly.

Edward's embarrassment lingered for some time, eventually settling into a profound preoccupation. He remained unusually grave throughout the entire morning. Marianne chided herself harshly for her remark, though her self-forgiveness might have been quicker had she understood how little offense it had truly caused her sister.

Before midday, they were visited by Sir John and Mrs. Jennings. Having heard news of a gentleman's arrival at the cottage, they came to inspect the guest. With the aid of his mother-in-law, Sir John was not long in deducing that the name Ferrars began with an "F." This discovery laid the groundwork for a future mine of teasing directed at the devoted Elinor—a jest that, had they known her longer, they might have launched immediately. For now, however, she gleaned only the extent of their insight from significant glances exchanged between them.

Sir John never failed to invite the Dashwoods either to dinner at the Park the following day or for tea that evening. On this occasion, wishing to ensure his guest's amusement, he proposed engaging them for both events.

"You *must* join us for tea tonight," he insisted, "for we shall be quite alone. And tomorrow, you must absolutely dine with us, as we shall be hosting a large party."

Mrs. Jennings reinforced the invitation. "And who knows but you might raise a dance," she added. "That would certainly tempt *you*, Miss Marianne."

"A dance!" Marianne cried out. "Impossible! Who is there to dance?"

"Why, yourself, of course, along with the Careys and the Whitakers!" Sir John replied cheerfully. "What! Did you think nobody could dance because a certain person, let's say nameless, has departed?"

"I wish with all my soul," Sir John declared, "that Willoughby were among us again."

These words, coupled with Marianne's sudden blush, sparked fresh suspicions in Edward's mind. "And who is Willoughby?" he asked quietly to Miss Dashwood, to whom he happened to be seated. She offered him a brief answer, but Marianne's face was far more expressive. Edward observed enough to understand not only the implications of the others' remarks but also the meanings behind Marianne's earlier puzzling expressions. Once their visitors departed, he moved immediately to stand beside her and whispered, "I've been guessing. Shall I share my theory?"

"What do you mean?"

"Shall I tell you?"

"Certainly."

"Well then," he said, "I guess Mr. Willoughby hunts."

Marianne was taken aback and flustered, yet she couldn't help but smile at the quiet wit of his manner. After a brief pause, she said, "Oh, Edward! How can you?—But perhaps the time will come, I hope... I am sure you will like him."

"I do not doubt it," he replied, somewhat surprised by her earnestness and warmth. Had he not assumed it was merely a lighthearted joke intended to entertain her acquaintances generally, based on nothing more substantial than a vague rumor regarding Mr. Willoughby and herself, he would never have dared to mention it.

Edward stayed at the cottage for a full week. Mrs. Dashwood urged him earnestly to remain longer, yet he seemed determined to depart just as his time among them reached its peak. For the final few days, though his spirits remained somewhat uneven, they had noticeably brightened. He grew increasingly fond of the house and its surroundings, sighing whenever anyone mentioned his departure. He insisted that his schedule was entirely free, even doubting where he would go once he left, yet he knew he must go. No week had ever flown by so quickly; he could scarcely believe it was truly over. He spoke often of this, along with other remarks that revealed the true shift in his feelings, contradicting his outward resolve. He found no joy at Norland, and he loathed life in town, yet somehow he was destined for one of those places. He cherished their kindness above all else, and his greatest happiness lay simply in being with them. Still, despite their wishes and his own reluctance, and without any restriction on his time, he had to leave.

Elinor attributed everything surprising about his behavior to his mother's influence. It was fortunate for her that her son's mother was so poorly understood by everyone else, serving as a convenient excuse for any strange conduct on Edward's part. Though disappointed and vexed, and occasionally displeased by his inconsistent treatment of herself, Elinor was generally inclined to view his actions with generous understanding—a leniency she had previously struggled to muster regarding Willoughby. His lack of spirit, openness, and consistency was usually blamed on his lack of independence and his awareness of Mrs. Ferrars' strict disposition and schemes. The brevity of his visit and his steadfast intention to leave stemmed from the same constrained inclination, the same unavoidable need to compromise with his mother. The age-old conflict between duty and desire, parent and child, was the root cause. She wished desperately to know when this struggle would end, when this opposition would yield, when Mrs. Ferrars would reform, and when her son would finally be free to be happy. Forced to turn from these vain hopes for comfort, she clung to her renewed faith in Edward's affection, recalling every sign of regard in his looks and words during his stay at Barton, and above all, the flattering ring he constantly wore on his finger.

"I think, Edward," Mrs. Dashwood remarked one last morning at breakfast, "that you would be a happier man if you had a profession to occupy your time and lend interest to your plans. Yes, there might be some inconvenience to your friends; you wouldn't be able to give them so much of your time. But (smiling) you would be materially benefited in at least one particular: you would know where to go when you leave us."

"I assure you," Edward replied, "that I have long considered this very point, just as you do now. It has been, and remains, and likely will always be, a heavy misfortune for me to have no necessary business to engage me, no profession to provide employment or any semblance of independence. Unfortunately, my own fastidiousness and that of my friends have rendered me what I am: an idle, helpless creature. We could never agree on a career. I always preferred the church, and still do. But that was not 'smart' enough for my family. They recommended the army. That was far too flashy for me. The law was deemed sufficiently genteel; many young men with chambers in the Temple made excellent appearances in high society and drove about town in stylish carriages. Yet I had no inclination for the law, even in this less abstract branch that my family approved. As for the navy, it certainly had fashion on its side, but I was too old when the topic was first raised to join it. Eventually, since there was no pressing necessity for me to hold any profession—I could be just as dashing and expensive without a red coat as with one—idleness was declared, overall, the most advantageous and honorable path. A young man of eighteen is rarely so eager to be busy as to resist his friends' pleas to do nothing. Thus, I was entered at Oxford and have been properly idle ever since."

"The consequence of which, I suppose, will be," Mrs. Dashwood countered, "that since leisure has not promoted your own happiness, your sons will be brought up to pursue as many careers, employments, professions, and trades as Columella's."

"They will be brought up," Edward said seriously, "to be as unlike myself as possible—in feeling, in action, in condition, in everything."

"Come, come!" Mrs. Dashwood dismissed him gently. "This is merely an overflow of your current lack of spirits, Edward. You are in a melancholy mood and imagine that anyone unlike yourself must be happy. Remember that the pain of parting from friends is felt by everyone, regardless of their education or station. Know your own happiness. You lack nothing but patience—or, if you prefer a more captivating term, call it hope. Your mother will secure you that independence you crave; it is her duty, and it will surely become her happiness to ensure your entire youth is not wasted in discontent. What cannot a few months achieve?"

"I think," Edward replied, "that I may defy many months to produce any good for me."

This despondent turn of mind, though hidden from Mrs. Dashwood, caused additional pain to everyone during the farewell, leaving an uncomfortable impression particularly on Elinor. Determined to overcome it and to avoid appearing to suffer more than the rest of her family, she did not follow the method Marianne had wisely adopted on similar occasions: seeking silence, solitude, and idleness to amplify and fix her sorrow. Their approaches differed as much as their goals, yet both served their purposes perfectly.

As soon as Edward left the house, Elinor sat at her drawing table, occupied throughout the day. She neither sought nor avoided mentioning his name, appearing almost as interested as ever in the family's general affairs. If this conduct failed to diminish her own grief, it at least prevented its unnecessary growth and spared her mother and sisters much worry. Such behavior, so diametrically opposed to Marianne's, held no merit in Marianne's eyes, just as Marianne's own had seemed flawed to Elinor. The task of self-command came easily to her; with strong affections, it was impossible, but with calm ones, it required no virtue. That her sister's affections *were* calm, Elinor dared not deny, though she blushed to admit it. Regarding the strength of her own, she offered striking proof by continuing to love and respect her sister, despite the mortifying realization of their difference.

Without isolating herself from her family or retreating into determined solitude, Elinor found that every day afforded her ample leisure to reflect on Edward and his behavior, exploring every nuance her varying moods could conjure—with tenderness, pity, approval, criticism, and doubt. There were countless moments when, due to the absence of her mother and sisters or the nature of their tasks, conversation was stifled, creating an atmosphere of enforced solitude. Her mind was inevitably free; her thoughts could not be chained elsewhere. Consequently, the past and future concerning such an engaging subject filled her presence, forcing her attention and consuming her memory, reflection, and imagination.

One morning, shortly after Edward's departure, a reverie at her drawing table was broken by the arrival of visitors. Happening to be alone, she noticed the small gate at the entrance of the green court close, catching her eye toward the window. She saw a large group approaching the door. Among them were Sir John and Lady Middleton, along with Mrs. Jennings, but there were also two strangers, a gentleman and a lady, whom she did not recognize. Sitting near the window, as soon as Sir John spotted her, he left the others to handle the knock, stepping across the grass and compelling her to open the casement to speak to him, despite the narrow gap making it nearly impossible to converse without being overheard.

"Well," he said, "we've brought you some strangers. How do you like them?"

"Hush! They'll hear you."

"Never mind if they do. It's only the Palmers. Charlotte is very pretty, I can tell you. You can see her if you look this way."

Certain she would see her within moments without granting that courtesy, Elinor politely requested to be excused.

"Where is Marianne? Has she fled because we arrived? I see her instrument is open."

"She is walking, I believe."

Mrs. Jennings then joined them, lacking the patience to wait for the door to open before sharing her tale. She shouted toward the window, "How do you do, my dear? How is Mrs. Dashwood? And where are your sisters? What! All alone! You'll be delighted with a bit of company to keep you. I've brought my other son and daughter to see you. Just think of their sudden arrival! I thought I heard a carriage last night while we were drinking tea, but it never occurred to me it could be them. I was thinking only of whether it mightn't be Colonel Brandon returning. So I said to Sir John, 'I do think I hear a carriage; perhaps it is Colonel Brandon come back again.'"

Elinor was compelled to turn away mid-story to welcome the rest of the party. Lady Middleton introduced the two strangers; Mrs. Dashwood and Margaret descended the stairs simultaneously, and they all sat staring at one another while Mrs. Jennings continued her narrative as she walked through the passage into the parlor, followed by Sir John.

Mrs. Palmer was several years younger than Lady Middleton and utterly unlike her in every way. She was short and plump, possessed a very lovely face, and radiated the finest expression

The following day, as the Dashwood sisters entered the drawing-room of the Park at one door, Mrs. Palmer burst in at the opposite entrance, radiating a cheerful merriment that seemed entirely restored. With affectionate warmth, she greeted them all, expressing profound delight at their return. "How glad I am to see you!" she exclaimed, settling herself between Elinor and Marianne. "For it has been such a dreadful day, I feared you wouldn't come, which would have been truly shocking, considering we depart again tomorrow. We simply must go; the Westons arrive next week, you know. It was quite impulsive on our part—we knew nothing until the carriage pulled up to the door, and then Mr. Palmer merely asked if I would accompany him to Barton. He is so whimsical! He rarely tells me anything beforehand. I am terribly sorry we cannot stay longer; however, I trust we shall meet again in London very soon."

They felt compelled to dampen this hopeful anticipation. "Not go to town?" Mrs. Palmer cried, laughing. "I shall be utterly disappointed if you do not. I could secure the finest house imaginable for you, right next door to ours in Hanover Square. You must come, indeed. I am certain I would be delighted to act as your chaperone at any time until I am confined, should Mrs. Dashwood prefer avoiding public society." They offered thanks but firmly resisted her persistent entreaties.

"Oh, my dear," Mrs. Palmer called out to her husband, who had just entered the room, "you must assist me in persuading the Miss Dashwoods to spend the winter in London." Her husband offered no reply; after a slight bow to the ladies, he launched into a complaint about the weather. "How horrid everything is!" he declared. "Weather like this renders everyone and everything repugnant. Dullness is generated indoors just as readily as outdoors by the rain; it makes one detest all acquaintances. What on earth does Sir John mean by lacking a billiard room in his house? How few people understand true comfort! Sir John is as obtuse as the weather itself."

Shortly thereafter, the remainder of the party arrived. "I fear, Miss Marianne," Sir John remarked, "that you were unable to take your customary walk to Allenham today." Marianne appeared deeply somber and remained silent. "Oh, don't be so secretive before us," Mrs. Palmer teased. "We know all about it, I assure you. Besides, I admire your taste immensely; I find him extremely handsome. We do not reside far from him in the countryside, you know—not more than ten miles, I daresay."

"Much closer to thirty," her husband interjected.

"Ay, well! There isn't much difference. I have never visited his home, but they say it is a lovely, pretty place."

"As vile a spot as I have ever seen in my life," Mr. Palmer countered sharply.

Marianne remained perfectly silent, though her expression clearly betrayed her intense interest in the conversation. "Is it very ugly?" Mrs. Palmer pressed. "Then it must be some other location that is so charming, I suppose."
