When they moved to the dining room, Sir John noted with regret that there were only eight of them present. "My dear," he said to his wife, "it is exceedingly vexing that we are so few. Why didn't you invite the Gilberts to join us today?"

"Didn't I tell you, Sir John, when you first suggested it, that it wasn't possible? They dined with us yesterday."

"You and I, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings remarked, "should not stand on such formalities."

"Then you would be dreadfully ill-bred," Mr. Palmer retorted.

"My love, you contradict everyone," his wife replied with her characteristic laughter.

"Do you realize you are being quite rude?"

"I did not know I was contradicting anyone by calling your mother ill-bred."

"Ay, you may abuse me as you please," the kindly old lady said. "You have taken Charlotte off my hands, and you cannot give her back. So, there, I hold the upper hand over you." Charlotte laughed heartily at the thought that her husband could not escape her, exclaiming triumphantly that she cared not how cross he became, for they were bound to live together forever.

It was impossible to find someone more thoroughly good-natured or more determined to cultivate happiness than Mrs. Palmer. Her husband's calculated indifference, insolence, and dissatisfaction caused her no distress; indeed, when he scolded or insulted her, she found it highly diverting. "Mr. Palmer is so droll!" she whispered to Elinor. "He is perpetually in a bad mood."

Elinor, after observing him closely, was disinclined to accept his claim of genuine, unfeigned ill-nature or rudeness. Perhaps his temperament had become somewhat sour due to discovering, like many men, that through some inexplicable bias toward beauty, he had married a very foolish woman. Yet, she recognized that such errors were too common for any sensible man to suffer lasting harm from them. Rather, she believed it was a desire for distinction—a wish to appear superior—that drove his contemptuous treatment of others and his general denigration of everything around him. The motive was commonplace enough not to warrant wonder, but the methods, however successful they might be in establishing his superiority through bad breeding, were unlikely to attract anyone to him save his wife.

"Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood," Mrs. Palmer resumed shortly afterward, "I have a favor to request of you and your sister. Would you consider spending some time at Cleveland this Christmas? Please, do—and come while the Westons are still with us. You cannot imagine how happy I shall be! It will be absolutely delightful!—My love," turning to her husband, "do you not long to have the Miss Dashwoods visit Cleveland?"

"Certainly," he replied with a sneer—"I came to Devonshire with no other purpose."

"There now," she said, "you see Mr. Palmer expects you; therefore, you cannot refuse."

Both sisters eagerly and resolutely declined her invitation.

"But indeed you must and shall come. I am sure you will enjoy it above all else. The Westons will be with us, making it quite delightful. You cannot conceive what a charming place Cleveland is; moreover, we are so lively now, for Mr. Palmer is constantly traveling the country campaigning for the election, and so many people have come to dine with us that I have never seen before—it is simply enchanting! But, poor fellow! It is very exhausting for him, for he is forced to make everyone like him."

Elinor could scarcely maintain her composure as she acknowledged the hardship of such an obligation. "How charming it will be," Charlotte added, "once he enters Parliament! Won't it? How I shall laugh! It will be so absurd to see all his letters addressed to him with 'M.P.' appended. But do you know, he insists he will never frank them for me? He declares he won't. Don't you, Mr. Palmer?"

Mr. Palmer ignored her completely.

"He cannot bear writing, you know," she continued. "He says it is quite shocking."

"No," he replied, "I never said anything so irrational. Do not attribute all your misuse of language to me."

"There now; you see how droll he is. This is always his manner! Sometimes he refuses to speak to me for half a day, and then suddenly bursts forth with something so witty—about anything in the world."

She greatly surprised Elinor as they returned to the drawing-room by asking if she did not like Mr. Palmer excessively.

"Certainly," Elinor answered. "He seems very agreeable."

"Well, I am so glad you feel that way. I thought you would; he is so pleasant. And Mr. Palmer is excessively pleased with you and your sisters, I can tell you. You cannot imagine how disappointed he will be if you do not come to Cleveland. I cannot fathom why you should object."

Elinor was once again compelled to decline, putting a halt to her entreaties by shifting the topic. She hoped that, living in the same county, Mrs. Palmer might provide a more detailed account of Willoughby's general character than could be gleaned from the Middletons' limited acquaintance. Eager to obtain confirmation of his virtues sufficient to alleviate Marianne's fears, she began by inquiring if they saw much of Mr. Willoughby at Cleveland and whether they were intimate with him.

"Oh, dear, yes; I know him extremely well," Mrs. Palmer responded. "Though I never actually spoke to him, indeed; I have merely seen him frequently in town. Somehow, I never happened to be staying at Barton while he was at Allenham. My mother saw him here once before, but I was with my uncle at Weymouth. However, I suppose we should have encountered him often in Somersetshire if it hadn't unfortunately occurred that we were never in the country simultaneously. He is very seldom at Combe, I believe; but even if he were there more often, I doubt Mr. Palmer would visit him, for he is in the opposition, you know, and besides, it is so remote. I know precisely why you are inquiring about him; your sister intends to marry him. I am monstrously glad of it, for then I shall have her as a neighbor."

"Upon my word," Elinor replied, "you seem to know much more about the matter than I do, if you have grounds to expect such a union."

"Don't

The Palmers departed for Cleveland the following day, leaving the households at Barton Park to their own devices. However, this solitude proved fleeting. Elinor had scarcely finished reflecting on Charlotte's inexplicable happiness, Mr. Palmer's surprisingly simple demeanor despite his evident talents, and the peculiar disconnects that often marred marital unions, before Sir John and Mrs. Jennings' fervent dedication to social engagement introduced her to fresh acquaintances. During a morning outing to Exeter, they encountered two young ladies whom Mrs. Jennings delightedly identified as relatives. This discovery prompted Sir John to immediately extend an invitation for them to visit the park once their business in Exeter concluded.

Lady Middleton received news of this impending visit with genuine alarm. She had never laid eyes on these strangers, possessed no evidence of their refinement or gentility, and found her husband's and mother's assurances utterly unpersuasive. The fact that they were her kin only compounded her dread. Mrs. Jennings attempted to soothe her daughter, advising her not to mind their lack of fashion, arguing that as cousins, they were bound to tolerate one another regardless. Yet, with no possibility of preventing their arrival, Lady Middleton resigned herself to the situation with the practiced stoicism of a well-bred woman, reserving her complaints for frequent, gentle reprimands directed daily at her husband.

When the sisters finally arrived, their appearance was neither ungenteel nor unfashionable. Dressed sharply and behaving with civility, they were enchanted by the house and rapturous over the furnishings. Furthermore, possessing an overwhelming affection for children, they won Lady Middleton's favor within the hour. Her ladyship declared them delightful girls—a sentiment of enthusiastic admiration in her household. Sir John's confidence swelled with this praise, and he hastened to the cottage to announce the Steeles' arrival to the Dashwood sisters, hailing them as the sweetest girls in existence.

Elinor, however, remained skeptical. She knew that "sweet" girls could be found everywhere in England, varying widely in form, face, temperament, and intellect. Sir John insisted that the entire family accompany him to the park to meet his guests. "Come now!" he urged. "Pray come—you must come! You'll adore them. Lucy is monstrously pretty, so good-humored, and agreeable! The children are already clinging to her as if she were an old friend. Both sisters are dying to see you; they've heard at Exeter that you are the most beautiful creatures in the world, and I've confirmed it for them, adding much more besides. You'll be delighted. They've filled the carriage with toys for the children. How can you be so cross? Besides, you're my cousins, and they are my wife's cousins, so you must be related." Despite his persistence, he failed to secure immediate company, managing only a promise of a visit within a day or two. Disappointed by their indifference, he returned home to boast anew to the Miss Steeles.

At the scheduled meeting, the older sister, nearing thirty, presented a plain and unsympathetic countenance, offering little to admire. The younger, however, aged barely twenty-three, displayed considerable beauty. Her features were pleasing, accompanied by a sharp, quick eye and a brisk air that lacked true elegance but granted her distinct presence. Their manners were notably civil, and Elinor quickly credited them with a measure of sense, observing how diligently and judiciously they endeavored to please Lady Middleton. With the children, they were in a state of perpetual ecstasy, praising their looks, seeking their attention, and accommodating their whims. Any spare moments were devoted to admiring Lady Middleton's activities or copying patterns from her latest dresses, which had captivated them previously.

Fortunately for those who court through such minor vices, a mother obsessed with praising her children is simultaneously the most demanding and the most gullible of humans. Her expectations are extreme, yet she accepts anything. Consequently, the Miss Steeles' excessive affection and patience toward Lady Middleton's offspring were viewed by the hostess without suspicion. She watched with maternal satisfaction as her cousins endured impertinent intrusions and mischievous pranks. She saw their ribbons loosened, hair tugged, bags rifled, and cutlery stolen, assuming it was mutual enjoyment. The only surprise she registered was that Elinor and Marianne sat so calmly by, seemingly uninterested in the commotion.

"John is in such high spirits today!" she exclaimed when Sir John tossed a pocket-handkerchief out the window. "Full of monkey tricks." Shortly after, upon seeing the second boy pinch one of the sisters' fingers, she fondly remarked, "How playful William is!" Then, tenderly embracing a three-year-old girl named Annamaria, who had been silent for two minutes, she proclaimed, "Here is my sweet little Annamaria... Never was there such a quiet little thing!" Unfortunately, the pin in her bonnet caught the child's neck during the embrace, eliciting screams so violent they rivaled any naturally noisy creature.

Lady Middleton's consternation was profound, but it paled compared to the panic of the Miss Steeles. All three acted swiftly to soothe the victim. The child was cradled in her mother's lap, kissed incessantly, treated with lavender water by one sister kneeling beside her, and fed sugar plums by the other. Yet, rewarded thus, the child continued to scream and kick her brothers, rendering all united efforts futile until Lady Middleton recalled a previous incident where apricot marmalade cured a bruised temple. Proposing the same remedy, they noted a brief cessation in the screaming, fostering hope. The child was carried out to seek the medicine, followed by the two eager boys, leaving the four young ladies alone in a silence unknown to the room for hours.

"Poor little creatures," sighed Miss Steele as the children departed. "It might have been a very sad accident."
"I hardly know how," cried Marianne, "unless it had been under totally different circumstances. But this is the usual method of heightening alarm where there is nothing truly alarming."
"What a sweet woman Lady Middleton is!" observed Lucy Steele.
Marianne remained silent; she could not feign feelings she did not possess, however trivial the occasion. Thus, the burden of lying for the sake of politeness fell entirely upon Elinor. She managed the situation by speaking of Lady Middleton with more warmth than she felt, though far less effusively than Lucy.

"And Sir John too!" cried the elder sister. "What a charming man he is!"
Again, Elinor's simple, just commendation lacked the éclat of her companion's enthusiasm. She merely noted that he was perfectly good-humored and friendly.
"And what a charming little family they have! I never saw such fine children in my life. I declare I quite doat upon them already, and indeed I am always distractedly fond of children."
"I should guess so," Elinor smiled, "given what I witnessed this morning."
"I have a notion," said Lucy, "that you think the little Middletons rather too indulged; perhaps they are spoiled enough. But it is so natural in Lady Middleton. For my part, I love to see children full of life and spirit; I cannot bear them if they are tame and quiet."
"I confess," replied Elinor, "that while I am at Barton Park, I never think of tame and quiet children with any abhorrence."

A short pause ensued, broken by Miss Steele, who seemed eager for conversation. "And how do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood?" she asked abruptly. "I suppose you were very sorry to leave Sussex."
Surprised by the familiarity, Elinor admitted she was.
"Norland is a prodigious beautiful place, isn't it?" added Miss Steele.
"We've heard Sir John admire it excessively," said Lucy, offering a mild apology for her sister's boldness.
"I think everyone *must* admire it," Elinor replied. "Though it is not to be supposed that anyone can estimate its beauties as we do."
"And had you a great many smart beaux there? I suppose you haven't so many here; for my part, I think they are a vast addition always."
"But why should you think," Lucy interjected, looking ashamed, "that there aren't as many genteel young men in Devonshire as Sussex?"
"Nay, my dear, I'm sure I don't pretend to say there aren't. There are plenty in Exeter. But I couldn't tell about Norland, and I was only afraid the Miss Dashwoods might find it dull without them. Perhaps you young ladies don't care about beaux and'd rather be without them. For my part, I think they're vastly agreeable, provided they dress smart and behave civil. But I can't bear them dirty and nasty. Now, take Mr. Rose at Exeter—a prodigiously smart young man, a clerk to Mr. Simpson, quite a beau. Yet if you meet him in the morning, he's not fit to be seen. Suppose your brother was quite a beau before he married, Miss Dashwood, since he was so rich?"
"Upon my word," Elinor responded, "I cannot tell you, for I do not perfectly comprehend the meaning of the word. But this I can say: if he ever was a beau before marriage, he is one still, for there is not the slightest alteration in him."
"Oh! dear! One never thinks of married men being beaux—they have something else to do."
"Lord! Anne," cried her sister, "you can talk of nothing but beaux; you'll make Miss Dashwood believe you think of nothing else." Turning the topic, she began admiring the

Marianne, who possessed little patience for anything resembling impudence, vulgarity, mediocrity, or merely differing tastes, was currently in such a fragile state of spirit that she was entirely incapable of finding pleasure in the presence of the Miss Steeles or encouraging their overtures. Consequently, her behavior toward them remained consistently cool, effectively halting any attempt at intimacy on their part. It was largely due to this unwavering reserve that Elinor observed a distinct shift in the sisters' demeanor; they began to favor her specifically, a preference that grew increasingly obvious, particularly in Lucy's actions. Lucy seized every opportunity to engage Elinor in conversation, striving to deepen their acquaintance through an air of ease and frankness.

Lucy was naturally quick-witted; her observations were often astute and amusing, and as a companion for thirty minutes, she could prove genuinely agreeable. However, her intellect had received no cultivation from formal education. She was ignorant and unlettered, and her lack of mental refinement, coupled with a deficit of general knowledge, could not be hidden from Miss Dashwood despite Elinor's best efforts to maintain a favorable impression. Elinor viewed Lucy's wasted potential with pity, mourning the talents that education might have dignified. Yet, she regarded Lucy's utter lack of delicacy, moral uprightness, and integrity with far less compassion. These deficiencies were glaringly exposed by Lucy's relentless attentions, her persistence, and her flattery at the Park. Elinor found no lasting satisfaction in the company of someone who combined insincerity with ignorance; whose lack of instruction prevented them from conversing as equals, and whose treatment of others rendered any display of deference toward herself utterly hollow.

"You will probably find my question peculiar," Lucy remarked one day as they strolled from the park toward the cottage. "But pray, are you personally acquainted with your sister-in-law's mother, Mrs. Ferrars?"

Elinor *did* consider the query highly unusual, and her expression reflected this as she replied that she had never met Mrs. Ferrars.

"Indeed!" exclaimed Lucy. "I am surprised by that, for I assumed you must have encountered her occasionally at Norland. Then, perhaps, you cannot describe her character?"

"No," Elinor responded, carefully withholding her true opinion of Edward's mother and disinclined to satisfy what appeared to be idle curiosity. "I know nothing of her."

"I am certain you think me strangely inquisitive for asking in such a manner," Lucy said, watching Elinor intently. "Perhaps there are reasons... I hope I may venture; but regardless, I trust you will grant me the justice of believing I have no intention of being rude."

Elinor offered a polite response, and they walked in silence for several minutes until Lucy broke it, hesitating slightly. "I cannot bear for you to view me as curiously intrusive. I would sooner endure anything in the world than lose your good opinion, which is so valuable to me. I would gladly trust you; indeed, I would welcome your counsel regarding such an uncomfortable position as mine. However, there is no need to trouble you. I am simply sorry you do not happen to know Mrs. Ferrars."

"I am sorry I do *not*," Elinor said, taken aback. "If knowing my opinion of her could benefit *you*, I regret it. But truly, I never realized you had any connection with that family, which makes this serious inquiry into her character somewhat surprising."

"I suppose you are right, and I do not wonder at your reaction. But if I were to reveal everything, you would not be so astonished. Mrs. Ferrars is certainly irrelevant to me at present—but the future *may* bring changes. How quickly that arrives depends entirely on her; eventually, we may become intimately connected." She lowered her gaze, feigning bashfulness, casting only a fleeting glance at her companion to gauge her reaction.

"Good heavens!" cried Elinor. "What do you mean? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Could it be?" The prospect of such a sister-in-law held little appeal for her.

"No," Lucy replied. "Not Mr. *Robert* Ferrars—I have never seen him in my life; but..." She fixed her eyes upon Elinor. "...to his eldest brother."

What surged through Elinor at that moment was a profound astonishment, sharp and intense, tempered only by an immediate skepticism of the claim. She turned to Lucy in silent bewilderment, unable to fathom the motive behind such a declaration. Though her complexion flushed, she maintained her composure, resisting the urge to faint or succumb to hysteria.

"You have ample cause for surprise," Lucy continued. "Naturally, you knew nothing of this before. I daresay he never hinted at it to you or anyone in your family, for it was intended as a strict secret, which I have diligently guarded to this hour. No one among my relations knows except Anne, and I would never have disclosed it to you had I not felt absolute reliance on your discretion. I truly believed my persistent questioning about Mrs. Ferrars seemed so odd that it required explanation. I also do not think Mr. Ferrars would be displeased that I have entrusted you with this, since I know he holds the highest regard for your entire family and considers you and the other Miss Dashwoods akin to his own sisters."

She paused. Elinor remained silent for a few moments. Her initial shock was too overwhelming for speech, but gradually, she forced herself to speak, choosing her words with caution. With a calm demeanor that successfully masked her surprise and anxiety, she asked, "May I inquire if your engagement has been of long duration?"

"We have been engaged for four years."

"Four years!"

"Yes."

Though deeply shaken, Elinor still found it difficult to accept. "I was unaware," she said, "that you were even acquainted until recently."

"Our acquaintance, however, dates back many years. He was under my uncle's tutelage for a significant period."

"My uncle!"

"Yes; Mr. Pratt. Have you never heard him speak of Mr. Pratt?"

"I believe I have," Elinor replied, summoning strength as her emotions intensified. "He spent four years with my uncle, who resides at Longstaple, near Plymouth. That is where our acquaintance began, as my sister and I stayed with my uncle frequently. Our engagement was formed there, though not until a year after he ceased being a pupil; yet, he remained almost constantly with us thereafter. I was initially reluctant to proceed without my mother's knowledge and approval, but I was too young and loved him too dearly to exercise the prudence I should have shown. Although you do not know him as well as I do, Miss Dashwood, you must have observed enough to realize he is fully capable of inspiring sincere devotion in a woman."

"Certainly," Elinor answered, unsure of her own words. After a brief pause, she added, bolstered by her faith in Edward's honor and love, alongside her conviction of Lucy's deceit, "Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars! I confess I am so utterly stunned by your revelation that I must assume there is a mistake of identity or name. We cannot possibly mean the same Mr. Ferrars."

"We mean no other," Lucy cried, smiling. "Mr. Edward Ferrars, the eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars of Park Street and brother of your sister-in-law, Mrs. John Dashwood, is precisely whom I refer to. You must admit I am unlikely to be mistaken regarding the name of the man on whom my happiness rests."

"It is strange," Elinor murmured, trapped in agonizing confusion, "that I should never have heard him mention your name."

"No; given our circumstances, it was not strange. Our primary concern has been secrecy. You knew nothing of me or my family, so there was no occasion to mention my name. Furthermore, since he was perpetually fearful of his sister suspecting anything, that alone was sufficient reason for his silence."

She fell silent. Elinor's sense of security crumbled, though her self-control held firm. "For four years you have been engaged," she stated with steady resolve.

"Yes; and Heaven knows how much longer we may wait. Poor Edward! It leaves him completely disheartened." Taking a small miniature from her pocket, she added, "To avoid any misunderstanding, please examine this portrait. It fails to capture his true beauty, certainly, but I believe you cannot mistake the likeness. I have possessed it for over three years."

As she handed it to Elinor, the sight of the painting silenced all lingering doubts. Whatever fears Elinor harbored regarding hasty judgments or deception vanished instantly. She acknowledged the resemblance immediately and returned it.

"I have never been able," Lucy continued, "to reciprocate with my portrait, which troubles me greatly, as he has always been so eager to obtain it! I am resolved to send it the very next opportunity."

"You are entirely correct," Elinor replied calmly.

They walked a few steps in silence before Lucy spoke again. "I am absolutely certain," she said, "of your fidelity in keeping this secret, for you must understand its critical importance to us. Its exposure to his mother would be disastrous; she would never approve, I daresay. I possess no fortune, and I suspect she is an exceedingly proud woman."

"I certainly did not solicit your confidence," Elinor noted. "Yet, you do me no injustice in assuming I can be relied upon. Your secret is safe with me; however, forgive me if I express surprise at such an unnecessary disclosure. You must

Though Elinor generally relied heavily on Lucy's word, a moment of serious reflection made it impossible for her to harbor any doubt about the current situation. There was simply no conceivable motive for Lucy to fabricate a lie of such magnitude. Therefore, Elinor could no longer, and dared not, question what Lucy had claimed. This assertion was bolstered by overwhelming probability and corroborating evidence, standing in stark contrast to nothing but Elinor's own desperate wishes. The foundation of their past acquaintance at Mr. Pratt's house rendered the rest of the story undeniable yet deeply alarming. When combined with Edward's recent visit near Plymouth, his evident melancholy, his dissatisfaction with his prospects, his erratic behavior toward Elinor, and the Miss Steeles' surprisingly intimate knowledge of Norland and the Dashwoods, the evidence became complete. Along with the picture, the letter, and the ring, these facts formed an irrefutable body of proof that silenced every fear of unfair judgment. It was established as an absolute truth that Edward had treated her poorly—a reality no amount of partiality could erase.

Initially, Elinor's resentment and indignation at having been duped caused her to focus solely on her own pain. However, other thoughts quickly surfaced. *Had Edward been intentionally deceiving her?* *Had he feigned feelings he did not possess?* *Was his engagement to Lucy one of the heart?* No. Whatever sentiment might have existed in the past, she could not believe it persisted now. His affection belonged entirely to her; she could not be deceived regarding that. Her mother, sisters, and Fanny had all witnessed his devotion at Norland; it was not a product of her own vanity. He certainly loved her.

What a soothing realization this proved to be! How much it tempted her to forgive! While Edward was undeniably blameworthy for staying at Norland once he realized the depth of his influence over her, he could not be excused for that. Yet, if he had harmed her, he had inflicted far greater injury upon himself. If her situation was pitiable, his was truly hopeless. His imprudence had made her suffer briefly, but it seemed to have stripped him of any chance of happiness. She might eventually regain her peace, but what did he have to look forward to? Could he possibly be content with Lucy Steele? Even setting aside his love for Elinor, could a man of his integrity, delicacy, and cultivated mind be satisfied with a wife who was illiterate, artful, and selfish? The youthful infatuation of nineteen might blind him to everything but beauty and good nature, but the next four years—if spent wisely—would surely open his eyes to her lack of education. Meanwhile, those same years spent in inferior society and frivolous pursuits might have eroded the very simplicity that once gave her charm. Furthermore, if the prospect of marrying against his mother's wishes had seemed daunting before, how much harder it must be now, knowing his fiancée was undoubtedly inferior in both connection and fortune. With a heart so alienated from Lucy, these difficulties might not weigh heavily on his patience; yet, melancholy was the fate of anyone who found relief in expecting family opposition and unkindness.

As these painful thoughts succeeded one another, Elinor wept—not for herself, but for him. Supported by the conviction that she had done nothing to deserve her misery, and consoled by the belief that Edward had forfeited nothing of her esteem, she felt strong enough to shield her mother and sisters from the full weight of the truth. Indeed, she managed so well that when she joined them at dinner just two hours after her hopes were extinguished, no one could guess that Elinor was secretly mourning obstacles that would forever separate her from her beloved. Nor could anyone imagine that Marianne was inwardly obsessing over the perfection of a man whose heart she believed fully hers, whom she expected to see in every carriage passing their home.

The necessity of keeping this secret from her mother and Marianne, though demanding constant effort, was actually a relief to Elinor. It spared them the agony of the news and protected them from the harsh condemnation of Edward that flowed from their excessive adoration of her—condemnation she felt unable to withstand. Knowing that their tender sorrow would only deepen her distress, and that their advice offered no solace, she found strength in solitude. Her own good sense upheld her firmness; her cheerfulness remained unshakable despite the poignancy of her grief.

Soon, however, Elinor felt an urgent desire to speak with Lucy again. She needed to hear the details of their engagement reiterated, to better understand the sincerity of Lucy's declared affection, and above all, to demonstrate through her calm demeanor that she viewed Lucy merely as a friend. She feared her earlier agitation during their morning talk had cast doubt on this stance. It was obvious that Lucy was jealous; Edward had praised her highly, trusting her with such a vital secret based on such brief acquaintance, and even Sir John's teasing remarks must have played a part. But given Elinor's certainty of Edward's love, jealousy was a natural reaction for Lucy. Her very willingness to confess the affair proved it. Elinor understood Lucy's motives: to inform her of her "superior" claim on Edward and teach her to stay away. Resolved to act with honor and to see Edward as little as possible, Elinor could not deny herself the comfort of trying to reassure Lucy that her own heart was unharmed. Since there was nothing more painful to hear than what had already been revealed, she trusted she could recount the events with composure.

Yet, opportunities were scarce. The weather rarely permitted walks where they could speak privately. Though they met frequently at the park or cottage, these gatherings were strictly social affairs arranged by Sir John and Lady Middleton for games and laughter, leaving no room for deep conversation. Finally, Sir John arrived at the cottage one morning, inviting everyone to dine with Lady Middleton at the Park House. He claimed he must attend a club meeting in Exeter and feared she would be utterly alone with her mother and the Miss Steeles. Elinor saw this as a perfect opportunity. A group including Lady Middleton would offer a quieter atmosphere than the chaotic energy of Sir John's presence. Margaret agreed readily, and Marianne, though reluctant, was persuaded by her mother to accompany them.

The gathering was exactly as dull as Elinor anticipated. The conversation lacked novelty, and the presence of the children in the drawing room made it impossible to engage Lucy. Once the tea things were cleared, the card tables were set up. Elinor wondered why she had ever hoped to find time for serious talk at the park. Everyone prepared for a game of Casino.

"I am glad," Lady Middleton said to Lucy, "that you aren't finishing poor little Annamaria's basket tonight. I'm sure working filigree by candlelight hurts your eyes. We'll make up for her disappointment tomorrow, and I hope she won't mind missing it."

This subtle hint was enough. Lucy instantly recollected herself. "Indeed, you are greatly mistaken, Lady Middleton," she replied briskly. "I am only waiting to see if you can manage without me, for I wouldn't disappoint the little angel for all the world. If you need me at the card table now, I am determined to finish the basket after supper."

"You are very kind," Lady Middleton said, hoping it wouldn't harm her eyes. "Will you ring for some working candles? My poor little girl would be terribly disappointed if the basket isn't finished tomorrow, especially since I promised her it certainly would be, even though I know she relies on it."

Lucy promptly moved her worktable closer and resumed her seat with an alacrity that suggested she derived no greater pleasure than crafting a basket for a spoiled child. Lady Middleton then proposed a rubber of Casino. Everyone objected except Marianne, who, ignoring the conventions of polite society, exclaimed, "Your Ladyship will please excuse me—I detest cards. I shall go to the piano-forte; I haven't touched it since it was tuned." Without further ceremony, she turned and walked to the instrument. Lady Middleton looked visibly relieved that she had never heard such a blunt remark.

"Marianne can never stay away from that instrument long, ma'am," Elinor interjected smoothly, attempting to ease the tension. "And I don't blame her; it is the finest-toned piano-forte I have ever heard."

With the remaining five preparing to deal, Elinor continued, "Perhaps, if I happen to cut out, I could assist Miss Lucy Steele in rolling her papers. There is still so much work left on the basket that it seems impossible for her to finish it alone this evening. I would be delighted to help if she allows me a share."

"Oh, I shall be so obliged!" cried Lucy. "I find there is more to do than I thought, and it would be dreadful to disappoint dear Annamaria."

"Oh! That would be terrible indeed," added Miss Steele. "My dear little soul, how I adore her!"

"You are very kind," Lady Middleton said to Elinor. "Since you enjoy the work, perhaps you'd prefer not to cut until the next hand, or would you like to try your luck now?"

Elinor gladly took the first option. Through a touch of diplomacy that Marianne could never muster, she achieved her goal and pleased Lady Middleton simultaneously. Lucy made room for her with ready attention, and the two rivals sat side by side at the same table, harmoniously collaborating on the basket. The piano-forte, where Marianne was now lost in her own music and thoughts, forgetting everyone else was present, stood conveniently close. Under the cover of its sound, Elinor decided she could safely introduce the

Elinor approached the conversation with a steady, albeit guarded, composure. "I would feel unworthy of the confidence you've placed in me," she began, "if I harbored no wish to maintain it, or no further curiosity regarding its subject. Therefore, I offer no apology for raising it once more."

"Thank you!" Lucy cried warmly, her relief palpable. "You have broken the ice; you have set my heart at ease. I confess I was terrified I had offended you back on Monday."

"Offend you?" Elinor replied with genuine warmth. "How could you possibly imagine that? Believe me, nothing was further from my intent than to suggest such a thing. Could you have any motive for trusting me that wasn't honorable and flattering?"

"And yet," Lucy countered, her sharp eyes gleaming with hidden meaning, "I felt a distinct coldness and displeasure in your manner that left me utterly uncomfortable. I was convinced you were angry, and I have been tormenting myself ever since over the liberty I took in troubling you with my affairs. But how glad I am to discover it was merely my own imagination, and that you truly hold no grudge. If only you knew how consoling it was to unload my heart onto someone who listens, or how much I think of you every moment of my life, your compassion would surely forgive everything else."

"I can easily believe it brought you great relief to speak freely," Elinor said gently. "Be assured you shall never regret it. Your situation is undeniably unfortunate; you appear surrounded by difficulties, and you will require all your mutual affection to sustain you. Regarding Mr. Ferrars, I believe he relies entirely on his mother's provision."

"He possesses only two thousand pounds of his own," Lucy explained. "It would be sheer madness to marry on such a sum, though personally, I could surrender every prospect of wealth without a sigh. I have always known a very small income and could endure any poverty for his sake. Yet, I love him too deeply to become the selfish instrument of robbing him of whatever his mother might bestow if he married solely to please her. We must wait—it may be for many years. For almost any other man, this would be an alarming prospect, but Edward's affection and constancy are treasures nothing can strip from me. I know that."

"That conviction must be everything to you," Elinor observed carefully, ensuring her face remained unreadable. "And he is undoubtedly sustained by the same faith in yours. Had the strength of your reciprocal attachment failed—as it often does between many couples under similar circumstances during a four-year engagement—your position would indeed be pitiable."

Lucy looked up expectantly, but Elinor held her gaze firmly. "Edward's love for me," Lucy declared, "has already undergone a severe test. Our engagement has endured a very long period of separation since we first promised, and it has passed this trial so successfully that I would be unpardonable to doubt it now. I can safely assert he has never given me a single moment of alarm."

Elinor struggled internally, unsure whether to smile or sigh at such a confident assertion. Lucy pressed on. "I am naturally somewhat jealous, and given our disparate social standings, his immersion in the world compared to my seclusion, and our constant separations, I was prone to suspicion. Had there been the slightest change in his behavior toward me upon our meetings, any inexplicable drop in spirits, or excessive talk of another lady, I would have discovered the truth instantly. I do not claim to be particularly observant in general matters, but in such a critical instance, I am certain I could not be deceived."

*All this,* Elinor thought, *is very pretty, but it cannot deceive either of us.*

After a brief silence, Elinor ventured, "But what are your plans? Or do you view nothing but waiting for Mrs. Ferrars' death—a melancholy and shocking extremity? Is her son resolved to submit to this, and to the tedious years of suspense it may entail, rather than risk her displeasure temporarily by revealing the truth?"

"If we could be certain it would be only temporary! But Mrs. Ferrars is a headstrong, proud woman. Her initial fury upon hearing the truth would likely see her secure everything for Robert. The mere thought of that, for Edward's sake, deters me from taking any hasty measures."

"And for your own sake as well, or you are pushing your disinterest beyond reason." Lucy glanced at Elinor, then fell silent.

"Do you know Mr. Robert Ferrars?" Elinor asked.

"Not at all; I have never seen him. However, I fancy he is very unlike his brother—silly and a great coxcomb."

"A great coxcomb!" repeated Miss Steele, having caught the phrase amidst a sudden pause in Marianne's piano playing. "Oh, they are discussing their favorite beaux, I suppose."

"No, sister," Lucy corrected sharply. "You are mistaken; our favorite beaux are *not* great coxcombs."

"I can vouch for it that Miss Dashwood's beau is not," Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily. "He is one of the most modest and well-behaved young men I have ever encountered. As for Lucy, she is such a sly little creature; there is no telling whom she actually likes."

"Oh," cried Miss Steele, casting significant glances around the room, "I dare say Lucy's beau is quite as modest and well-behaved as Miss Dashwood's."

Elinor blushed involuntarily. Lucy bit her lip and shot an angry look at her sister. A heavy silence ensued until Lucy broke it in a lowered tone, despite Marianne providing a musical shield with a magnificent concerto.

"I will honestly disclose one scheme recently conceived to bring matters to a resolution. Indeed, I am bound to share this secret, for you are a party concerned. You have likely seen enough of Edward to know he prefers the clergy to any other profession. My plan is for him to take holy orders as soon as possible. Then, through your influence—which I am sure you would kindly exert out of friendship for him, and hopefully out of some regard for me—your brother might be persuaded to grant him the Norland living. I understand it is a very good rectory, and the current incumbent is unlikely to survive much longer. This would provide sufficient income for us to marry upon, leaving us to trust in time and chance for the remainder."

"I should always be happy," Elinor replied, "to demonstrate any mark of esteem and friendship for Mr. Ferrars. But do you not perceive that my interest in such a matter would be entirely superfluous? He is the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood—that connection alone must suffice for his father-in-law."

"But Mrs. John Dashwood would scarcely approve of Edward entering the clergy."

"Then I rather suspect my influence would achieve very little."

They lapsed into silence again for many minutes. Finally, Lucy sighed deeply. "I believe the wisest course is to dissolve the engagement outright. We seem besieged by difficulties on every side. Though it would render us miserable for a time, we might ultimately be happier. But you will not give me your advice, Miss Dashwood?"

"No," Elinor answered with a smile that masked her agitated feelings. "On such a subject, I certainly will not. You know very well that my opinion would carry no weight with you unless it aligned with your own desires."

"You wrong me greatly," Lucy responded with grave solemnity. "There is no one whose judgment I hold in higher esteem than yours. I truly believe that if you were to say, 'I advise you by all means to end your engagement with Edward Ferrars; it will be more beneficial for the happiness of both of you,' I would resolve to act immediately."

Elinor blushed at the insincerity of the future wife-to-be and replied, "Such a compliment would effectively deter me from offering any opinion had I formed one. It elevates my influence far too high; the power to divide two people so tenderly attached is too great a burden for an indifferent observer."

"'Tis precisely because you are an indifferent person," Lucy retorted with some pique, emphasizing the word heavily, "that your judgment might justly carry such weight with me. Were you suspected of bias by your own feelings, your opinion would be worthless."

Elinor deemed it wiser to remain silent, fearing they might provoke each other into an unsuitable display of familiarity and candor. She was even partially resolved never to revisit the topic. Consequently, a prolonged silence followed, and Lucy was once again the first to break it.

"Will you be in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?" she asked with her customary air of complacency.

"Certainly not."

"I am sorry to hear that," the other replied, her eyes brightening at the news. "It would have given me such pleasure to meet you there! But I suppose you will go regardless. After all, your brother and sister will invite you."

"It will not be within my power to accept their invitation if they do."

"How unfortunate!" Lucy exclaimed. "I had fully relied on meeting you there. Anne and I are traveling to visit some relations late in January—they have been urging us to pay them a visit for years! But I go solely for the sake of seeing Edward. He will be there in February; otherwise, London holds no charm for me. I lack the spirit for it."

Soon, Elinor was summoned to the card table by the conclusion of the first game, ending the confidential discourse between the two ladies. Both submitted to the interruption without

Although Mrs. Jennings spent much of the year visiting her children and friends, she maintained a permanent residence of her own. Following the death of her late husband—a successful merchant who operated in a less fashionable district of the city—she had taken up lodging every winter in a house situated near Portman Square. As January approached, she began to turn her thoughts toward returning to this home, and one day, she abruptly and surprisingly invited the elder Miss Dashwoods to join her.

Elinor, failing to notice the shifting expression on her sister's face or the animated look that betrayed her lack of indifference to the proposal, immediately offered a grateful yet firm refusal. She believed she was voicing their shared sentiment. "We have made a determined resolution not to leave our mother at this time of year," she stated.

Mrs. Jennings received the rejection with surprise and immediately renewed her invitation. "Oh, Lord! I am certain your mother can spare you quite well, and I *beg* you to favor me with your company. My heart is truly set on it. Do not imagine you will be any inconvenience; I shall not put myself out of my way for you. It will simply involve sending Betty by the coach, and I trust I can afford that. The three of us shall travel comfortably in my chaise. Furthermore, once we are in town, if you prefer not to follow where I go, you are welcome to stay with one of my daughters. I am sure your mother will not object; having successfully managed to send my own children away, I believe I am a fit person to take charge of you. If I fail to secure a marriage for either of you before I am done with you, it shall not be my fault. I shall speak highly of you to all the eligible young men; you may depend on it."

"I suspect," interjected Sir John, "that Miss Marianne would not object to such a scheme if her elder sister would join her. It is terribly unfair that she should be denied even a little pleasure simply because Miss Dashwood refuses. I would advise you both to depart for town as soon as you grow weary of Barton, without telling Miss Dashwood anything about it."

"Nay!" cried Mrs. Jennings. "I am absolutely delighted to have Miss Marianne's company, regardless of whether Miss Dashwood accompanies her. The more the merrier, as they say. I thought it would be more comforting for them to be together; if they tire of me, they can converse and laugh at my peculiarities behind my back. However, I must have one or the other, if not both. Good heavens, how can I endure poking around alone, accustomed as I have been until this winter to have Charlotte with me? Come, Miss Marianne, let us seal the bargain with a handshake. Should Miss Dashwood change her mind later, so much the better."

"I thank you, madam, sincerely," said Marianne warmly. "Your invitation secures my gratitude forever, and it would bring me such happiness—almost the greatest happiness I am capable of feeling—to accept it. Yet, my mother, my dearest, kindest mother... I recognize the validity of what Elinor has argued. If our absence were to make her less happy or less comfortable—Oh! No, nothing should tempt me to leave her. It must not, and it cannot be a struggle."

Mrs. Jennings reiterated her assurance that Mrs. Dashwood could spare them perfectly well. Elinor, now understanding her sister and seeing how her eagerness to reunite with Willoughby had rendered her indifferent to nearly everything else, ceased her direct opposition. Instead, she deferred the matter entirely to her mother's decision. From her mother, however, she scarcely expected support in preventing a visit she disapproved of for Marianne and one she personally wished to avoid due to specific reasons. Whatever Marianne desired, her mother was eager to facilitate; Elinor could not expect to sway her toward caution regarding an affair in which she had never instilled distrust. Moreover, she dared not reveal the motives behind her own reluctance to travel to London.

It struck Elinor as particularly striking that Marianne, despite being fastidious and thoroughly aware of Mrs. Jennings' manners, chose to overlook every potential discomfort and disregard anything likely to wound her sensitive feelings in pursuit of a single objective. This demonstrated the profound importance of that goal to Marianne, a fact Elinor found difficult to reconcile with everything that had transpired between them.

Upon learning of the invitation, Mrs. Dashwood, convinced that the trip would provide immense amusement for both her daughters and perceiving through her affectionate attentions just how deeply Marianne's heart was involved, refused to hear of their declining the offer on her account. She insisted they accept it immediately and, with her characteristic cheerfulness, began anticipating various advantages for everyone resulting from this separation.

"I am delighted with the plan!" she exclaimed. "It is precisely what I could wish. Margaret and I shall benefit as much as you. Once you and the Middletons depart, we shall settle down quietly and happily with our books and music! You will find Margaret greatly improved upon your return. I also have a small plan to alter your bedrooms, which can now be executed without inconveniencing anyone. It is very right that you should visit London; I believe every young woman of your station ought to be acquainted with its manners and amusements. You will be under the care of a motherly, kind woman, and I have no doubt of her goodness. In all likelihood, you will see your brother. Regardless of his faults—or those of his wife, considering who his father was—I cannot bear to have you so completely estranged from one another."

"Though, with your usual concern for our happiness," Elinor remarked, "you have removed every obstacle you could conceive, there remains one objection which, in my opinion, cannot be so easily dismissed." Marianne's countenance fell.

"And what," asked Mrs. Dashwood, "does my dear, prudent Elinor intend to suggest? What formidable obstacle is she bringing forth now? Pray, do not mention the expense."

"My objection is this: while I hold Mrs. Jennings' heart in high regard, her society offers us neither pleasure nor the social standing we require."

"That is very true," replied her mother, "but apart from accompanying others, you will have little interaction with her socially anyway, and you will almost invariably appear in public with Lady Middleton."

"If Elinor is deterred by her dislike of Mrs. Jennings," said Marianne, "it need not prevent *my* acceptance. I have no such scruples, and I am confident I could endure every unpleasantness associated with it with minimal effort."

Elinor could not suppress a smile at this display of indifference toward the manners of someone she had often struggled to persuade Marianne to treat with basic politeness. Resolving internally that if her sister persisted, she would go as well—deeming it improper for Marianne to rely solely on her own judgment or for Mrs. Jennings to be left to Marianne's mercy during their domestic hours—she felt more easily reconciled to this decision. She recalled that Edward Ferrars, according to Lucy's report, would not arrive in town until February, meaning their visit could conclude reasonably early.

"I will have you *both* go," declared Mrs. Dashwood. "These objections are nonsense. You will derive great pleasure from being in London, especially from being together. If Elinor would ever condescend to anticipate enjoyment, she would foresee it arising from various sources; perhaps she expects some from improving her acquaintance with her sister-in-law's family."

Elinor had long wished for an opportunity to gently undermine her mother's reliance on the attachments of Edward and herself, hoping the eventual revelation of the whole truth would be less shocking. Seizing this moment, though she knew success was unlikely, she forced herself to begin her design as calmly as possible. "I like Edward Ferrars very much and shall always be glad to see him," she said. "But regarding the rest of the family, it is a matter of perfect indifference to me whether I am ever known to them or not."

Mrs. Dashwood smiled and said nothing. Marianne looked up in astonishment, and Elinor realized she might as well have remained silent.

After very little further discussion, it was finally settled that the invitation would be fully accepted. Mrs. Jennings received the news with boundless joy, offering countless assurances of kindness and care; it was a source of pleasure not only for her. Sir John was equally delighted; for a man whose chief anxiety was the fear of solitude, acquiring two additional companions in London was a significant gain. Even Lady Middleton made the effort to feign delight, stretching herself somewhat; as for the Miss Steeles, particularly Lucy, they had never been happier in their lives than this intelligence made them.

Elinor submitted to the arrangement contrary to her wishes with less reluctance than anticipated. Regarding herself, it became a matter of indifference whether she traveled to town or not. Seeing her mother so thoroughly pleased and her sister so exhilarated—restored to her usual animation and elevated to a gaiety surpassing her normal state—Elinor could not be dissatisfied with the cause and hardly allowed herself to doubt the outcome. Marianne's joy bordered on ecstasy; her spirits were so agitated and her eagerness to depart so intense that her only anchor to calmness was her reluctance to leave her mother. At the moment of parting, her grief over this loss was excessive. Her mother's sorrow was hardly less, and Elinor was the only one of the three who viewed the separation as anything short of eternal.

Their departure took place in the first week of January. The Middletons were scheduled to follow in about a week, while the

Elinor found herself seated beside Mrs. Jennings in a carriage, embarking on a journey to London as her guest, yet she could not suppress a profound sense of wonderment at her own position. Their acquaintance had been so brief, their ages and temperaments so disparate, and her objections to such an arrangement so numerous just days prior! Yet, swept away by that youthful fervor shared by Marianne and her mother alike, those objections had been effortlessly dismissed. Despite lingering doubts regarding Willoughby's fidelity, Elinor watched Marianne's face—a vessel brimming with ecstatic anticipation—and felt a stark contrast in her own spirit. While Marianne radiated hope, Elinor's prospects seemed bleak and cheerless. In comparing their states, she realized how desperately she would trade her own emptiness for the animating hope that filled her sister's heart.

However, fate was poised to reveal Willoughby's true intentions within a mere handful of days; he was likely already in town. Marianne's impatience to depart confirmed her belief that he awaited her there. Consequently, Elinor resolved to pursue every available clue regarding his character through her own observation and the reports of others. She intended to scrutinize his behavior toward her sister with intense vigilance, aiming to discern his true nature and motives before many meetings occurred. If her findings proved unfavorable, she vowed to enlighten her sister immediately. Conversely, if the outlook remained promising, her efforts would shift: she must learn to suppress self-comparison and banish any regret that might dim her joy in Marianne's potential happiness.

The journey lasted three days. Marianne's demeanor during travel offered a glimpse of her future companionship with Mrs. Jennings: she sat in near-silence, lost in reverie, speaking only when a picturesque sight prompted an exclamatory address solely to her sister. To compensate, Elinor assumed the role of civility she had assigned herself. She attended to Mrs. Jennings with utmost care, engaging her in conversation, sharing laughter, and listening patiently whenever possible. In turn, Mrs. Jennings treated them with boundless kindness, fussing over their comfort and expressing only mild frustration that she could not dictate their meals or extract preferences between salmon and cod, or fowl and veal.

They arrived in London at three o'clock on the third day, eager to escape the carriage's confinement and bask in the warmth of a good fire. Their lodging was handsome and comfortably appointed; the young ladies were settled in a spacious apartment that had previously belonged to Charlotte. Above the mantelpiece hung a landscape painted in colored silks, a testament to Charlotte's seven years spent at a prestigious school in the city. With dinner delayed by two hours, Elinor decided to write to her mother and took her seat. Moments later, Marianne joined her.

"I am writing home, Marianne," Elinor remarked. "Would you not prefer to wait a day or two?"

"I am *not* going to write to my mother," Marianne replied sharply, clearly wishing to end the topic.

Elinor said nothing further, but the implication struck her instantly: Marianne must be writing to Willoughby. The conclusion that followed was unavoidable—they were engaged. Though this realization lacked total certainty, it brought Elinor a surge of pleasure, and she resumed her letter with renewed vigor. Marianne finished hers in moments; it was merely a note, folded, sealed, and directed with frantic speed. Elinor believed she could discern a large 'W' in the address. Before the task was complete, Marianne rang the bell and instructed the footman to send the letter via the two-penny post. The mystery was solved.

Marianne's spirits soared, yet a nervous flutter kept her expression from bringing true joy to her sister. This agitation intensified as evening approached. She barely touched her dinner and, upon returning to the drawing-room, seemed to listen intently to the clatter of every approaching carriage. Elinor was relieved that Mrs. Jennings, absorbed in her own room, witnessed little of the turmoil. When tea was served, Marianne had already been thwarted several times by knocks at neighboring doors. Then, a sharp, unmistakable rap echoed through the air. Elinor felt certain it signaled Willoughby's arrival. Marianne sprang up and hastened toward the door.

Silence reigned. Unable to endure it, she opened the door, stepped onto the landing, and listened for half a minute before retreating into the room, trembling with the conviction that she had heard him. Overcome by ecstasy, she cried out, "Oh, Elinor, it is Willoughby, indeed it is!" appearing ready to rush into his embrace, until Colonel Brandon appeared.

The blow was too severe to withstand calmly, and Marianne fled the room. Elinor felt a pang of disappointment, yet her respect for Colonel Brandon ensured his welcome. She was deeply wounded that a man so fond of her sister perceived only her grief and despair. Instantly recognizing that he noticed her distress as she left, observing her with such astonishment and concern that he barely remembered the courtesy due her, Elinor felt a fresh ache.

"Is your sister ill?" he asked.

Answering with evident distress, Elinor spoke of headaches, low spirits, and exhaustion, attributing Marianne's behavior to these ailments. He listened with earnest attention but, seemingly recalling himself, changed the subject to his pleasure at seeing them in London, asking standard questions about their journey and old acquaintances. Both women, lacking spirits and distracted by their respective thoughts, conversed in a calm, perfunctory manner.

Elinor longed to ask if Willoughby was in town but feared causing pain by inquiring about her rival. Finally, seeking conversation, she asked if he had been in London since their last meeting.

"Yes," he replied, with noticeable embarrassment, "almost ever since. I visited Delaford once or twice for a few days, but I have never been able to return to Barton."

These words, delivered with that specific tone, instantly resurrected the memory of his departure from that estate and the unease it had caused Mrs. Jennings. Elinor feared her question revealed far more curiosity than she possessed.

Soon, Mrs. Jennings entered. "Oh! Colonel," she exclaimed with her characteristic boisterous cheerfulness, "I am monstrously glad to see you—so sorry I couldn't come sooner! Pray forgive me, but I've been forced to tidy up and settle my affairs; it's been ages since I was home, and one always has a world of odd tasks after an absence. Besides, I've had Cartwright to deal with. Lord, I've been as busy as a bee since dinner! But pray, Colonel, how did you guess I'd be in town today?"

"I learned of it at Mr. Palmer's, where I dined."

"Oh, you did! And how are they all? How does Charlotte fare? I wager she's grown quite large by now."

"Mrs. Palmer looked perfectly well, and I'm sent to inform you that you shall certainly see her tomorrow."

"Ay, to be sure, I thought as much. Well, Colonel, I've brought two young ladies with me—you see, well, only one of them is here now, but there's another somewhere. Your friend, Miss Marianne, too—which you won't be sorry to hear. I don't know what you and Mr. Willoughby will manage between you regarding her. Ay, it's a fine thing to be young and handsome. Well! I was young once, but never very handsome—bad luck for me. However, I secured a very good husband, and I don't know what the fairest woman could achieve more. Ah! Poor man! He's been dead these eight years, and better. But Colonel, where have you been since we parted? And how fares your business? Come, come, let's share no secrets among friends."

He responded to her inquiries with his customary mildness, offering no clear answers. Elinor then prepared the tea, forcing Marianne to reappear. Once inside, Colonel Brandon grew increasingly thoughtful and silent, and Mrs. Jennings failed to persuade him to stay long. No other visitors arrived that evening, and the ladies unanimously agreed to retire early.

The following morning, Marianne rose with restored spirits and a radiant countenance. The previous night's disappointment vanished, replaced by the anticipation of the day's events. Hardly had breakfast concluded when Mrs. Palmer's carriage pulled up, and shortly thereafter, she burst into the room laughing. She was so delighted to see everyone that it was difficult to tell if she derived more pleasure from reuniting with her mother or the Dashwood sisters. Surprised by their visit to town, though she had suspected it, she feigned anger at their acceptance of her mother's invitation after rejecting her own, yet admitted she would never have forgiven them had they not come.

"Mr. Palmer will be so happy to see you," she said. "What do you suppose he said when he heard you were coming with Mama? I forget exactly, but it was something quite droll!"

After an hour or two of what her mother termed "comfortable chat"—essentially endless inquiries about mutual acquaintances mixed with baseless laughter from Mrs. Palmer—it was suggested that they all accompany her to some shops where she had business. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor readily agreed, needing purchases themselves, and Marianne, initially reluctant, was persuaded to join them.

Throughout their outing, Marianne remained constantly vigilant. Especially in Bond Street, her eyes scanned the crowd incessantly. In every shop, her mind drifted far from the items displayed, disengaging herself from everything

"If this fine weather continues much longer," remarked Mrs. Jennings as they gathered for breakfast the following morning, "Sir John won't be pleased with the prospect of leaving Barton next week. It's truly pitiful for men of sport to lose a single day of pleasure. Poor fellows! I always feel sorry for them when they do; they seem to carry it so deeply in their hearts."

"That is quite true," exclaimed Marianne, her voice bright and cheerful. As she spoke, she moved toward the window to inspect the day outside. "I hadn't considered *that* aspect. This weather will surely keep many sportsmen right here in the countryside." It was a fortunate realization; her spirits, previously dampened, were instantly restored.

"It is simply ideal for them," she continued, settling into her chair at the breakfast table with a radiant expression. "One can only imagine how delightful it must be for them! However" (a flicker of anxiety returning to her tone), "one shouldn't expect it to endure forever. Given the season and the prolonged rainfall we've endured, we shall undoubtedly see very little more of it. Frosts will inevitably arrive soon, likely with considerable severity. Perhaps within a day or two; this extreme mildness can hardly persist—nay, perhaps tonight itself will bring freezing temperatures!"

"At any rate," interjected Elinor, eager to divert her mother's gaze from her sister's evident distress, "I daresay Sir John and Lady Middleton will be back in town by the end of next week."

"Ay, my dear, I'll wager you're right," Mrs. Jennings agreed. "Mary always gets her own way."

"And now," Elinor observed silently to herself, "she will dispatch a letter to Combe via today's post." Yet, if she did, the letter was composed and dispatched with such discretion that it evaded all of Elinor's attempts to verify the fact. Regardless of the truth, and despite Elinor's lack of total contentment regarding the matter, she felt no great discomfort so long as she witnessed Marianne's buoyant mood. Marianne was indeed spirited, delighted by the gentle warmth of the air and even more thrilled by the anticipation of an impending frost.

The morning was largely devoted to calling on acquaintances of Mrs. Jennings to announce their presence in London. Throughout these visits, Marianne remained engrossed in monitoring the wind's direction, scrutinizing shifts in the sky, and envisioning atmospheric changes.

"Do you not find it cooler than this morning, Elinor?" she asked. "There appears to be a distinct difference. I struggle to keep my hands warm even inside my muff. It wasn't so yesterday, I believe. The clouds seem to be breaking apart; the sun will emerge momentarily, and we shall have a clear afternoon."

Elinor felt alternating waves of amusement and pain, yet Marianne persisted. Whether gazing into the bright glow of the fireplace at night or observing the morning atmosphere, she interpreted every sign as a sure indication of coming frost.

The Miss Dashwoods possessed no greater cause for dissatisfaction with Mrs. Jennings' lifestyle or circle of friends than with her treatment of them, which was consistently kind. Everything in her household management followed the most generous principles. Aside from a handful of elderly city friends whom, to Lady Middleton's chagrin, she had never managed to drop, she visited no one whose acquaintance might disturb the sensibilities of her younger companions. Delighted to find herself more comfortably situated in this regard than anticipated, Elinor was quite willing to trade off the lack of genuine enjoyment found in their evening gatherings. These parties, held at home or elsewhere, consisted primarily of card games and offered little amusement for her.

Colonel Brandon, holding a general invitation to the house, visited almost daily. He came ostensibly to admire Marianne and converse with Elinor. While Elinor often derived more satisfaction from talking with him than from any other daily occurrence, she simultaneously harbored deep concern regarding his enduring attachment to her sister. She feared it was a growing sentiment. It grieved her to witness the earnestness with which he often watched Marianne, and his spirits were noticeably lower than they had been at Barton.

About a week after their arrival in London, it became confirmed that Willoughby had also arrived. His visiting card lay on the table when they returned from their morning drive.

"Good heavens!" cried Marianne. "He has been here while we were out!"

Elinor, relieved to confirm his presence in London, ventured to suggest, "Be assured, he will call again tomorrow." But Marianne seemed barely to register her words. Upon Mrs. Jennings' entry, Marianne snatched up the precious card and fled the room.

This event, while lifting Elinor's spirits, ignited in her sister a renewed and intensified agitation. From that moment onward, her mind found no peace; the hourly expectation of seeing him rendered her incapable of focusing on anything else. She insisted on staying behind the next morning while the others went out driving. Elinor's thoughts filled with speculation about what might be transpiring in Berkeley Street during their absence, but a fleeting glance at her sister upon their return sufficed to reveal that Willoughby had paid no second visit.

Just then, a note was delivered and placed on the table.

"For me!" cried Marianne, hastening forward.

"No, madam, for my mistress," the servant replied.

But unconvinced, Marianne seized the note immediately. "It is indeed for Mrs. Jennings! How vexatious!"

"You are expecting a letter, then?" Elinor asked, unable to remain silent any longer.

"Yes, a little—not much." After a brief pause, she added, "You have no confidence in me, Marianne."

"Nay, Elinor, this reproach from *you*—from one who claims to trust no one!"

"Me!" Elinor responded, somewhat flustered. "Indeed, Marianne, I have nothing to disclose."

"Nor I," Marianne answered with vigor. "Then our situations are identical. Neither of us has anything to tell; you, because you choose not to communicate, and I, because I conceal nothing."

Distressed by this accusation of reserve against herself—a trait she felt powerless to deny under the circumstances—Elinor did not know how to urge Marianne toward greater openness.

Mrs. Jennings soon appeared, and upon receiving the note, read it aloud. It was from Lady Middleton, informing them of her arrival in Conduit Street the previous night and inviting her mother and cousins to join her the following evening. Business obligations on Sir John's part and a severe cold afflicting Lady Middleton had prevented them from calling at Berkeley Street. The invitation was accepted.

However, as the appointed hour approached, Elinor encountered significant difficulty persuading her sister to accompany her. Though civil attendance was necessary, Marianne, having seen nothing of Willoughby, felt neither inclined for social diversion nor willing to risk his appearing again in her absence.

When the evening concluded, Elinor realized that changing one's residence does not necessarily alter one's nature. Although scarcely established in London, Sir John had managed to gather nearly twenty young people around him and entertain them with a ball. This was an affair, however, that Lady Middleton disapproved of. In the country, an unplanned dance was perfectly acceptable; but in London, where the reputation for elegance was paramount and difficult to maintain, it was a dangerous gamble to allow it to become known that Lady Middleton had hosted a modest dance of eight or nine couples, accompanied by only two violins and a scant refreshment spread.

Mr. and Mrs. Palmer attended. Having not seen them since their arrival in town, and mindful of avoiding any appearance of undue attention to his mother-in-law, Mr. Palmer carefully kept his distance. Upon their entrance, he cast a slight, seemingly unaware glance at them, offering only a nod to Mrs. Jennings from across the room.

Marianne glanced around the room as she entered; it was sufficient—*he* was not there. Consequently, she sat down, equally disinclined to receive or share any pleasure.

After an hour of assembly, Mr. Palmer strolled over to the Miss Dashwoods to express his surprise at seeing them in town, noting that Colonel Brandon had been the first informed of their arrival and had made a witty remark upon hearing the news.

"I thought you were both still in Devonshire," he said.

"Did you?" replied Elinor.

"When do you intend to return?"

"I do not know." Thus ended their exchange.

Never in her life had Marianne been so reluctant to dance, nor so exhausted by the exertion. She complained bitterly as they returned to Berkeley Street.

"Aye, aye," said Mrs. Jennings. "We understand the reason for all that perfectly well. If a certain individual, whom I shall not name, had been present, you would not have felt a fraction of that fatigue. To tell the truth, it was not very becoming of him to fail to meet you when he was invited."

"Invited!" cried Marianne.

"So my daughter Middleton informed me, for it seems Sir John encountered him somewhere in the streets this morning."

Marianne said nothing further but looked profoundly hurt.

Impatient to do something that might alleviate her sister's suffering, Elinor resolved to write the following morning to her mother. Hoping to awaken maternal fears for Marianne's health and thereby secure the long-delayed inquiries, she was driven even more urgently to this course by observing, after breakfast the next morning, that Marianne was once again writing to Willoughby. She could not possibly suppose it was addressed to anyone else.

Around

For the next three or four days, nothing happened to make Elinor regret her decision to seek advice from her mother; Willoughby neither visited nor sent a letter. By the end of this period, they were scheduled to accompany Lady Middleton to a gathering, an event from which Mrs. Jennings was excused due to the illness of her youngest child. For this occasion, Marianne appeared utterly disheartened, neglectful of her attire, and seemingly indifferent to whether she attended or stayed behind. She sat by the drawing-room fire after tea until Lady Middleton's arrival, remaining motionless in her seat, lost in thought and oblivious to her sister's presence. Only when they were summoned to the door did she start as if she had momentarily forgotten anyone was expected.

Upon arriving at the venue, they alighted as soon as the line of carriages allowed, climbed the stairs, and heard their names called aloud from one landing to the next before entering a room brilliantly illuminated, packed with guests, and stiflingly warm. After offering the customary courtesy to the hostess, they mingled with the crowd, inevitably adding to the discomfort of the heat. After spending some time exchanging minimal pleasantries, Lady Middleton seated herself at the game table. Since Marianne lacked the energy to move about, Elinor and Marianne fortunately secured seats near the table.

It was not long before Elinor spotted Willoughby, standing just a few yards away, engaged in earnest conversation with a highly fashionable young woman. He quickly caught her gaze and bowed, yet made no attempt to speak to her or approach Marianne, despite clearly seeing her, continuing his discourse with the same lady. Elinor instinctively glanced at Marianne to gauge her reaction. It was at that precise moment that Marianne first noticed him; her entire face flushed with sudden joy, and she would have rushed toward him instantly had her sister not seized her arm.

"Good heavens!" she cried. "He is here—he is there! Oh, why doesn't he look at me? Why can't I speak to him?"

"Please, please compose yourself," Elinor urged. "Do not reveal your feelings to everyone present. Perhaps he hasn't seen you yet."

However, this reassurance offered no comfort to Marianne, who found it impossible to remain calm or even desire to do so. She sat trembling with an agony of impatience that distorted every feature. Finally, Willoughby turned around and looked at them both. Marianne sprang up, uttering his name with affectionate emphasis, and extended her hand. He approached, directing his words primarily to Elinor as if wishing to avoid her gaze, deliberately ignoring her gesture, and inquired hurriedly about Mrs. Dashwood and how long they had been in London.

Elinor was so stunned by this greeting that she lost all presence of mind and could not utter a word. Yet, her sister's emotions were immediately evident. Her face turned crimson, and in a voice thick with emotion, she exclaimed, "Good God! Willoughby, what is the meaning of this? Have you not received my letters? Will you not shake hands with me?"

He could no longer avoid it, but her touch seemed painful to him, and he held her hand for only a fleeting moment. Throughout this exchange, he was visibly struggling to maintain his composure. Elinor watched his face and noted its expression growing increasingly tranquil. After a brief pause, he spoke calmly.

"I did myself the honor of calling in Berkeley Street last Tuesday and deeply regretted not finding you and Mrs. Jennings at home. I trust my card was not lost."

"But have you not received my notes?" Marianne shrieked in wild anxiety. "There must be some mistake—some dreadful mistake. What could possibly explain this? Tell me, Willoughby; for heaven's sake, tell me what is wrong?"

He offered no reply. His complexion darkened, and all his earlier embarrassment returned. However, upon catching the eye of the young lady with whom he had been conversing, he felt compelled to exert himself instantly. Recovering his poise, he said, "Yes, I had the pleasure of receiving notice of your arrival in town, which you were so kind as to send me," gave a slight, hasty bow, and turned away to join his friend.

Marianne, now looking ghastly pale and unable to stand, sank back into her chair. Elinor, fearing she might faint, attempted to shield her from the view of others while reviving her with lavender water.

"Go to him, Elinor," Marianne cried as soon as she could speak. "Force him to come to me. Tell him I must see him again—I must speak to him instantly. I cannot rest; I shall know no peace until this is explained. Some terrible misunderstanding must exist. Oh, go to him right now."

"How can that be done? No, my dearest Marianne, you must wait. This is not the place for explanations. Wait only until tomorrow."

With great difficulty, Elinor managed to prevent her from pursuing him herself. Persuading Marianne to curb her agitation and appear composed until she could speak privately and effectively was impossible; Marianne continued to break down in whispers, lamenting her misery. Shortly thereafter, Elinor saw Willoughby leave the room via the staircase. Telling Marianne he was gone, she argued against the possibility of further contact that evening, urging her to remain calm. Instantly, Marianne begged Elinor to ask Lady Middleton to take them home, claiming she was too wretched to stay another minute. Though Lady Middleton was mid-game, she was too polite to object. Exchanging cards with a friend, the sisters departed as soon as a carriage could be found.

Scarcely a word was exchanged on the journey back to Berkeley Street. Marianne was in a silent agony, too overwhelmed even to weep. Fortunately, Mrs. Jennings had not yet returned, allowing them to proceed straight to their rooms, where smelling salts eventually brought Marianne back to herself. Soon undressed and in bed, and apparently desiring solitude, her sister left her. While waiting for Mrs. Jennings' return, Elinor had ample opportunity to reflect on the day's events.

She could not doubt that a prior engagement existed between Willoughby and Marianne, nor that Willoughby was now weary of it.
