Regardless of how Marianne might still nourish her own hopes, Elinor could not attribute such behavior to error or misunderstanding. Nothing short of a complete change of heart could account for it. Her indignation would have been far greater had she not witnessed the embarrassment that suggested a consciousness of his own misconduct, preventing her from believing him so unprincipled as to have toyed with her sister's affections from the start without cause. Absence might have dulled his regard, and convenience might have driven him to suppress it, but she could never convince herself that such a regard had never existed.

As for Marianne, reflecting on the pain such an unfortunate meeting had already caused, and the even severer suffering likely to follow, filled Elinor with profound sorrow. In comparison, her own situation seemed preferable; while she might love Edward as deeply as ever, whatever their future separation, her mind could find support. Conversely, every circumstance seemed to conspire to intensify Marianne's misery through a final, irrevocable separation from Willoughby—an immediate and unyielding rupture.

Before the housemaid had kindled the fire for the following morning, or until the pale sun of a bleak January day had managed to pierce the heavy gloom outside, Marianne knelt beside a window seat, half-dressed and trembling. She pressed her cheek against the cold glass, striving to capture even a sliver of light to illuminate her frantic handwriting. Tears flowed continuously, blurring her vision and slowing her pen, yet she wrote with a desperate urgency.

Elinor, awakened from sleep by the sharp, ragged sounds of her sister's sobbing, crept quietly toward the bedside. Watching Marianne for a moment with a heart full of silent dread, she spoke in a voice of gentle consideration, "Marianne, may I ask—"

"No, Elinor," Marianne interrupted, her voice strikingly steady despite the storm within. "Ask nothing; you will soon know everything."

That brief moment of composure vanished instantly, replaced by a fresh wave of overwhelming grief. Minutes passed before she could resume her letter, her hand frequently pausing as fresh bursts of sorrow forced her to lift it from the page. Each interruption confirmed what she feared most: that this correspondence might be the final one she would ever write to Willoughby.

Elinor offered her every ounce of quiet support, yet Marianne, driven by a nervous irritability, pleaded with all her might that Elinor say nothing to her at all. Under such circumstances, prolonged proximity was painful for both sisters. Marianne's agitated mind refused stillness; needing constant movement and isolation, she wandered through the house until breakfast, deliberately avoiding the gaze of everyone she encountered.

At the table, she ate nothing. Elinor, meanwhile, focused her entire attention on diverting Mrs. Jennings's curiosity, ensuring her sister remained unnoticed. The meal dragged on, a favorite pastime for the elderly hostess, until finally, a letter was delivered to Marianne. She snatched it eagerly from the servant's hand, her complexion draining to a deathly pallor, and fled the room.

Elinor recognized the sender immediately. Seeing Marianne's reaction, she felt a sickening lurch in her own chest, her limbs trembling so violently she feared Mrs. Jennings would notice her distress. However, the kindly old lady merely assumed Marianne had received a playful missive from Willoughby. Laughing cheerfully, she remarked on how delightfully absurd it was, asking when the couple might finally be wed.

Elinor, though reluctant to speak, felt compelled to correct this dangerous misunderstanding. Trying to force a smile, she replied, "Have you truly, Ma'am, convinced yourself that my sister is engaged to Mr. Willoughby? I thought it mere jest, but such a serious inquiry implies more. Please, do not deceive yourself further. Nothing would surprise me more than to hear they intend to marry."

"For shame!" Mrs. Jennings exclaimed, laughing again. "How can you speak so? Do we not all know it must be a match? They were head-over-heels in love from the very first meeting! Did I not see them together in Devonshire every single day? And did I not know your sister came to London specifically to purchase wedding attire? Come, come, this secrecy is unnecessary. Because you are so coy, you assume others lack sense, but I assure you, the news has circulated throughout the town for ages. I tell everyone, and so does Charlotte."

"Indeed, Ma'am," Elinor said gravely, "you are mistaken. You are doing a grave injustice by spreading this rumor, and I fear you will find yourself unable to believe me now."

Mrs. Jennings chuckled, dismissing Elinor's protest, while Elinor, drained of spirit, hurried upstairs to their chamber. Upon entering, she found Marianne collapsed upon the bed, nearly choking on her sobs, clutching one letter while two or three lay scattered nearby.

Elinor approached silently, sitting beside her sister. She took Marianne's hand, kissing it repeatedly, until her own restraint broke and she joined her in a flood of tears. After sharing this initial outpouring of shared grief, Marianne handed all the letters to Elinor. Covering her face with a handkerchief, she screamed with agony.

Knowing that such profound suffering required time to pass, Elinor waited until the intensity subsided slightly before turning to the letter from Willoughby. It read:

*I have just had the honor of receiving your letter, for which I beg to return my sincere acknowledgments. I am much concerned to find there was anything in my behavior last night that did not meet your approbation; and though I am quite at a loss to discover in what point I could be so unfortunate as to offend you, I entreat your forgiveness of what I can assure you to have been perfectly unintentional.*

*I shall never reflect on my former acquaintance with your family in Devonshire without the most grateful pleasure, and flatter myself it will not be broken by any mistake or misapprehension of my actions. My esteem for your whole family is very sincere; but if I have been so unfortunate as to give rise to a belief of more than I felt, or meant to express, I shall reproach myself for not having been more guarded in my professions of that esteem. That I should ever have meant more you will allow to be impossible, when you understand that my affections have been long engaged elsewhere, and it will not be many weeks, I believe, before this engagement is fulfilled.*

*It is with great regret that I obey your commands in returning the letters with which I have been honored from you, and the lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me.*

With what fury Elinor must have read such a letter can only be imagined. Although she suspected the truth before opening it—that it would confirm his infidelity and seal their eternal separation—she had not anticipated such language. She could not conceive that Willoughby possessed the audacity to depart so completely from the dignity of a gentleman, sending a message so brutally cruel. Instead of expressing regret or acknowledging any breach of faith, the letter denied all special affection, offering no defense, and serving only as an insult. Every line proclaimed a man steeped in hardened villainy.

She stared at it in stunned indignation, reading it over and over again, each passage deepening her loathing. So bitter was her hatred that she dared not speak aloud, fearing she might wound Marianne further by framing their breakup not as a tragedy, but as a liberation from a monstrous union. Lost in thoughts of the depravity required to write such words, and contrasting it with the pure heart of the person who held no part in this deceit, Elinor forgot her sister's immediate plight, ignored the three remaining letters on Marianne's lap, and lost track of time entirely.

Suddenly, the sound of a carriage approaching startled her. Rushing to the window, she was astonished to see Mrs. Jennings's chaise, which had not been scheduled to arrive until noon. Determined not to abandon Marianne, even though she felt powerless to help, Elinor excused herself from accompanying the visitor, citing her sister's illness. Mrs. Jennings accepted the excuse with her usual good-natured concern. Once the carriage departed, Elinor returned to find Marianne attempting to rise, weak and dizzy from days of starvation and sleeplessness. Now that the feverish suspense had faded, the physical toll became apparent: a throbbing headache, a hollow stomach, and profound weakness.

A glass of wine restored some color to Marianne's cheeks, allowing her to whisper, "Poor Elinor! How unhappy I make you!"

"I only wish," Elinor replied softly, "that there were anything I *could* do to comfort you."

These words struck Marianne like a blow. "Oh! Elinor, I am miserable, indeed," she cried, her voice dissolving into sobs.

Unable to watch this torrent of despair in silence, Elinor urged her, "Exert yourself, dear Marianne! If you would not destroy yourself and those who love you, try to think of your mother. Think of her misery while *you* suffer. For her sake, you must strive."

"I cannot, I cannot!" Marianne wailed. "Leave me, leave me if I distress you! Leave me, hate me, forget me! But do not torture me so. Oh! How easy for those who know no sorrow to speak of exertion! Happy, happy Elinor, *you* cannot possibly comprehend what I suffer."

"Do you call *me* happy?" Elinor asked, her voice trembling. "Ah! If you knew! And can you believe me so content while I see you in such agony?"

"Forgive me, forgive me," Marianne sobbed, throwing her arms around her sister's neck. "I know you feel for me; I know the depth of your heart. Yet you must be happy; Edward loves you—what, oh what, can erase such joy?"

"Many, many circumstances," Elinor said solemnly.

"No, no, no!" Marianne shrieked wildly. "He loves you, and only you. You *cannot* have any grief."

"I cannot find pleasure while witnessing your state."

"And you will never see me otherwise. Mine is a misery nothing can alleviate."

"You must not speak so, Marianne. Have you no comforts? No friends? Is your loss so absolute that no consolation remains

Upon returning home, Mrs. Jennings hastened straight to the sisters' chamber. Without awaiting permission, she pushed open the door and entered, her face etched with genuine alarm. "How do you do, my dear?" she asked, her voice thick with compassion, addressing Marianne, who had turned her face away and offered no reply. "How fares she, Miss Dashwood? Poor creature! She looks dreadfully ill. No wonder, truly. Alas, it is all too true. He is to be wed very shortly—to a woman of questionable character! I harbor no patience for such a scoundrel. Mrs. Taylor informed me of this mere half-hour past, having learned it from a close confidante of Miss Grey's; otherwise, I daresay I would never have credited it. Indeed, I nearly fainted upon hearing the news. 'Well,' I exclaimed, 'if this holds water, then this young gentleman has treated a lady of my acquaintance with abominable cruelty. May his new wife torment him until his dying day!' And so I shall always maintain, my dear; rely on it. I cannot comprehend why men behave thus. Were I to encounter him again, I would administer a beating he hasn't known in years. Yet, there is solace, my dear Miss Marianne: he is not the sole eligible bachelor in existence. With your radiant visage, you shall never lack suitors."

Having delivered this torrent of sentiment, she departed, moving on tiptoes lest her footsteps disturb the grief of her young friend, assuming that noise might exacerbate her sorrow. To her sister's astonishment, Marianne resolved to dine with the household. Elinor counseled against it, yet Marianne insisted, declaring she could endure it and preferred the distraction of the bustle to solitude. Elinor, relieved to see her sister momentarily governed by such practical motives—though doubting her ability to sustain the meal—offered no further objection. She adjusted Marianne's gown as best she could while the latter remained prostrate on the bed, preparing to escort her to the dining room upon summons.

At table, despite her wretched appearance, Marianne consumed more food and appeared calmer than Elinor anticipated. Had she attempted speech or been fully cognizant of Mrs. Jennings's well-intentioned yet clumsy attentions, such composure would have been impossible. Instead, she spoke not a single word; the trance-like absorption of her mind shielded her from awareness of the surrounding events. Elinor, acknowledging the kindness of Mrs. Jennings despite its frequent distressing and occasionally absurd nature, managed the social exchanges that her incapacitated sister could not. Observing Marianne's unhappiness, Mrs. Jennings felt compelled to offer every conceivable comfort to alleviate her pain. She treated her with the indulgent affection of a parent toward a beloved child on the final day of vacation. Marianne was granted the prime seat by the fire, tempted with every delicacy the house possessed, and bombarded with the latest gossip. Had Elinor not witnessed the tragic pallor of her sister's countenance, she might have found amusement in Mrs. Jennings's efforts to cure a broken heart with sweets, olives, and a roaring fire. However, once the relentless repetition of these gestures became unbearable to Marianne, she could endure no longer. With a sudden cry of "Misery!" and a frantic signal to her sister not to follow, she rose abruptly and fled the room.

"Poor soul!" exclaimed Mrs. Jennings immediately after her departure. "It breaks my heart to witness her plight! I declare she has vanished without finishing her wine! And the dried cherries! Good heavens, nothing seems to benefit her. If I knew of anything she desired, I would scour the entire town for it. Truly, it is the strangest thing that a man should treat such a lovely girl so poorly! But when vast wealth resides on one side and penury on the other, Lord bless you, they care naught for such trifles!"

"The lady, then—Miss Grey, I believe you termed her—is exceedingly wealthy?"

"Fifty thousand pounds, my dear! Have you ever beheld her? They say she is sharp and stylish, though not particularly handsome. I recall her aunt, Biddy Henshawe, who married a man of considerable fortune; the entire family is affluent. Fifty thousand pounds! And, by all accounts, it will not materialize until absolutely necessary, for they claim he is utterly ruined. No wonder! Dashing about in his curricle and hunting horses! Well, it serves no purpose discussing it, but when a young man, whoever he may be, courts a beautiful girl and pledges marriage, he has no right to abandon his vow merely because he falls into poverty and a richer suitor appears. Why, in such a circumstance, should he not liquidate his assets, dismiss his staff, and enact a complete reformation instantly? I wager Miss Marianne would have waited patiently for circumstances to improve. But that is not the fashion nowadays; nothing regarding pleasure can ever be relinquished by the youth of this era."

"Do you know the character of Miss Grey? Is she reputed amiable?"

"I have heard no ill of her; indeed, she is rarely spoken of, save that Mrs. Taylor remarked this morning that Miss Walker hinted to her she believed Mr. and Mrs. Ellison would welcome Miss Grey's marriage, as the two women could never agree."

"And who are the Ellisons?"

"Her guardians, my dear. Now that she is of age, she may choose her own path; and what a choice she has made!—But pray, after pausing a moment—"Your poor sister has retired to her chamber, I presume, to lament in solitude. Is there nothing we can do to console her? Poor dear, it seems cruel to allow her to remain alone. Well, soon we shall have visitors, which might amuse her slightly. What shall we play? She detests whist, I know; but does she favor any other game?"

"My dear madam, this kindness is entirely superfluous. Marianne, I trust, will not leave her room again tonight. I shall endeavor to persuade her to retire early, for I am certain she requires rest."

"Aye, I believe that is best for her. Let her dictate her own supper and retire. Good Lord! No wonder she has looked so pale and despondent for the past week or two; this matter has undoubtedly hung over her head for that duration. And so the letter arrived today to seal her fate! Poor soul! I am sure had I suspected it, I would not have teased her about it for all my wealth. But then, how could I possibly guess such a thing? I assumed it was merely a common love letter, and you know young people enjoy being laughed at regarding such matters. Good Lord, how distressed Sir John and my daughters will be upon hearing the news! If I possessed my wits, I might have stopped at Conduit Street on my way home to inform them. Yet, I shall see them tomorrow."

"It would be unnecessary for you to caution Mrs. Palmer and Sir John against mentioning Mr. Willoughby or alluding to our situation before my sister. Their inherent goodness will surely recognize the cruelty of feigning knowledge when she is present. Furthermore, the fewer words spoken to me concerning this subject, the greater relief my feelings will experience, as you, my dear madam, will readily understand."

"Oh! Lord! Yes, I do indeed. It must be dreadful for you to hear it discussed; and regarding your sister, I assure you, I would not utter a word about it to her for the world. You observed I paid no heed during dinner. Neither would Sir John, nor my daughters, for they are all remarkably thoughtful and considerate, especially if I provide them with a hint, which I certainly shall. For my part, I believe the less said about such matters, the better; the sooner it blows over and is forgotten. What good does talking accomplish, you know?"

"In this instance, it can only cause harm; perhaps more so than in many similar cases, for it has been accompanied by circumstances that render it unfit for public discourse for the sake of everyone involved. I must concede this much to Mr. Willoughby: he has broken no formal engagement with my sister."

"Law, my dear! Do not attempt to defend him. No formal engagement indeed! After touring Allenham House with her and selecting the very rooms they were to inhabit henceforth!"

For Marianne's sake, Elinor could not press the matter further, hoping it was not required for Willoughby's benefit; although Marianne might suffer greatly, he stood to gain little by enforcing the harsh reality. After a brief silence, Mrs. Jennings, with her characteristic vivacity, erupted anew.

"Well, my dear, 'tis a true proverb: an ill wind blows no good. It will prove advantageous for Colonel Brandon. He shall have her at last; ay, verily he shall. Mark my words, if they are not married by midsummer! Good Lord, how he will chuckle over this tidings! I hope he arrives tonight. It matters not to anyone whether it is a superior match for your sister. Two thousand a year, free of debt or deduction—save for the little love-child, indeed; ay, I forgot her; but she may be apprenticed out at minimal cost, and then what does it signify? Delaford is a charming estate, I can attest; precisely the sort of quaint, old-fashioned place filled with comforts and conveniences. Enclosed by high garden walls draped with the finest fruit trees in the countryside, and a magnificent mulberry tree in one corner! Good Lord, how Charlotte and I stuffed ourselves the only time we

Marianne awoke the following morning to a dawn that offered no respite, only a renewed and sharper consciousness of misery. Despite sleeping longer than anticipated, the shadow of her grief had deepened rather than lifted. Before breakfast was served, Elinor had exhausted every avenue of conversation regarding her sister's torment. With steadfast affection and rational counsel on one side, and impetuous emotion and fluctuating despair on the other, the cycle of sorrow repeated itself endlessly. Marianne swung violently between believing Willoughby to be a victim of circumstance, innocent and worthy of her devotion, and viewing him as irredeemably lost, a man capable of no redemption. Her mood shifted with terrifying speed: one moment she cared not for the judgment of the world; the next, she wished to vanish from it forever; the moment after, she bristled with defensive energy against any scrutiny. Yet, in one specific matter, her resolve was ironclad: she rigorously avoided Mrs. Jennings' company whenever possible, and when forced to endure her presence, she maintained a cold, determined silence. Her heart had grown calloused toward the idea that Mrs. Jennings could truly sympathize with her anguish. "No, no, no!" Marianne cried out, her voice trembling with indignation. "She cannot feel. Her kindness is merely politeness; her good-nature is not tenderness. She craves only gossip, and she values me solely because I provide it."

Elinor observed these outbursts with a mixture of pity and understanding. She recognized that Marianne, gifted with excellent abilities and a noble disposition, was plagued by an irritable refinement of spirit and an excessive valuation of emotional delicacy. Like many of the clever and virtuous, Marianne lacked candor and reason. She demanded that others possess the exact same opinions and feelings she held, judging their motives strictly by the immediate impact their actions had upon her own sensitivities.

This flaw in Marianne's reasoning was cruelly exposed shortly after breakfast. Mrs. Jennings, armed with a letter and a radiant smile, entered the room, convinced she was delivering a balm to Marianne's wounds. "Now, my dear," she announced cheerfully, "I bring you something certain to do you good."

To Marianne, the words were a thunderclap. In a single heartbeat, her imagination conjured a scene of ultimate salvation: a letter from Willoughby, overflowing with repentance and tenderness, explaining everything, satisfying every doubt, followed immediately by Willoughby himself, rushing in to plead his case with eloquent eyes. The ecstasy of this fantasy was shattered in the next breath. Instead of Willoughby, the letter lay open before her, written by her own mother. The handwriting, previously unwelcome, now struck her with the force of a physical blow. The sharp sting of disappointed hope made her feel, in that agonizing second, as though she had never suffered before.

Mrs. Jennings stood frozen, her face pale with horror. Marianne's reaction was not one of quiet sadness, but of violent, streaming tears—a torrent of reproach that Mrs. Jennings could not possibly interpret correctly. Misunderstanding the depth of Marianne's betrayal, Mrs. Jennings offered only platitudes of pity, urging Marianne to read the comforting letter again. But when Marianne finally calmed enough to examine it, the contents brought little solace. Every page was dominated by Willoughby's name. Her mother, still blissfully unaware of the truth, wrote with warm confidence in their engagement, relying on Willoughby's alleged constancy. She pleaded with Marianne to be more open with them, expressing such tender affection for Willoughby and such certainty of their future happiness that Marianne wept with agony reading her mother's ignorance.

In that moment, Marianne's longing to return home intensified. Her mother became dearer to her than ever, precisely *because* of her mother's misguided faith in Willoughby. Desperate to escape the painful reality of Barton, Marianne urged her departure with wild urgency. Elinor, torn between knowing what was best for Marianne and respecting her mother's wishes, offered no advice of her own, suggesting only patience until their mother's response arrived. Eventually, Marianne agreed to wait.

As Mrs. Jennings departed earlier than usual, unable to rest until the Middletons and Palmers shared her grief, she refused Elinor's offer to accompany her, venturing out alone for the remainder of the morning. Left behind, Elinor sat with a heavy heart, aware of the terrible news she must convey. Having failed to prepare Marianne's mind for the blow, she began to write to her mother, seeking guidance for the future. Meanwhile, Marianne entered the drawing-room and stood fixed beside Elinor, watching the movement of her sister's pen. She mourned for Elinor the burden of such a difficult task, grieving even more deeply for the pain it would inflict upon their mother.

They sat thus for about fifteen minutes when a sharp rap at the door broke the silence. Marianne, whose nerves were already frayed, jumped. "Who can this be?" cried Elinor. "So early! I thought we were safe." Marianne moved to the window. "It is Colonel Brandon!" she exclaimed with visible vexation. "We are never safe from him."

"He will not enter, as Mrs. Jennings is away," Elinor reasoned.

"I will not trust to that," Marianne snapped, retreating to her room. "A man who owns no time of his own possesses no conscience to prevent him intruding on another's."

Her prediction proved correct, though based on a profound misunderstanding. Colonel Brandon did enter. Elinor, recognizing the genuine solicitude in his troubled gaze and his anxious inquiry after Marianne, could not forgive her sister for holding such a shallow view of the man.

"I met Mrs. Jennings in Bond Street," Brandon explained after the initial greeting, "and she encouraged me to come. I was eager to find you alone, which I desired greatly. My sole wish—to be honest—is to offer not temporary comfort, but a lasting conviction to your sister's mind. My regard for her, for yourself, and for your mother... will you allow me to demonstrate it by sharing certain circumstances? Nothing but a very sincere regard and an earnest desire to be useful could justify me, though I confess I have spent many hours convincing myself I am right, leading me to fear I may be wrong."

He paused, looking uncertain.

"I understand," Elinor said gently. "You have information concerning Mr. Willoughby that will reveal his true character. For Marianne, your disclosure would be the greatest act of friendship imaginable. My gratitude is secured immediately by any truth that serves this end, and hers will surely follow in time. Please, let me hear it."

"You shall," Brandon replied. "Briefly, when I left Barton last October..." He hesitated, realizing the complexity of his tale. "I must go further back. I am an awkward narrator, Miss Dashwood; I hardly know where to begin. Perhaps a short account of myself is necessary. On such a subject, I can expect little temptation to be diffuse."

Sighing heavily, he continued. "You likely forgot a conversation between us one evening at Barton Park, during a dance. I mentioned a lady I once knew, noting a resemblance to your sister, Marianne."

"Indeed," Elinor answered, "I have not forgotten it."

Brandon smiled slightly. "If I am not misled by the partiality of memory, the resemblance is striking, both in mind and person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy. This lady was my nearest relative, an orphan from infancy under my father's guardianship. Our ages were nearly identical, and from childhood we were inseparable friends. I cannot recall a time when I did not love Eliza. As we matured, my affection deepened into something I might now deem impossible to feel given my current state of desolation. Hers, for me, was equally fervent, though born of different causes, and equally unfortunate.

"At seventeen, she was lost to me forever. She was married against her will to my brother. His fortune was substantial, but our family estates were encumbered. I fear this is all that can be said of the conduct of a man who was simultaneously her uncle and guardian. My brother did not deserve her, nor did he love her. I had hoped her love for me would sustain her through difficulties; for a time, it did. But eventually, the misery of her position overwhelmed her resolve. Though she had promised me nothing would separate us—not even blindness to the truth! I have never explained how this came to pass. We were mere hours away from eloping to Scotland. The treachery—or perhaps the sheer folly—of my cousin's maid betrayed us. I was banished to a distant relative's house, and she was confined, denied liberty, society, or amusement, until my father's anger subsided. I had relied too heavily on her fortitude; the blow was severe. If her marriage had been happy, perhaps a few months would have reconciled me, or at least I would not lament it today. But it was not. My brother held no regard for her; his pleasures were base, and from the start, he treated her cruelly.

"The consequence on a mind so young, lively, and inexperienced was inevitable. She initially submitted to her misery, but happily, she did not live long enough to succumb to the regret caused by memories of me. Can we wonder that, with a husband provoking infidelity and no friend to guide or restrain her—for my father died shortly after their wedding, and I was stationed in the East Indies—

When Miss Dashwood recounted the details of her conversation to her sister, the impact was not entirely what she had anticipated. Marianne did not question the truth of a single word; she listened with steady, submissive attention, offering no objections, making no remarks, and attempting no defense of Willoughby. Instead, her tears revealed that she accepted the reality of his guilt as an impossibility to deny. While this behavior confirmed to Elinor that Marianne's conscience was finally burdened—with evidence of this shift appearing in her willingness to speak to Colonel Brandon with a newfound, albeit sorrowful, respect, and in her reduced agitation—Elinor also saw that her sister remained profoundly wretched.

Marianne's mind had settled, but only into a deep, gloomy depression. She weighed the loss of Willoughby's character far heavier than the loss of his affection. The memory of his seduction and abandonment of Miss Williams, the suffering of that innocent girl, and the haunting suspicion of what his intentions toward her once truly were, preyed relentlessly on her spirit. So consumed was she by these thoughts that she could not bring herself to speak of them even to Elinor. By brooding silently over her grief, she inflicted more pain on her sister than any open confession could have caused.

To describe Mrs. Dashwood's reaction to Elinor's letter would merely repeat the sentiments already shared by her daughters: a disappointment nearly as acute as Marianne's, and an indignation surpassing even Elinor's. Through a series of rapid, lengthy letters, Mrs. Dashwood poured out her anguish, expressing her anxious concern for Marianne and imploring her daughter to endure the misfortune with fortitude. How terrible must Marianne's affliction be, her mother reasoned, that she could speak of "fortitude"? How humiliating must the cause of such regret be, that her mother wished her not to indulge it?

Against her own desire for comfort, Mrs. Dashwood decided it was better for Marianne to be anywhere else than at Barton. There, every sight would resurrect the past in its most agonizing form, constantly presenting Willoughby as she had always known him. Therefore, she urged her daughters to extend their stay in London, a visit originally expected to last five or six weeks. The city offered a variety of occupations, sights, and company unavailable in the countryside, which might, she hoped, distract Marianne enough to find fleeting interests beyond her own sorrow, however repugnant those distractions might seem to her now. Furthermore, Mrs. Dashwood believed London safer than the country regarding Willoughby. In the crowded capital, his acquaintances would naturally sever ties with her family, making a meeting unlikely through design or accident. Chance held less sway in the throng of London than in the quiet retreat of Barton, where fate might have forced an encounter during his upcoming wedding at Allenham—a union Mrs. Dashwood had resigned herself to expecting.

Finally, a letter from her son-in-law informed her that he and his wife would arrive in town by mid-February, and she deemed it proper for them to occasionally see their brother. Though Marianne internally protested, believing her mother's plan entirely misguided and depriving her of the only true solace—her mother's personal sympathy—she submitted without opposition. Yet, Elinor found consolation in the fact that while this arrangement brought hardship to Marianne, it offered protection. Elinor, anticipating she could not avoid Edward Ferrars, comforted herself by reasoning that staying in London was preferable to an immediate return to Devonshire.

Elinor's vigilance in shielding her sister from the name of Willoughby proved effective. Neither Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, nor Mrs. Palmer ever mentioned him before Marianne. Elinor, however, could not enjoy this same privacy and was forced to endure the daily indignation of her circle. Sir John, unable to comprehend the situation, raged against the man he had once admired. "A man of whom I had such high regard! Such a good-natured fellow! I don't believe there is a bolder rider in England!" he exclaimed. "It is an unaccountable business. I wish him to the devil with all my heart. I would not speak another word to him, meet him where I might, for all the world! No, not if it were beside Barton Covert, and we were kept watching for two hours together. Such a scoundrel! Such a deceitful dog! It was only the last time we met that he offered me one of Folly's puppies, and this is the end of it!"

Mrs. Palmer echoed this sentiment in her own way. "She is determined to drop his acquaintance immediately," she declared, relieved she had never been close to him. "I wish Combe Magna wasn't so near Cleveland, but it doesn't matter; it's too far to visit anyway. I hate him so much I'm resolved never to mention his name again. I'll tell everyone I meet how useless he is." Mrs. Palmer's sympathy further manifested in her eagerness to share gossip about the impending marriage, detailing the coachmaker building the new carriage, the artist painting Willoughby's portrait, and the shop selling Miss Grey's dresses.

In stark contrast, Lady Middleton's calm and polite indifference provided a welcome relief to Elinor, whose spirits were often crushed by the clamorous kindness of the others. It was a profound comfort to Elinor to know there was at least one person in her circle who showed no curiosity about the particulars and no anxiety for her sister's health. Every social qualification seems elevated by circumstance; sometimes, one is driven to value good breeding more highly than good nature. Lady Middleton voiced her disapproval daily, simply stating, "It is very shocking, indeed!" This gentle, continuous vent allowed her to greet the Miss Dashwoods without emotion, soon forgetting the matter entirely. Having upheld the dignity of her sex and condemned the error, she felt free to focus on her own social engagements, deciding (much to Sir John's dismay) to call on Mrs. Willoughby once she was married, recognizing her as a woman of elegance and fortune.

Colonel Brandon's delicate, unobtrusive inquiries were always a comfort to Elinor. He had earned the right to discuss Marianne's despair through his zealous efforts to soothe it, and they always spoke with complete confidence. His greatest reward was the pitying glance Marianne occasionally cast his way and the softness of her voice when she was compelled to address him. These signs assured the Colonel that his efforts had fostered goodwill, giving Elinor hope for future reconciliation. Meanwhile, Mrs. Jennings, unaware of this silent progress, noticed only that the Colonel remained as grave as ever. Unable to persuade him to propose or to ask her to do so, she began to fear that the marriage would be delayed until Michaelmas, and eventually concluded it would not happen at all. The apparent harmony between the Colonel and Miss Dashwood suggested that the garden's attractions—the mulberry tree, the canal, and the yew arbor—would belong to her alone, leading Mrs. Jennings to cease thinking of Mrs. Ferrars entirely.

Early in February, barely two weeks after receiving Willoughby's letter, Elinor performed the painful duty of informing her sister of his marriage. She ensured the news reached Marianne privately, sparing her the shock of reading it in the public papers, which she eagerly scanned each morning. Marianne received the tidings with resolute composure, shedding no initial tears. However, shortly thereafter, they burst forth, leaving her for the remainder of the day in a state scarcely less miserable than when she first feared the event.

The newlyweds departed London immediately after the ceremony. Elinor now hoped to gradually encourage her sister to venture outdoors again, as she had previously done. Around this time, the two Miss Steeles returned to their relatives' homes in Conduit and Berkeley Streets, greeted with great warmth. Only Elinor felt sorrow at their presence; their arrival always pained her, and she struggled to respond graciously to Lucy's overwhelming delight at finding her still in town.

"I should have been quite disappointed if I hadn't found you here *still*," Lucy remarked, emphasizing the word. "But I always thought I *should*. I was almost sure you wouldn't leave London for a while, though you *told* me at Barton you wouldn't stay more than a month. I thought, at the time, you'd likely change your mind when it came to the point. It would have been such a pity to have gone before your brother and sister arrived. And now, surely, you aren't in a hurry to leave. I am amazingly glad you didn't stick to your word."

Elinor understood the sting behind these words and used all her self-control to mask her displeasure. "Well, my dear," Mrs. Jennings interjected, "how did you travel?"

"Not in the stage, I assure you," Miss Steele replied with quick exultation. "We came post all the way and had a very smart beau to attend us. Dr. Davies was coming to town, so we joined him in a post-chaise. He behaved very genteelly and paid ten or twelve shillings more than we did."

"Oh, oh!" cried Mrs. Jennings. "Very pretty, indeed! And the Doctor is a single man, I warrant you."

"There now," said Miss Steele, feigning a simper, "everyone laughs at me about the Doctor, and I can't understand why. My cousins say they're sure I've made a conquest, but for my part, I declare I never think about him from one hour's end to another. 'Lord! Here comes your beau, Nancy,' my cousin said the other

After some initial resistance, Marianne eventually relented to her sister's earnest pleas and agreed to venture out with Elinor and Mrs. Jennings one morning for a brief half-hour excursion. However, she insisted on strict conditions: no social calls were to be made, and their sole purpose was to accompany Elinor to Mr. Gray's jewelry shop in Sackville Street, where negotiations regarding the exchange of her mother's antique jewels were underway.

As they approached the shop door, Mrs. Jennings suddenly remembered a lady residing at the opposite end of the street whom she felt compelled to visit. Since she had no personal business at Mr. Gray's, it was decided that while the younger ladies attended to their errands, Mrs. Jennings would pay her respects and return promptly. Upon entering the shop, the Dashwood sisters found themselves confronted by a crowded room teeming with customers. With no clerk available to assist them, they were forced to wait. Their only recourse was to take a seat at the end of the counter that appeared to promise the swiftest turnover. Only one gentleman stood nearby, and perhaps Elinor harbored a faint hope that his inherent politeness would expedite the process. Yet, his keen eye and refined taste proved superior to his courtesy. He was deeply engrossed in commissioning a toothpick-case, meticulously deliberating over its dimensions, form, and ornamentation. For nearly fifteen minutes, he examined and debated every option presented, guided solely by his own inventive whims. Until this intricate decision was finalized, he offered the two ladies merely a handful of wide, staring glances—a perfunctory acknowledgment that left Elinor with a vivid memory of a face possessing a striking, natural, and utterly insignificant charm, despite being dressed in the height of fashion.

Marianne, spared the pangs of contempt and irritation caused by this intrusive scrutiny and the dog-like manner in which he dismissed various "horrors" of toothpick-cases, remained blissfully unaware. She successfully retreated inward, collecting her thoughts until she was as oblivious to the chaotic scene unfolding in Mr. Gray's shop as she would be in her own bedroom. Finally, the transaction concluded. Ivory, gold, and pearls were assigned their places. The gentleman, having declared the final date by which his existence could endure without his new treasure, slowly donned his gloves. Bestowing one last look upon the Miss Dashwoods—a gaze that seemed to demand admiration rather than simply offer it—he departed with an air of genuine self-satisfaction and feigned indifference.

Elinor wasted no time advancing her own business and was on the verge of completing it when another gentleman approached her side. Turning to meet his gaze, she was surprised to recognize her brother, John Dashwood. Their mutual affection and delight in重逢 were sufficient to create a respectable impression amidst the bustle of the shop. John was genuinely pleased to see his sisters again; his inquiries concerning their mother were respectful and attentive. Elinor learned that he and his wife, Fanny, had arrived in London two days prior.

"I had hoped to call upon you yesterday," he remarked, "but it was impossible; we were obliged to take Harry to see the wild beasts at Exeter Exchange, and spent the remainder of the day with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was thoroughly delighted. This morning, I had fully intended to drop by if I could spare half an hour, but one invariably finds so much to do upon first arriving in town. I am here today to reserve a seal for Fanny. Tomorrow, however, I expect to be able to visit in Berkeley Street and introduce myself to your friend, Mrs. Jennings. I understand she is a woman of considerable fortune. Furthermore, you must introduce me to the Middletons. As my mother-in-law's relatives, I shall be eager to extend them every due respect. I gather they are excellent neighbors to you in the countryside."

"Excellent indeed," Elinor replied warmly. "Their attention to our comfort and their friendliness in every detail are beyond expression."

"I am extremely glad to hear this, upon my word; truly glad. But so it ought to be; they are people of substantial means, related to you, and every civility and accommodation that serves to make your situation pleasant might reasonably be expected. And you are most comfortably settled in your little cottage, lacking for nothing!"

"Eleanor brought us a most charming account of the place," he continued. "He described it as the most complete residence of its kind ever seen, noting that you all seemed to enjoy it beyond measure. It was a great satisfaction to us to hear this, I assure you."

Elinor felt a twinge of shame toward her brother and was relieved when Mrs. Jennings's servant arrived to inform them that his mistress awaited them at the door. Mr. Dashwood escorted them downstairs, introducing them to Mrs. Jennings beside her carriage. Repeating his hope to call the following day, he took his leave. His subsequent visit was duly paid, accompanied by a pretextual apology from his sister-in-law for her absence, claiming she was entirely occupied with her mother and thus had no leisure to go anywhere. Mrs. Jennings, however, immediately assured him that she would not stand on ceremony, reminding him that they were all cousins or something akin to kin. She promised to visit Mrs. John Dashwood shortly, bringing her sisters along.

His demeanor toward the Dashwood sisters was calmly kind, while his treatment of Mrs. Jennings was notably attentive. Soon after, Colonel Brandon entered. John eyed him with a curious calculation that suggested he sought to know him primarily to ascertain his wealth, offering equal civility only once that condition was met. After remaining for half an hour, John invited Elinor to walk with him to Conduit Street to introduce him to Sir John and Lady Middleton. The weather was exceptionally fine, and she readily agreed.

Once outside, his inquiries commenced immediately. "Who is Colonel Brandon? Is he a man of fortune?"

"Yes; he possesses very good property in Dorsetshire."

"I am glad to hear that. He appears to be a most gentlemanlike man, and I think, Elinor, I may congratulate you on the prospect of a very respectable establishment in life."

"Me, brother? What do you mean?"

"He likes you. I observed him closely and am convinced of it. What is the extent of his fortune?"

"I believe it amounts to about two thousand a year."

"Two thousand a year!" he exclaimed, rising to a pitch of enthusiastic generosity. "Elinor, I wish with all my heart it were *twice* as much, for your sake."

"Indeed, I believe you," Elinor replied dryly, "but I am very certain that Colonel Brandon has not the slightest intention of marrying me."

"You are mistaken, Elinor; greatly mistaken. A very little effort on your part would secure him. Perhaps he is currently undecided; the modesty of your fortune may cause hesitation, or his friends may advise against it. But certain little attentions and encouragements—which ladies can so easily dispense—would fix him, regardless of his inclinations. There is no reason why you should not attempt to win him. It is not to be supposed that any prior attachment exists on your side—in short, you know as well as I do that such matters are quite out of the question; the objections are insurmountable. You possess too much sense not to see this clearly. Colonel Brandon must be the man, and no lack of civility on my part shall prevent him from becoming pleased with you and your family. It is a match destined to give universal satisfaction. In short, it is a matter of—"he lowered his voice to an important whisper—"which will be exceedingly welcome to *all parties*."

Recalling himself, he added, "That is, I mean to say—your friends are truly anxious to see you well settled; Fanny especially, for she holds your interests very close to her heart, I assure you. And her mother, Mrs. Ferrars, a woman of excellent nature, is sure it would bring her great pleasure; she mentioned as much the other day."

Elinor refused to offer any response.

"It would be somewhat remarkable, now," he continued, "perhaps even droll, if Fanny were to settle a brother while I settle a sister simultaneously. And yet, it is not entirely unlikely."

"Is Mr. Edward Ferrars," Elinor asked with resolve, "going to be married?"

"It is not officially settled, but there is talk of it. He has a most excellent mother. Mrs. Ferrars, with the utmost liberality, intends to provide him with an annual settlement of one thousand pounds if the union proceeds. The lady is the Honorable Miss Morton, the only daughter of the late Lord Morton, endowed with thirty thousand pounds. It is a highly desirable connection on both sides, and I have no doubt it will materialize in time. A thousand a-year is a significant sum for a mother to bestow permanently, yet Mrs. Ferrars possesses a noble spirit. To cite another example of her generosity: upon our arrival in town, realizing that funds were scarce for us at the moment, she placed banknotes totaling two hundred pounds into Fanny's hands. It was extremely welcome, for we must maintain a high standard of expenditure while here."

He paused, expecting her assent and compassion, forcing himself to reply, "Your expenses, both in town and country, must certainly be considerable; yet your income is substantial."

"Not so large, I dare say, as many suppose. I do not intend to complain, however; it is undoubtedly a comfortable income, and I hope it will improve with time. The enclosure of Norland Common, currently underway, is a severe drain. Moreover, I made a small purchase within the

Mrs. John Dashwood possessed such absolute faith in her husband's discernment that she waited until the very next day to pay calls upon Mrs. Jennings and her daughter. Her trust was richly rewarded; she discovered that even the former, the woman with whom her sisters were residing, was far from unworthy of her attention. As for Lady Middleton, she found her to be one of the most charming women in existence! Lady Middleton, in turn, was equally captivated by Mrs. Dashwood. Between them lay a certain cold-hearted selfishness that acted as a powerful magnet, drawing them together through a shared insipid propriety of manner and a mutual lack of genuine understanding.

However, the very qualities that secured Mrs. John Dashwood the high regard of Lady Middleton failed to win the favor of Mrs. Jennings. To the latter, she appeared merely as a somewhat proud-looking woman of uninviting address, who greeted her husband's sisters without affection and seemingly had nothing meaningful to say to them. Indeed, during the quarter-hour spent at Berkeley Street, Mrs. Jennings sat in silence for at least seven and a half minutes.

Elinor yearned intensely to inquire, though she chose not to ask aloud, whether Edward was currently in London. Yet, nothing would have prompted Fanny to volunteer his name before her until she could announce that his marriage to Miss Morton was finalized, or until her husband's hopes regarding Colonel Brandon were realized. She believed them still so deeply attached that they required constant vigilance to keep them apart in word and deed. Nevertheless, the information Fanny withheld soon surfaced from another source. Lucy arrived shortly thereafter to solicit Elinor's sympathy for her inability to see Edward, despite his arrival in town with Mr. and Mrs. Dashwood. He dared not visit Bartlett's Buildings for fear of being detected, and though their mutual longing to meet was palpable, they could do nothing but correspond. Edward confirmed his presence in town twice by leaving cards on the table at Berkeley Street. Elinor was pleased that he had called, and even more so that she had managed to miss him.

The Dashwoods were so extravagantly delighted with the Middletons that, despite rarely giving entertainments, they resolved to host them. Soon after forming their acquaintance, they invited the couple to dine in Harley Street, where they had recently leased a fine house for three months. Their sisters and Mrs. Jennings were cordially invited, and John Dashwood took care to secure Colonel Brandon. Always eager to be near the Miss Dashwoods, the Colonel received his enthusiastic courtesies with some surprise, but with even greater pleasure. They intended to invite Mrs. Ferrars, though Elinor could not ascertain if her sons would attend. The prospect of meeting *her*, however, filled Elinor with keen interest. Though she could now face Edward's mother without the intense anxiety that once plagued such introductions, and could view her with perfect indifference regarding her own self-worth, her desire to be in Mrs. Ferrars' company and her curiosity about her appearance remained as vivid as ever.

This anticipation was soon intensified, more powerfully than pleasantly, by news that the Miss Steeles were also to attend. Having won such favorable impressions from Lady Middleton, and rendered so agreeable by their assiduous efforts, that although Lucy lacked true elegance and her sister was scarcely genteel, they were as readily invited by Sir John to spend a week or two in Conduit Street. It proved particularly convenient for the Miss Steeles that their visit commenced a few days prior to the party, once the Dashwoods' invitation became known. While their connection as nieces of the gentleman who had long cared for her brother might not have secured them seats at Mrs. John Dashwood's table otherwise, as guests of Lady Middleton, they were welcome. Lucy, who had long desired to be personally acquainted with the family, to gauge their characters and assess her own predicaments, and to find opportunities to please them, had never been happier in her life upon receiving Mrs. John Dashwood's card.

For Elinor, the effect was starkly different. She immediately resolved that Edward, living with his mother, must be invited to a party hosted by his sister, and she dreaded the thought of seeing him for the first time since everything transpired, surrounded by Lucy! She struggled to comprehend how she could endure it. These fears, perhaps not entirely grounded in reason and certainly devoid of truth, were alleviated not by her own reflection, but by Lucy's goodwill. Believing she was dealing a severe blow, Lucy informed Elinor that Edward would certainly not be in Harley Street on Tuesday, hoping further to soothe her by convincing her that he was absent solely due to his profound affection for her, which he could not hide when they were together.

Tuesday finally arrived, the day destined to introduce the two young ladies to this formidable mother-in-law. "Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!" cried Lucy as they ascended the stairs together—for the Middletons had arrived immediately after Mrs. Jennings, and they all followed the servant simultaneously—"there is no one here but you who can truly feel for me. I declare I can hardly stand. Good heavens! In a moment I shall behold the person upon whose happiness my entire future depends—that is to become my mother!"

Elinor could have offered immediate relief by suggesting they might be about to see Miss Morton's mother rather than her own, but instead, she assured Lucy with great sincerity that she pitied her, to the utter astonishment of Lucy, who, though genuinely uncomfortable, had hoped at least to inspire in Elinor a feeling of irrepressible envy.

Mrs. Ferrars was a small, thin woman, upright to the point of formality in her bearing, and serious to the verge of sourness in her expression. Her complexion was sallow; her features were small, lacking beauty and naturally devoid of expression. However, a fortunate furrowing of the brow saved her countenance from the disgrace of dullness, lending it instead the strong marks of pride and ill-nature. She was not a woman of many words; unlike most, she measured her speech strictly against the number of her thoughts. Of the few syllables that escaped her lips, not one was directed toward Miss Dashwood, whom she regarded with a spirited determination to dislike her regardless of circumstances.

Elinor could no longer be distressed by such behavior. A few months prior, it would have wounded her deeply, but Mrs. Ferrars held no power to trouble her now. Moreover, the disparity in her treatment of the Miss Steeles—a difference seemingly engineered to humble them further—only amused Elinor. She could not help smiling at the graciousness displayed by both mother and daughter toward the very person whom, had they known the full extent of the situation, they would have sought most diligently to mortify. Meanwhile, Elinor herself, possessing comparatively little power to wound them, sat pointedly slighted by both.

As she smiled at this misplaced grace, she could not refrain from despising all four of them. She observed the mean-spirited folly from which it sprang and the calculated attentions with which the Miss Steeles courted its continuation. Lucy was in a state of pure exultation at being so honorably distinguished, while Miss Steele needed only to be teased about Dr. Davies to achieve perfect happiness.

The dinner was a grand affair, attended by numerous servants, signaling the mistress's penchant for display and the master's capacity to sustain it. Despite the ongoing improvements to the Norland estate and the fact that its owner had once been within a thousand pounds of selling at a loss, no trace of the indigence he had attempted to project appeared. No poverty existed anywhere, save for a distinct lack of conversation, which was considerable. John Dashwood had little of value to contribute, and his wife even less. Yet, there was no special shame in this, for it was common among their chief guests, who almost universally suffered from one or more disqualifications for being agreeable: a lack of natural or cultivated sense, a lack of elegance, a lack of spirit, or a lack of temper.

When the ladies retired to the drawing-room after dinner, this conversational poverty became especially apparent. The gentlemen had indeed supplied the discourse with variety—politics, enclosed lands, and broken horses—but that was all. Only one topic engaged the ladies until coffee was served: the relative heights of Harry Dashwood and Lady Middleton's second son, William, who were nearly the same age. Had both children been present, the matter might have been settled easily by measurement; but since only Harry was there, it devolved into conjecture. Everyone felt entitled to be equally positive in their opinions and to repeat them ad infinitum. The standings were as follows: the two mothers, though each firmly convinced her own son was taller, politely decided in favor of the other. The two grandmothers, with equal partiality but greater sincerity, were equally earnest in supporting their own descendants. Lucy, hardly less anxious to please one parent than the other, deemed both boys remarkably tall for their age and could not conceive of any difference whatsoever. Miss Steele, with even greater address, gave credit to each boy as quickly as possible.

Elinor, having once expressed her opinion in favor of William (thereby offending Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny further), saw no need to enforce it with additional assertions. Marianne, when called upon, offended everyone by declaring she had no opinion to offer, as she had never considered the matter.

Before departing Norland, Elinor had painted a very attractive pair of screens for her sister-in-law. Now freshly mounted and brought home, they adorned the current drawing-room. Catching John Dashwood's eye as he followed the other gentlemen into the room, they were officiously handed to Colonel Brandon for his admiration. "

Elinor's curiosity regarding Mrs. Ferrars was finally sated. Having observed everything necessary to confirm that a deeper alliance between their families was impossible, she had witnessed firsthand the depth of her aunt-in-law's pride, her pettiness, and her stubborn prejudice against herself. These traits illuminated the formidable obstacles that had long complicated the engagement between Edward and Elinor, had circumstances permitted it. Indeed, having seen nearly enough to appreciate, for her own sake, that a higher barrier protected her from the torment of depending on Mrs. Ferrars' whims or seeking her approval, Elinor resolved to be grateful. While she did not find herself exulting in Edward's entanglement with Lucy, she firmly decided that, had Lucy possessed any genuine amiability, she *ought* to have celebrated it.

Yet, she marveled at how Lucy's spirits could remain so buoyant despite the cold civility of Mrs. Ferrars. How could her vanity be so blinded that she mistook attention paid solely because she was *not* Elinor for personal compliments? How could she draw encouragement from a preference granted merely because her true position remained hidden? These truths had been evident in Lucy's gaze during the previous evening and were confirmed the following morning with startling clarity. At Lucy's specific request, Lady Middleton had seated her beside Elinor in Berkeley Street, ostensibly to discuss matters, but truly to provide an opportunity for Lucy to declare her happiness privately. Fate intervened kindly; shortly after Elinor's arrival, a message from Mrs. Palmer whisked Mrs. Jennings away, leaving the two sisters alone.

"My dear friend," Lucy exclaimed the moment they were secluded, "I must speak to you of my bliss. Was there ever anything so flattering as Mrs. Ferrars's demeanor toward me yesterday? She was so excessively affable! You know how I dreaded the thought of encountering her, yet the instant I was introduced, her behavior radiated such favor that it seemed she had instantly taken a fancy to me. Does it not strike you as true? You witnessed it all; surely you were equally impressed?"

"She was certainly very civil to you," Elinor replied cautiously.

"Civil!" Lucy retorted sharply. "Did you perceive nothing beyond mere politeness? I saw far more. A kindness reserved exclusively for me! No pride, no haughtiness—unlike your sister, who remains exactly the same, all sweetness and charm!"

Elinor attempted to steer the conversation elsewhere, but Lucy persisted, demanding acknowledgment of her reasons for happiness. Reluctantly, Elinor complied.

"Undoubtedly, if they knew of your engagement," Lucy pressed, "their treatment would seem even more flattering. But since that is not the case—"

"I suspected you would say that," Lucy interrupted quickly. "However, there is absolutely no reason for Mrs. Ferrars to appear fond of me unless she is, and her affection is everything. Do not try to dissuade me from my contentment. I am certain it will conclude happily, devoid of the difficulties I once feared. Mrs. Ferrars is a charming woman, and so is your sister. They are both delightful! I wonder I never heard you praise how agreeable Mrs. Dashwood was!"

Elinor had no response to offer and offered none.

"Are you ill, Miss Dashwood?" Lucy asked, noting her silence. "You seem listless; you do not speak. Are you unwell?"

"I have never been in better health," Elinor assured her.

"I am glad to hear it with all my heart, though you certainly did not look it. I would be sorrowful to lose your friendship; heaven knows what I should have done without you."

Elinor managed a polite reply, though she doubted its efficacy. Yet, it appeared to satisfy Lucy, who immediately responded, "Indeed, I am fully convinced of your regard for me. Next to Edward's love, it is my greatest comfort. Poor Edward! But now, there is one bright prospect: we shall be able to meet frequently. Lady Middleton delights in Mrs. Dashwood, so we shall likely spend much time in Harley Street. Besides, Edward spends half his time with his sister, and Lady Middleton and Mrs. Ferrars will begin visiting soon. Both Mrs. Ferrars and your sister have stated repeatedly how glad they would be to see me. They are such charming women! If you ever tell your sister what I think of her, you cannot praise her too highly."

Elinor refused to encourage Lucy's hopes that she would ever speak so freely to her sister. Undeterred, Lucy continued, "I am certain I would have noticed instantly if Mrs. Ferrars disliked me. Had she merely offered a formal courtesy, ignored me thereafter, and avoided eye contact with a pleasant expression—as you understand—I would have despaired completely. I could not endure it. Where she dislikes, I know her aversion is fierce."

Before Elinor could respond to this triumphant assessment of her rival's acumen, the door opened, and a servant announced Mr. Ferrars. Moments later, Edward entered.

The atmosphere grew instantly tense. Each face betrayed the awkwardness of the situation. They appeared collectively foolish, and Edward seemed torn between fleeing the room and advancing further inside. It was precisely the uncomfortable convergence they had all sought to avoid. Not only were the three of them together, but they were isolated without the buffer of any other guest.

The ladies recovered composure first. It was not Lucy's role to assert dominance, and the facade of secrecy required maintenance. She could only display her tenderness through her gaze, offering a slight greeting before falling silent. Elinor, however, faced a greater challenge. Driven by anxiety for Edward's sake and her own, she compelled herself, after a brief pause, to greet him with a demeanor that was nearly natural and open. Through renewed effort, she succeeded. She would not let Lucy's presence or her own sense of injustice prevent her from expressing her joy at seeing him and voicing her regret at missing his earlier call in Berkeley Street. She would not allow Lucy's watchful eyes to deter her from offering the attentions due to a friend and near relative, even as she sensed Lucy scrutinizing her every move.

Edward's manners received some reassurance from Elinor's efforts, granting him the courage to sit, though his embarrassment surpassed theirs in proportion to the absurdity of the scene—a rarity perhaps for men, but understandable given the circumstances. His heart lacked Lucy's indifference, and his conscience could not find the peace Elinor enjoyed. Lucy, maintaining a demure and fixed expression, contributed nothing to alleviate the tension, remaining utterly silent. Consequently, nearly all conversation flowed from Elinor, who volunteered details about her mother's health and their journey to town—information Edward should have inquired about but neglected to ask.

Her efforts did not end there. Soon, feeling a heroic impulse, she decided, under the pretext of fetching Marianne, to withdraw the others to privacy. She executed this maneuver with remarkable grace, lingering on the landing for several minutes with dignified resolve before descending to her sister. Once separated, however, Edward's rapture ceased abruptly. Marianne's enthusiasm drew her immediately into the drawing-room. Her joy in seeing him was intense and vocal, characteristic of her nature. Meeting him with an outstretched hand and a voice brimming with affection, she cried, "Dear Edward! This is a moment of profound happiness! It would almost redeem everything!"

Edward endeavored to reciprocate her kindness appropriately, yet before such witnesses, he dared not utter half of what he truly felt. They resumed their seats, and for a few moments, silence reigned. Marianne gazed alternately at Edward and Elinor with tender intensity, lamenting only that their shared delight was dampened by Lucy's unwanted presence.

Edward broke the silence first, commenting on Marianne's changed complexion and expressing concern that London might not agree with her constitution.

"Oh, do not think of me!" she replied with spirited earnestness, though tears filled her eyes. "Do not consider *my* health. See, Elinor is well. That must suffice for us both."

This remark offered no solace to Edward or Elinor, nor did it win Lucy's goodwill, whose gaze toward Marianne held no benevolence.

"Do you enjoy London?" Edward asked, attempting to shift the topic.

"Not at all. I anticipated great pleasure, yet found none. The sight of you, Edward, is the sole comfort it has provided. Thank Heaven, you remain as you always were!" She paused, and no one spoke.

"I believe, Elinor," Marianne added shortly, "we must entrust Edward with our care upon returning to Barton. In a week or two, I presume, we shall depart. I trust Edward will not be overly reluctant to accept this responsibility."

Poor Edward murmured indistinctly, unsure even himself of what he said. Marianne, perceiving his agitation and interpreting it according to her own inclinations, was perfectly satisfied and swiftly changed the subject.

"We spent such a dreadful day in Harley Street yesterday! So dull, so wretchedly dull! But I have much to tell you about it that cannot wait until now." With admirable discretion, she deferred her declaration that their mutual relations were increasingly disagreeable and her particular disgust for his mother until they were alone.

"But why were you not there, Edward? Why did you not come?"

"I was engaged elsewhere."

"Engaged! But what engagement outweighs the meeting of such friends?"

"Perhaps, Miss Marianne," cried Lucy, seizing the opportunity to retaliate, "you believe young men disregard engagements whenever they choose, regardless of their size?"

Within a few days of their reunion, the newspapers proclaimed to the world that Mrs. Thomas Palmer, Esquire, had safely delivered a son and heir. While this news was merely a standard social update, it held profound significance for those intimately connected to the family, particularly Mrs. Jennings. The birth became the central focus of her domestic life, prompting a significant shift in her schedule. Determined to be with her daughter Charlotte as much as possible, Mrs. Jennings began visiting Conduit Street every morning immediately after dressing, remaining until late evening. Consequently, the Dashwood sisters, at the specific request of the Middletons, relocated to spend their entire days in Conduit Street.

Although the Miss Dashwoods would have preferred to remain at Mrs. Jennings's home, especially during the mornings, they felt compelled to comply with the collective wishes of everyone involved. Thus, their time was effectively transferred to Lady Middleton and the two Miss Steeles. In reality, the Dashwoods' company was valued by these ladies no more than it was claimed to be desired. The sisters possessed too much sense to serve as desirable companions for Lady Middleton, whose intellect required constant flattery. Furthermore, the Miss Steeles viewed the Dashwoods with suspicion, regarding them as intruders encroaching on territory they intended to monopolize.

Lady Middleton's behavior toward Elinor and Marianne was outwardly impeccable, yet her internal sentiments were quite different. Unable to believe anyone capable of goodness who refused to flatter her or her children, she judged the Dashwoods lacking in nature. Additionally, their evident fondness for reading led her to assume they were satirical—a label she applied loosely to anyone she disliked, regardless of whether they truly understood satire. Their presence acted as a constraint on both Lady Middleton and Lucy Steele; it curbed the former's idleness and threatened the latter's ability to dispense the flattery she cherished. Lady Middleton feared the Dashwoods would despise her for offering such praise, while Lucy worried they would mock her efforts.

Miss Steele, however, was the least disturbed by their arrival. She would have gladly accepted a detailed account of the affair between Marianne and Mr. Willoughby in exchange for yielding her prime spot by the fire after dinner. Yet, such information was withheld. Occasional expressions of pity from Miss Steele toward Marianne, or remarks on the fickleness of lovers dropped before Marianne herself, elicited only indifference from Elinor or visible disgust from Marianne. Even light-hearted teasing about the doctor would have secured Miss Steele's friendship, but the Dashwoods showed no inclination to oblige her. Should Sir John dine elsewhere, Miss Steele might spend an entire day without hearing a single jest directed at herself, save for the ones she kindly offered.

Despite these underlying tensions and resentments, Mrs. Jennings remained blissfully unaware. She delighted in the prospect of her daughters being together, frequently congratulating the young women nightly on escaping the company of a "stupid old woman" for so long. Whether joining them at Sir John's residence or her own, she arrived in high spirits, brimming with self-importance. She attributed Charlotte's well-being entirely to her own care and recounted the infant's condition with exhaustive detail, a narrative style that only Miss Steele possessed the curiosity to appreciate.

One persistent annoyance, however, troubled Mrs. Jennings daily. Mr. Palmer adhered to the conventional, albeit unfatherly, belief shared by many men that all infants are identical. Despite observing striking resemblances between the baby and various relatives on both sides, he refused to acknowledge the child's unique features. He could not be persuaded that the baby was anything other than a generic specimen of infancy, nor could he be moved to admit it was the finest child in the world.

A separate misfortune soon befell Mrs. John Dashwood. While her two sisters visited her in Harley Street alongside Mrs. Jennings, another acquaintance unexpectedly dropped by. Although this encounter seemed innocuous, human imagination often leads to erroneous conclusions based on scant evidence. Upon hearing the name of the Miss Dashwoods and learning they were Mr. Dashwood's sisters, the visitor immediately assumed they were residing in Harley Street. This misconception resulted in invitations, issued within a day or two, for a small musical gathering at her home, extending to the Dashwoods, their brother, and their sister.

Mrs. John Dashwood was forced to accept this invitation, submitting to the considerable inconvenience of arranging carriage transport for the Miss Dashwoods. Worse still, she dreaded the obligation of appearing attentive to them, fearing they might expect further outings. While she retained the power to disappoint them, she recognized that those determined to act wrongly feel aggrieved by any suggestion of higher standards. By this time, Marianne had developed a routine of daily excursions; it mattered little to her whether she attended or not. She prepared mechanically for every evening's engagement, often unaware of the destination until the last moment, and displayed perfect indifference regarding her attire.

In contrast, Miss Steele exercised minute scrutiny over Marianne's appearance. During the first five minutes of their meeting, she assessed every aspect of Marianne's toilette, questioning the price of every garment item, estimating the total number of dresses owned, and speculating on weekly laundry costs and annual spending allowances. These intrusive inquiries invariably concluded with a complimentary remark, intended as a sweetener but perceived by Marianne as the height of impudence. After enduring examinations of her gown's fabric, shoe color, and hairstyle, Marianne was typically dismissed with assurances that she looked "vastly smart" and destined to make numerous conquests. On this particular evening, such encouragement proved futile as the sisters departed promptly for their brother's carriage, leaving Mrs. John Dashwood disappointed that they had not delayed their departure to inconvenience her or her coachman.

The musical party itself was unremarkable, comprising a mixture of genuine connoisseurs and casual attendees, with performers who considered themselves—and were praised by their circle—as premier private musicians in England. Elinor, neither musically inclined nor pretentious, freely diverted her gaze from the grand piano to other objects in the room. Among a group of young men, she spotted the individual who had previously lectured them on toothpick cases at Gray's Inn. Shortly thereafter, he approached her brother, engaging in familiar conversation. Just as Elinor resolved to inquire about his identity, Mr. Dashwood introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

Mr. Ferrars greeted Elinor with easy civility, executing a bow that clearly signaled his reputation as the coxcomb Lucy had described. Had Elinor's affection for Edward relied less on his personal merits and more on those of his kin, this interaction might have sealed her disapproval. However, observing the stark contrast between the two brothers, she found no reason to lose her charitable view of Edward's modesty and worth.

During a brief conversation, Robert explained the disparity between them. Lamenting his brother's awkwardness (*gaucherie*), which he believed hindered his social integration, he candidly attributed it not to natural deficiency but to the misfortune of a private education. He argued that his own success stemmed solely from attending a public school, despite possessing no superior natural talents. "Upon my soul," he remarked, "I believe it is nothing more. I often assure my mother of this. 'My dear Madam,' I tell her, 'the damage is now irreparable, and entirely your fault. Why did you yield to my uncle Sir Robert's persuasion against your own judgment to send Edward to Mr. Pratt's at such a critical juncture? Had you sent him to Westminster like myself, this would never have occurred.' This is my perspective, and my mother is fully convinced of her error."

Elinor refrained from contradicting him, although she could not bring herself to approve of Edward's upbringing in Mr. Pratt's household. Shifting the topic, Robert noted their residence in Devonshire, specifically a cottage near Dawlish. Elinor corrected his geographical assumption, noting that one need not live near Dawlish to reside in Devonshire. Nevertheless, he enthusiastically endorsed their choice of housing. "I am excessively fond of cottages," he declared. "There is always such comfort and elegance about them. If I had funds to spare, I would purchase land and build one near London, allowing me to invite friends and enjoy happiness. I advise everyone building to construct a cottage."

He recounted an anecdote involving his friend Lord Courtland, who had presented three architectural plans by Bonomi. Robert had immediately rejected all three, advising instead to build a cottage. Later, at his friend Elliott's estate near Dartford, he demonstrated that a cottage could comfortably host a large dance, utilizing the dining parlour for couples, the drawing-room for card tables, the library for refreshments, and the saloon for supper. Elinor agreed with his reasoning, feeling no need to offer rational counterarguments.

As John Dashwood shared little enthusiasm for music, his mind wandered freely. A thought occurred to him during the evening, which he proposed to his wife upon their return home. Reflecting on Mrs. Dennison's error in assuming the Dashwoods were her guests, he suggested formally inviting them to stay while Mrs. Jennings was occupied elsewhere. He reasoned the expense would be negligible and the inconvenience minimal, viewing it as a necessary gesture to clear his conscience regarding his promise to his father.

Fanny reacted with alarm. "I cannot see how this can be done without offending Lady Middleton," she protested. "Since they spend every day with her, asking them away seems improper. I am always eager to show hospitality, as tonight proves, but they are Lady Middleton's visitors." Her husband, humbly disagreeing, argued that since they had already spent a week in Conduit Street, Lady Middleton would not be displeased if they spent an equal duration with such close relations.

After a fortnight, Mrs. Palmer recovered sufficiently for her mother to cease devoting her entire days to her care. Contenting herself with visits once or twice daily, Mrs. Jennings returned to her own home and routine, finding the Dashwood sisters eager to resume their previous level of hospitality.

It was about the third or fourth morning following this resumption of normalcy that Mrs. Jennings entered the drawing-room at Berkeley Street. Having just returned from her customary visit to Mrs. Palmer, she found Elinor sitting alone, radiating an air of hurried importance that suggested she expected to hear something extraordinary. Seizing the moment, Mrs. Jennings launched directly into her revelation.

"Good heavens, my dear Miss Dashwood!" she exclaimed. "Have you heard the news?"

"No, ma'am. What is it?"

"It is something truly strange! But you shall hear everything. Upon arriving at Mr. Palmer's, I found Charlotte in a complete panic regarding the child.
