She insisted it was terribly ill—that it was crying, fretting, and covered in pimples. I examined it immediately and remarked, 'Good heavens, my dear, it is nothing at all, merely the red gum.' Nurse agreed instantly. Yet, Charlotte refused to be reassured, so Mr. Donovan was summoned. Fortunately, he had just arrived from Harley Street and dropped in. Upon seeing the child, he concurred with us that it was simply the red gum, and Charlotte finally calmed down. However, just as he departed, it occurred to me—I confess I cannot explain how—to inquire if there was any other news. Instantly, he smirked, simpered, adopted a grave demeanor, and appeared to possess some hidden knowledge. Finally, he whispered, 'To prevent any unpleasant reports reaching the young ladies under your care concerning their sister's indisposition, I deem it advisable to state that there is no cause for alarm; I hope Mrs. Dashwood will recover fully.'"

"What! Is Fanny ill?"

"That is precisely what I asked, my dear. 'Good heavens!' I replied, 'Is Mrs. Dashwood ill?' Then the whole truth emerged. Here is the summary of the matter: Mr. Edward Ferrars—the very young man I used to jest with you about (though, happily, as events prove, there was never anything between us)—appears to have been secretly engaged to my cousin, Lucy, for over a year! There you have it, my dear! Not a soul knew a syllable of this except Nancy. Could you possibly believe such a thing feasible? Their mutual affection is understandable, but for matters to advance to this point without anyone suspecting it! That is the marvel! I never witnessed them together, or I certainly would have discovered it sooner.

Well, this secret was maintained strictly to avoid offending Mrs. Ferrars, leaving neither she, your brother, nor your sister aware of the arrangement. Until this very morning, when poor Nancy—who, you know, is well-intentioned but lacks cunning—let the cat out of the bag. Reasoning that everyone loves Lucy and would approve, she rushed to your sister. Your sister was sitting alone at her embroidery work, unsuspecting of the impending shock; mere moments earlier, she had been telling your brother she intended to arrange a match between Edward and some Lady's daughter. Imagine the blow to her vanity and pride! She immediately collapsed into violent hysterics, screaming loudly enough to be heard by your brother in his study below, where he was contemplating a letter to his country steward. He rushed upstairs, and a dreadful scene ensued. By then, Lucy had arrived, unaware of the turmoil brewing. Poor soul! I pity her immensely. I must add, she was treated very harshly. Your sister scolded like a fury, driving Lucy into a fainting fit. Nancy fell to her knees weeping bitterly, while your brother paced the room, utterly at a loss. Mrs. Dashwood declared they must leave immediately, forcing your brother to kneel and plead for permission to let them pack their belongings until they could depart. Only then did she relapse into hysterics, frightening him so much he called for Mr. Donovan.

Mr. Donovan found the house in chaos. The carriage stood ready at the door as he arrived; poor Lucy was barely able to walk, and Nancy was nearly as ill. I lack patience with your sister, though I sincerely hope this becomes a match despite her objections. Good heavens, what a torment poor Mr. Edward will face upon hearing this! To have his love so scornfully rejected! They say he adores her, as he ought. I should not be surprised if he were furious—Mr. Donovan agrees. We discussed it extensively. Best of all, he has returned to Harley Street to be available when Mrs. Ferrars is informed, for she was summoned immediately after my cousins left. Your sister was certain *she* would also fall into hysterics, and indeed, she likely will. I harbor no pity for either of them. I cannot comprehend people making such a fuss over money and status. There is no earthly reason why Mr. Edward and Lucy cannot marry. I am sure Mrs. Ferrars can provide adequately for her son. Although Lucy possesses little herself, she knows how to maximize resources. If Mrs. Ferrars granted him £500 a year, she could maintain an appearance equal to anyone else with double that sum. Good heavens, how comfortably they might live in a cottage slightly larger than yours, with two maids and two servants! I believe I could even secure a housemaid for them, as my Betty has a sister seeking employment."

Mrs. Jennings paused here. With sufficient time to compose herself, Elinor responded calmly, offering observations that the gravity of the situation warranted. She was relieved not to be suspected of harboring special feelings for Edward—a hope she had nurtured recently—and doubly grateful that Marianne was absent, allowing her to discuss the affair without embarrassment and judge the conduct of all parties with apparent impartiality.

Elinor struggled to define her own expectations of the outcome, though she desperately tried to dismiss the possibility of anything other than the eventual marriage of Edward and Lucy. Curiosity burned within her to hear what Mrs. Ferrars would say and do, though she anticipated the nature of the reaction. More urgently, she sought to understand how Edward would behave. She felt profound compassion for him, very little for Lucy (finding it difficult to muster even that small amount), and none whatsoever for the rest of the group.

Since Mrs. Jennings could converse on no other topic, Elinor quickly realized the necessity of preparing Marianne for the inevitable disclosure. Time was of the essence to reveal the truth, acquaint her with reality, and ensure she could discuss the matter with others without revealing her own anxiety for her sister or her resentment toward Edward.

Elinor's task was painfully difficult. She was about to dismantle what she believed to be her sister's sole solace, expose details of Edward that might permanently tarnish his image in Marianne's eyes, and force Marianne to relive her own disappointment through a striking parallel of circumstances. Though the assignment was unwelcome, it was imperative, and Elinor hastened to fulfill it.

She made no effort to dwell on her own feelings or portray herself as suffering, aside from the stoic self-command she had practiced since learning of Edward's engagement. Her narrative remained clear and simple. While delivered with emotion, it lacked violent agitation or impetuous grief. Such displays belonged to the listener; Marianne listened with horror and wept profusely. Elinor resolved to be a comfort to others amidst her own distress, offering assurance of her mental composure and a fervent defense of Edward against every charge save imprudence.

Yet, for some time, Marianne accepted neither explanation nor comfort. Edward seemed a second Willoughby. Acknowledging, as Elinor did, that she had loved him most sincerely, how could Marianne feel differently? Regarding Lucy Steele, she deemed her so utterly unlovable and incapable of winning a sensible man's heart that she initially refused to believe, and later to forgive, any former affection Edward might have held for her. She could not even accept it as natural, leaving Elinor to rely solely on a deeper knowledge of human nature to eventually convince her.

Elinor's initial communication stated only the fact of the engagement and its duration. Marianne's emotions then overwhelmed her, halting further detail. For a while, the only recourse was to soothe her distress, alleviate her fears, and counteract her resentment. The first question Marianne posed, prompting further specifics, was: "How long has this been known to you, Elinor? Has he written to you?"

"I have known it for four months," Elinor replied. "When Lucy first visited Barton Park last November, she confided in me regarding her engagement."

At these words, astonishment flashed across Marianne's eyes, which her lips could not express. After a pause filled with wonder, she cried out, "Four months! Have you known of this for four months?"

Elinor confirmed it.

"While attending me in all my misery, has this burden weighed on your heart? And I have reproached you for being happy!"

"It was not fitting that you should then know how much I was the opposite!"

"Four months!" Marianne repeated. "So calm! So cheerful! How have you sustained yourself?"

"By believing I was fulfilling my duty. My promise to Lucy obligated me to secrecy. I owed her the avoidance of hints, and I owed my family and friends the prevention of unnecessary anxiety that I could not satisfy."

Marianne appeared deeply moved.

"I have often wished to undeceive you and my mother," Elinor added. "On occasion, I attempted it; but without

Mrs. Jennings spoke warmly of Edward's conduct, yet only Elinor and Marianne truly grasped its worth. They alone knew how scant the temptation had been for him to disobey, and how meager the solace remained to him beyond the simple knowledge that he had acted rightly, given the loss of both friends and fortune. Elinor took pride in his integrity; Marianne, moved by compassion for his suffering, forgave all his past faults. Though the breach of confidence between the sisters was healed by this public revelation, neither wished to dwell upon it during private moments. Elinor avoided the subject on principle, fearing that Marianne's overly warm and absolute assurances of Edward's enduring love would only cement a belief she secretly hoped to dispel. For Marianne, the courage to discuss the topic quickly faded; the inevitable comparison between her own actions and Elinor's left her more dissatisfied with herself than ever. She felt the full weight of this contrast, but not as her sister intended—to spur her to action. Instead, it bred only relentless self-reproach. She bitterly regretted her failure to act sooner, yet this realization brought only the torment of penitence without the promise of improvement. Her spirit was so weakened that she believed further effort impossible, leaving her utterly disheartened.

For a day or two thereafter, no news arrived regarding events in Harley Street or Bartlett's Buildings. Yet, despite knowing so much already, Mrs. Jennings had resolved from the outset to visit her cousins with comfort and inquiry as soon as possible. Only the unusual influx of visitors had delayed her journey. On the third day following their discovery of the truth, the weather proved so fine and beautiful—a rare occurrence in early March—that many flocked to Kensington Gardens. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor were among them. Marianne, however, aware that the Willoughbys had returned to London and harboring a constant dread of encountering them, preferred to stay indoors rather than risk appearing in such a crowded public space.

Shortly after entering the gardens, an intimate friend of Mrs. Jennings joined them. Elinor was relieved that this companion engaged Mrs. Jennings's attention, allowing Elinor a moment of quiet reflection. She saw neither the Willoughbys, nor Edward, nor anyone else who might hold interest for her, serious or cheerful. Eventually, however, she was surprised to be approached by Miss Steele. Although Miss Steele appeared somewhat shy, she expressed immense delight at meeting them. Encouraged by Mrs. Jennings's evident kindness, she temporarily left her own group to join theirs.

Mrs. Jennings immediately whispered to Elinor, "Extract everything from her, my dear. If you ask, she will tell you anything. Besides, I cannot leave Mrs. Clarke." Fortunately for both Mrs. Jennings's curiosity and Elinor's peace of mind, Miss Steele possessed the habit of volunteering information without prompting; otherwise, nothing would have been learned.

"I am so delighted to meet you!" Miss Steele exclaimed, taking Elinor's arm familiarly. "I wanted to see you above all things in the world." Lowering her voice, she added, "I suppose Mrs. Jennings has heard the whole story. Is she angry?"

"Not at all, I believe, with you," Elinor replied.

"That is wonderful. And Lady Middleton—is she angry?"

"I cannot imagine it possible," Elinor said.

"I am monstrously glad of that! Good gracious! I have had such a time of it! I never saw Lucy in such a rage in my entire life. She swore initially she would never trim a new bonnet for me or do anything else for me again so long as she lived. But now she has completely recovered, and we are as good friends as ever. Look, she made me this bow to my hat and inserted the feather last night. Now, *you* are going to laugh at me too. But why shouldn't I wear pink ribbons? I don't care if it is the Doctor's favorite color. I am certain, for my part, I would never have known he preferred it to any other shade if he hadn't happened to mention it. My cousins have been plaguing me endlessly! I declare sometimes I don't know which way to turn before them."

She drifted off onto a subject where Elinor had nothing to contribute, so Elinor soon deemed it prudent to steer the conversation back to its original course.

"Well, but Miss Dashwood," Miss Steele said triumphantly, "people may say whatever they please about Mr. Ferrars declaring he wouldn't have Lucy, but it isn't true—I can tell you that myself. It is quite shameful for such malicious rumors to circulate. Whatever Lucy might think privately, you know, it was no business of others to assume certainty."

"I never heard anything of the sort suggested before, I assure you," Elinor stated.

"Oh, did you not? But it *was* said, I know, quite clearly, by more than one person. Miss Godby told Miss Sparks that no sensible person expected Mr. Ferrars to abandon a woman like Miss Morton, with thirty thousand pounds to her fortune, for Lucy Steele, who had nothing at all. I received this from Miss Sparks myself. Furthermore, my cousin Richard admitted that when it came to the point, he feared Mr. Ferrars would back out. When Edward didn't appear near us for three days, I couldn't make sense of it. I believe in my heart Lucy thought it all lost. We left your brother's house on Wednesday, and we saw nothing of him throughout Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, unsure of his whereabouts. Once Lucy considered writing to him, but her spirits rebelled against that idea. However, this morning he arrived just as we returned from church, and then everything came out. He had been summoned Wednesday to Harley Street, spoken to by his mother and everyone else, and had declared before them all that he loved no one but Lucy and would have no one but Lucy. He was so distressed by these proceedings that as soon as he left his mother's house, he mounted his horse and rode into the countryside, somewhere or other. He stayed at various inns all Thursday and Friday, deliberately trying to recover his composure. After pondering it repeatedly, he remarked that since he had no fortune and nothing at all, it would seem unkind to maintain the engagement, as it would inevitably lead to her ruin. With only two thousand pounds and no hope of more, and considering that if he entered the clergy—as he was contemplating—he could secure nothing but a curacy, how could they survive on that? He couldn't bear the thought of her settling for less, so he begged, if she had the slightest inclination, to end the matter immediately and let him manage his own affairs. I heard him say all this as plainly as possible. It was entirely for *her* sake, and on *her* account, that he mentioned withdrawing, not for his own reasons. I swear on my life he never dropped a syllable about being tired of her, or wishing to marry Miss Morton, or anything similar. But, naturally, Lucy wouldn't listen to such reasoning. She told him directly—with plenty of talk about sweetness and love—you know, and all that—Oh, la! one can't repeat such things, you know—that she had not the slightest desire to break off. She insisted that she could live with him on a trifling amount, however little he might possess, and she would be very glad to accept it all, or something of that nature. Consequently, he was overjoyed and talked for some time about their future plans. They agreed he should take holy orders immediately and wait until he secured a living before marrying. Just then, I couldn't hear anymore, as my cousin called from downstairs to inform me that Mrs. Richardson had arrived in her carriage and would take one of us to Kensington Gardens. I was forced to go upstairs and interrupt them to ask Lucy if she wished to go, but she didn't care to leave Edward. So, I simply ran upstairs, put on a pair of silk stockings, and came down with the Richardsons."

"I do not understand what you mean by 'interrupting them,'" Elinor said. "You were all in the same room together, weren't you?"

"No, indeed, not us. La! Miss Dashwood, do you really think people make love when someone else is present? Oh, for shame! To be sure, you must know better than that. (Laughing affectingly.) No, no; they were locked away in the drawing-room together, and all I heard was merely by listening at the door."

"How!" cried Elinor. "Have you been recounting to me what you learned solely by eavesdropping at the door? I am sorry I wasn't aware beforehand, for I certainly would not have allowed you to share details of a conversation you ought not to have known yourself. How could you behave so unfairly toward your sister?"

"Oh, la! there is nothing in *that*. I simply stood at the door and heard what I could. And I am sure Lucy would have done exactly the same by me. A year or two ago, when Martha Sharpe and I shared so many secrets, she never hesitated to hide in a closet or behind a chimney-piece on purpose to overhear what we said."

Elinor attempted to change the subject, but Miss Steele could not be diverted for more than a couple of minutes from what occupied her mind.

"Edward speaks of going to Oxford soon," she said, "but currently, he is lodging at No.—, Pall Mall. What an ill-natured woman his mother is, isn't she? And your brother and sister weren't very kind! However, I won't say anything against them to *you*; and to be sure,

More than two months had now passed since the Dashwood sisters arrived in London, and Marianne's longing to depart grew daily stronger. She sighed constantly for the fresh air, the freedom, and the peaceful solitude of the countryside, convinced that if any place could bring her true comfort, it must be Barton. Elinor shared her anxiety for their departure, though hers was tempered by a practical awareness of the immense difficulties involved in such a long journey—a reality Marianne refused to acknowledge. Yet, Elinor began to seriously consider the logistics of their return and had already voiced their desires to their generous hostess. Lady Middleton had resisted with all the warmth of her goodwill, until a new proposal emerged.

Mrs. Palmer intended to move to Cleveland near the end of March for the Easter holidays. She extended a very enthusiastic invitation to Mrs. Jennings, along with her two guests, to accompany them. While this alone might not have satisfied Elinor's refined sensibilities, the invitation was delivered with such genuine politeness by Mr. Palmer himself—and coupled with the marked improvement in his demeanor toward them since learning of his wife's unhappiness—it compelled Elinor to accept with pleasure.

When she revealed her decision to Marianne, however, the younger sister reacted with immediate distress. "Cleveland!" she cried, agitated. "No, I cannot go to Cleveland."

"You forget," Elinor said gently, "that its location is not..."

"But it is in Somersetshire," Marianne interrupted. "I simply cannot go into Somersetshire. There, where I once hoped to travel... No, Elinor, you cannot expect me to go there."

Elinor saw no point in arguing against the propriety of suppressing such strong feelings. Instead, she sought to counteract them by appealing to other sentiments. She framed the trip as a way to secure a timely and comfortable reunion with their beloved mother, whose presence they both cherished dearly. From Cleveland, merely a few miles from Bristol, the journey to Barton would take no more than a single day, albeit a long one. Their mother's servant could easily meet them halfway, and since they would likely stay at Cleveland for no more than a week, they could be back home in under three weeks. Given Marianne's deep love for her mother, Elinor reasoned, this practical benefit should outweigh her imagined fears.

Indeed, Marianne's affection proved powerful. Furthermore, Mrs. Jennings was far from tired of her guests; she pressed them earnestly to return with her from Cleveland. Though Elinor appreciated the kindness, her plans remained unchanged. With their mother's agreement quickly secured, arrangements for their departure were finalized. Finding solace in the countdown, Marianne drafted a list detailing exactly how many hours separated them from Barton.

"Ach, Colonel," Mrs. Jennings exclaimed as he visited shortly after settling their departure, "what shall you and I do without the Miss Dashwoods? They are absolutely determined to leave the Palmers' house. And how desolate we shall be when I return! Lord, we shall sit staring at one another as dull as two cats."

Perhaps Mrs. Jennings hoped this vivid description of their impending boredom would prompt the Colonel to make an offer, thereby saving himself from such loneliness. If so, she soon realized her hope was justified. As Elinor moved to the window to measure a print she intended to copy for a friend, the Colonel followed her, speaking with a distinct intensity. The effect on the ladies was unmistakable. Though Mrs. Jennings, bound by honor, shifted her chair specifically to avoid overhearing, she could not help noticing that Elinor flushed, her face betraying agitation, and became completely absorbed in the Colonel's words, abandoning her task.

Later, during a pause in Marianne's piano practice, fragments of the conversation drifted to Mrs. Jennings' ears. The Colonel seemed to be apologizing for the poor quality of his house. This confirmed her suspicions. She marveled that he would deem such an apology necessary, assuming it was mere social etiquette. Unable to hear Elinor's response clearly, she guessed from the movement of her lips that the Colonel's comment was not a significant obstacle. Mrs. Jennings silently praised Elinor for her honesty.

They continued talking for a few moments without further interception, until another break in Marianne's playing allowed the Colonel's calm voice to reach her: "I am afraid it cannot take place very soon."

Startled and shocked by what sounded like a rejection, Mrs. Jennings nearly shouted, "Good heavens, what could possibly prevent it?" but checked herself, offering only a silent gasp. "This is peculiar! Surely he doesn't need to wait until he is older."

However, this apparent hesitation did not seem to wound or dishearten his companion. Shortly after the conversation ended, as they parted ways, Mrs. Jennings distinctly heard Elinor say, with a tone revealing her own feelings, "I shall always feel deeply indebted to you."

Delighted by this expression of gratitude, Mrs. Jennings only wondered why the Colonel could then bid them farewell with such perfect composure (*sang-froid*), departing without a single reply. She had never suspected her old friend could be such an indifferent suitor.

What actually transpired between them was quite different.

"I have heard," the Colonel said with profound sympathy, "of the injustice your friend Mr. Ferrars has suffered at the hands of his family. If I understand correctly, he has been entirely rejected by them for persisting in his engagement to a truly deserving young woman. Am I correct? Is this true?"

Elinor confirmed it.

"The cruelty, the politically disastrous cruelty," the Colonel replied with deep emotion, "of separating, or attempting to separate, two young people deeply devoted to one another is terrible. Mrs. Ferrars does not realize what she is doing—or what she may be driving her son to. I have met Mr. Ferrars a couple of times in Harley Street and am greatly pleased with him. He is not a man with whom one becomes intimately acquainted quickly, but I have seen enough to wish him well for his own sake, and as a friend of yours, I wish it even more. I understand he intends to take holy orders. Would you be so kind as to inform him that the living of Delaford, which has just become vacant according to today's post, is offered to him, should he deem it worthy of acceptance? However, given his unfortunate circumstances, it might seem foolish to suggest it without reservation; I only wish it were more substantial. It is a rectory, but a small one—the late incumbent earned no more than £200 per annum. While it is certainly capable of improvement, I fear not to the extent required to provide a truly comfortable income. Nevertheless, my pleasure in presenting it to him is immense. Please assure him of this."

Elinor's astonishment at this commission was scarcely less than if the Colonel had proposed marriage. The position, which she had considered hopeless for Edward just two days prior, was suddenly provided to facilitate his marriage—and *she*, of all people, was chosen to deliver it! Her emotions were intense, though Mrs. Jennings interpreted them as arising from a different cause. Regardless of any lesser, less noble feelings that might have contributed to her reaction, her admiration for the Colonel's general benevolence and her gratitude for his specific friendship were profoundly felt and warmly expressed.

She thanked him wholeheartedly, spoke highly of Edward's character and principles knowing them to be deserving, and promised to undertake the mission with pleasure if he truly wished to delegate such a pleasant duty to someone else. At the same time, she couldn't help thinking that no one could perform it better than he. It was an errand from which she would gladly have been spared, unwilling to cause Edward pain by accepting an obligation from her personally. Yet, the Colonel, motivated by similar delicacy, declined to handle it directly, appearing so eager for her to convey it that she could not oppose him further.

Edward, she believed, was still in town, and fortunately, she had learned his address from Miss Steele. She could therefore inform him of the news later that day.

Once this was settled, the Colonel turned the conversation to his own gain in securing such a respectable and agreeable neighbor. It was then, as Mrs. Jennings had anticipated, that he expressed regret regarding the smallness and mediocrity of the house. Elinor, as expected, dismissed this concern lightly, particularly regarding its size.

"The smallness of the house," she remarked, "I cannot imagine any inconvenience for them, as it will suit their family size and income perfectly."

To the Colonel's surprise, this indicated that she viewed Mr. Ferrars's marriage as a certainty resulting from the presentation. He did not believe the Delaford living could generate an income sufficient for anyone of his station to consider marrying on, and he stated as much.

"This little rectory," he explained, "can do no more than make Mr. Ferrars comfortable as a bachelor; it cannot enable him to marry. I am sorry to say that my patronage ends here; my influence is hardly more extensive. Should an unforeseen opportunity arise in the future to assist him further, I must view him very differently from how I do now if I am not equally ready to be helpful then as I sincerely wish to be now. What I am doing today seems insignificant compared to what must be his primary, and only, goal: happiness. His marriage must remain a distant prospect; at least, I fear it cannot happen very soon."

Such was the remark that, when misunderstood, so rightfully offended the sensitive feelings of Mrs. Jennings. However, after recounting the true nature of the conversation between Colonel Brandon and Elinor while standing by the window, the gratitude expressed by the latter upon their parting

"Well, Miss Dashwood," said Mrs. Jennings, flashing a sagacious smile the moment the gentleman had departed, "I needn't ask you what the Colonel has been saying to you. Though, upon my honor, I *tried* to stay out of earshot, I couldn't help catching enough to understand the nature of his business. And I assure you, I have never been happier in my life; I wish you joy of it with all my heart!"

"Thank you, ma'am," replied Elinor. "It *is* a matter of great joy to me, and I feel the goodness of Colonel Brandon most acutely. There are not many men who would act as he has. Few possess such a compassionate heart! I have never been more astonished in my life."

"Lord! My dear, you are very modest. I am not the least astonished in the world; indeed, I have often thought lately that nothing was more likely to happen."

"You judged from your knowledge of the Colonel's general benevolence; but at least you could not foresee that the opportunity would arise so very soon."

"Opportunity!" repeated Mrs. Jennings. "Oh! As to that, when a man has once made up his mind to such a thing, somehow or other he will soon find an opportunity. Well, my dear, I wish you joy of it again and again. If ever there was a happy couple in the world, I think I shall soon know exactly where to look for them."

"You mean to go to Delaford after them, I suppose?" said Elinor, with a faint smile.

"Aye, my dear, that I do, indeed. And as to the house being a bad one, I do not know what the Colonel would be at; it is as good a house as ever I saw."

"He spoke of its being out of repair."

"Well, and whose fault is that? Why doesn't he repair it? Who should do it but himself?"

Their conversation was interrupted by the servant entering to announce that the carriage was at the door. Mrs. Jennings immediately prepared to depart, remarking, "Well, my dear, I must be gone before I have had half my talk out. However, we may have it all over again in the evening, for we shall be quite alone. I do not ask you to come with me, for I dare say your mind is too full of the matter to care for company; and besides, you must long to tell your sister all about it."

Marianne had already left the room before the conversation began.

"Certainly, ma'am, I shall tell Marianne of it; but I shall not mention it to anyone else at present."

"Oh! Very well," said Mrs. Jennings, looking somewhat disappointed. "Then you wouldn't have me tell it to Lucy, for I intend to go as far as Holborn today?"

"No, ma'am, not even Lucy, if you please. One day's delay will not be very material; and until I have written to Mr. Ferrars, I think it ought not to be mentioned to anyone else. I shall do *that* directly. It is of importance that no time should be lost with him, for he will, of course, have much to attend to regarding his ordination."

This statement initially puzzled Mrs. Jennings exceedingly. She could not immediately comprehend why Mr. Ferrars was to be written to in such haste. After a few moments' reflection, however, a very happy idea struck her, and she exclaimed, "Oh, ho!—I understand you. Mr. Ferrars is to be the man. Well, so much the better for him. Ay, to be sure, he must be ordained in readiness; and I am very glad to find things are so forward between you. But, my dear, is this not rather out of character? Should not the Colonel write himself? Surely, he is the proper person."

Elinor did not quite grasp the beginning of Mrs. Jennings's speech, nor did she deem it worth inquiring into; consequently, she replied only to its conclusion.

"Colonel Brandon is so delicate a man that he rather wished someone else to announce his intentions to Mr. Ferrars than himself."

"And so *you* are forced to do it. Well, *that* is an odd kind of delicacy! However, I will not disturb you (seeing her prepare to write). You know your own concerns best. So goodbye, my dear. I haven't heard of anything to please me so well since Charlotte was brought to bed."

She departed, only to return a moment later. "I have just been thinking of Betty's sister, my dear. I should be very glad to secure her as a mistress. But whether she would do for a lady's maid, I am sure I can't tell. She is an excellent housemaid and works very well at her needle. However, you will think of all that at your leisure."

"Certainly, ma'am," replied Elinor, listening to little of what she said and feeling more anxious to be alone than to master the subject. How she should begin—how she should express herself in her note to Edward—was now her sole concern. The particular circumstances surrounding them made this task difficult, whereas for any other person it would have been the easiest thing in the world. Yet, she equally feared saying too much or too little, sitting deliberatively over her paper with the pen in her hand until she was interrupted by the entrance of Edward himself.

He had met Mrs. Jennings at the door on her way to the carriage as he came to leave his farewell card. She, after apologizing for not returning herself, had insisted that he enter by stating that Miss Dashwood was upstairs and wished to speak with him on very particular business.

Elinor had just been congratulating herself, amidst her perplexity, that however difficult it might be to express herself properly by letter, it was at least preferable to delivering the information verbally, when her visitor entered, forcing her into this greatest exertion of all. Her astonishment and confusion were profound upon his sudden appearance. She had not seen him since his engagement became public, and thus not since he knew she was aware of it; combined with the consciousness of what she had been thinking and what she had to tell him, this made her feel particularly uncomfortable for several minutes. He, too, was deeply distressed. They sat down together in a state of promising embarrassment. Whether he had asked her pardon for his intrusion upon first entering the room, he could not recall; but deciding to err on the side of caution, he offered a formal apology as soon as he could speak, after taking a seat.

"Mrs. Jennings told me," he said, "that you wished to speak with me, at least I understood her so—or I certainly should not have intruded in such a manner; though at the same time, I should have been extremely sorry to leave London without seeing you and your sister, especially as it will most likely be some time—it is not probable that I shall soon have the pleasure of meeting you again. I go to Oxford tomorrow."

"You would not have gone, however," said Elinor, recovering herself and determined to overcome what she so dreaded as quickly as possible, "without receiving our good wishes, even if we had not been able to give them in person. Mrs. Jennings was quite right in what she said. I have something of consequence to inform you of, which I was on the point of communicating by paper. I am charged with a most agreeable office..." (she breathed slightly faster than usual as she spoke) "...Colonel Brandon, who was here only ten minutes ago, has desired me to say that, understanding you mean to take orders, he has great pleasure in offering you the living of Delaford, now just vacant, and only wishes it were more valuable. Allow me to congratulate you on having so respectable and well-judging a friend, and to join in his wish that the living—it is about two hundred a year—were much more considerable, and such as might better enable you to... as might be more than a temporary accommodation to yourself—such, in short, as might establish all your views of happiness."

What Edward felt, as he could not say it himself, it cannot be expected that anyone else should articulate for him. He looked with all the astonishment that such unexpected, such unthought-of information could not fail to excite; but he said only these two words: "Colonel Brandon!"

"Yes," continued Elinor, gathering more resolution as some of the worst was over, "Colonel Brandon means it as a testimony of his concern for what has lately passed—for the cruel situation in which the unjustifiable conduct of your family has placed you—a concern which I am sure Marianne, myself, and all your friends, must share; and likewise as a proof of his high esteem for your general character, and his particular approbation of your behavior on the present occasion."

"Colonel Brandon gives *me* a living!—Can it be possible?"

"The unkindness of your own relations has made you astonished to find friendship anywhere."

"No," replied he, with sudden consciousness, "not to find it in *you*; for I cannot be ignorant that to you, to your goodness, I owe it all.—I feel it—I would express it if I could—but, as you well know, I am no orator."

"You are very much mistaken. I do assure you that you owe it entirely, at least almost entirely, to your own merit, and Colonel Brandon's discernment of it. I have had no hand in it. I did not even know, till I understood his design, that the living was vacant; nor had it ever occurred to

Edward, buoyed by his gratitude toward Colonel Brandon, hastened to share his joy with Lucy. By the time he arrived at Bartlett's Buildings, his elation was so profound that Lucy could confidently report to Mrs. Jennings, who visited the following day with her warmest congratulations, that she had never witnessed him in such high spirits. Lucy's own happiness was equally assured; she eagerly joined Mrs. Jennings in anticipating a comfortable reunion with the entire party at Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas. Far from hesitating to acknowledge the debt owed to Elinor, Lucy praised their mutual friendship with heartfelt warmth, readily admitting their obligations to her. She declared openly that no effort undertaken by Miss Dashwood for their benefit, past or future, would ever surprise her, for she believed Elinor capable of anything for those she truly cherished. Regarding Colonel Brandon, Lucy went so far as to venerate him as a saint, earnestly wishing he were treated as such in all worldly matters. She anxiously desired his tithes be maximized and secretly resolved to make full use of his servants, carriage, cattle, and poultry whenever she resided at Delaford.

It had been over a week since John Dashwood visited Berkeley Street, and during that interval, the Dashwoods had shown little regard for his wife's illness save for a single verbal inquiry. Consequently, Elinor felt compelled to pay her a visit. However, this errand clashed with her inclinations and lacked support from her companions. Marianne, not only refusing to accompany her sister but actively urging her against the trip, proved unhelpful. Mrs. Jennings, despite offering her carriage freely, harbored such a strong aversion to Mrs. John Dashwood that neither her curiosity regarding the recent revelations nor her desire to champion Edward's cause could overcome her reluctance to endure another encounter with the woman. Thus, Elinor departed alone to face a visit she held little desire for, risking a private confrontation with a woman whom none of the others wished to tolerate.

Upon arrival, Mrs. Dashwood was initially absent. Before the carriage could depart, Mr. Dashwood emerged unexpectedly. He expressed delight at meeting Elinor, claiming he had intended to visit Berkeley Street himself. Assuring her that Fanny would welcome her presence, he invited her inside. They ascended to the drawing-room, where no one was present. "Fanny is in her own room, I suppose," Mr. Dashwood remarked. "I shall go to her shortly; I am certain she will have no objection to seeing you. Indeed, she wouldn't. Especially now, though you and Marianne were always favorites. Why didn't Marianne come?" Elinor offered excuses for her sister's absence. "I am not sorry to see you alone," Mr. Dashwood replied, "for I have much to discuss. This living of Colonel Brandon's—is it true? Did he actually grant it to Edward? I heard rumors yesterday and came specifically to inquire further."

"It is perfectly true," Elinor confirmed. "Colonel Brandon has presented the rectory of Delaford to Edward."
"Truly!" Mr. Dashwood exclaimed, feigning astonishment. "No relation, no connection whatsoever! And considering the current value of livings, what was the worth of this position?"
"Approximately two hundred pounds per annum."
"Well then," he mused, "if the previous incumbent were elderly and infirm, likely to resign soon, one could secure fourteen hundred pounds for the right of presentation. How did he fail to settle this prior to the donor's death? Now, it would be too late to sell. Yet, a man of Colonel Brandon's discernment! I marvel at his imprudence regarding such a fundamental matter. Well, I am convinced there is inconsistency in almost every human character. Perhaps, upon reflection, the situation is this: Edward holds the living merely until the Colonel's actual buyer reaches maturity."
Elinor firmly contradicted this theory. Having personally conveyed the offer from Colonel Brandon to Edward, she possessed definitive knowledge of the terms, compelling Mr. Dashwood to accept her correction.
"It is truly astonishing!" he cried. "What could be the Colonel's motive?"
"A very simple one: to assist Mr. Ferrars."
"Well, well; regardless of Colonel Brandon's nature, Edward is a fortunate man." He lowered his voice, treating the subject with grave importance. "Do not mention this to Fanny. Although I have broken the news to her and she bears it remarkably well, she dislikes hearing it discussed repeatedly. Mrs. Ferrars knows nothing of it currently, and it is best to conceal it entirely until the marriage occurs. Then, I fear, she must learn everything."
"But why such caution? While it is unlikely Mrs. Ferrars finds joy in her son's financial security, surely she feels nothing given her recent behavior? She cast him aside forever and influenced others to reject him as well. After such actions, she cannot be expected to feel sorrow or joy concerning him. She would not be foolish enough to discard parental comfort while retaining parental anxiety!"
"Oh, Elinor," Mr. Dashwood interjected gently, "your reasoning is sound, but it stems from ignorance of human nature. When Edward's unfortunate marriage happens, rest assured his mother will feel exactly as if she had never rejected him. Therefore, every detail accelerating this calamitous event must be hidden from her. Mrs. Ferrars can never forget that Edward is her son."
"You surprise me; I would think her memory of this must have faded by now."
"You underestimate her excessively. Mrs. Ferrars is one of the most affectionate mothers in the world." Elinor remained silent.
"We are now considering," Mr. Dashwood continued after a pause, placing his hand kindly on hers in a hushed, solemn tone, "Robert's marriage to Miss Morton."
Elinor, amused by the gravity in her brother's demeanor, calmly responded, "I suppose the lady has no choice in the matter."
"Choice? What do you mean?"
"I merely imply that, judging by your tone, it seems indifferent to Miss Morton whether she marries Edward or Robert."
"Certainly, there is no difference. Robert is now effectively the elder son. As for other qualities, they are both pleasant young men; I know not which surpasses the other." Elinor said no more, and John fell silent briefly. His thoughts concluded thus: "On one thing, my dear sister, I can assure you—and I shall, for I know it will please you. From the best authority available, I have learned that while there may have been objections to a certain connection, it would have caused Mrs. Ferrars far less vexation than this current arrangement. 'It would have been incomparably preferable,' she reportedly stated, 'the lesser of two evils.' Nevertheless, that is irrelevant; such attachments are impossible. Still, I deemed it proper to inform you, knowing how much it would gratify you. Do not regret your situation, my dear Elinor. There is no doubt you fare exceedingly well—perhaps better, all things considered." He paused, then asked, "Has Colonel Brandon visited you recently?"

Elinor had absorbed enough information to stir her nerves and inflate her self-regard, finding relief in avoiding further conversation when Mr. Robert Ferrars entered. After a brief exchange, John Dashwood, remembering Fanny's unawareness of Elinor's presence, excused himself to seek her out. Left alone with Robert, Elinor observed how the younger Ferrars cultivated an air of cheerful indifference, deriving self-satisfaction from an unequal distribution of his mother's favor and generosity. This attitude, earned solely through his dissolute lifestyle in contrast to his banished brother's integrity, reinforced Elinor's unfavorable judgment of his intellect and character.

Scarcely two minutes passed before Robert turned the conversation to Edward, eager to discuss the newly acquired living. Elinor recounted the details as she had shared them with John. The impact on Robert, though distinct, was equally pronounced. He erupted into uncontrollable laughter. The notion of Edward becoming a clergyman, residing in a modest parsonage, amused him immensely. Adding to this the whimsical image of Edward reading prayers in a white surplice and announcing the banns for ordinary couples like John Smith and Mary Brown, he found the prospect utterly ridiculous. Elinor, maintaining a stoic silence, watched him with a gaze brimming with contempt. Fortunately, this expression served only to alleviate her own feelings without revealing her disdain.

Robert was brought back from levity to seriousness not by Elinor's rebuke, but by his own sudden sensitivity. "We may treat it as a jest," he finally said, recovering from his prolonged, forced laughter, "but, upon my soul, it is a grave matter. Poor Edward! He is ruined forever. I am deeply sorry; he is a good-hearted creature, perhaps the most well-meaning fellow in the world. Do not judge him lightly based on our limited acquaintance. His manners are certainly not the most engaging. We are not all born with equal talents or social grace. It was pitiable to see him among strangers, yet I swear I never felt so shocked in my life as when the truth revealed itself. I could hardly believe it. My mother was the first to tell me, and feeling compelled to act decisively, I immediately declared, 'Madam, whatever you intend, I must state that if Edward marries this young woman, I shall never see him again.' I was profoundly shocked! Poor Edward! He has sealed his fate, shutting himself out from decent society. Yet, as I told my mother, I am unsurprised; given his upbringing, it was inevitable. My poor mother was half-frantic."

"Have you ever met the lady?"
"Yes

The final social engagement of their time in town was a brief visit to Harley Street, where Elinor accepted her brother's congratulations on their successful journey toward Barton without financial strain, and learned that Colonel Brandon intended to accompany them to Cleveland within the next couple of days. This concluded the formal interactions between the siblings in London. What followed were only faint invitations: Fanny suggested they drop by Norland if their path ever crossed it—a prospect deemed highly improbable—and John offered Elinor a warmer, though private, promise of his own swift arrival at Delaford. These were the sole hints of future reunions in the countryside. It was amusing to Elinor how everyone seemed determined to send her to Delaford; yet, of all places, it was the one she least wished to visit or inhabit. Not only did her brother and Mrs. Jennings regard it as her destined home, but even Lucy, upon their parting, pressed an urgent invitation for her to stay there.

Very early in April, the two groups departed from their respective residences in Hanover Square and Berkeley Street, agreeing to meet en route. To accommodate Charlotte and her infant, the journey would take more than two days, while Mr. Palmer, traveling faster alongside Colonel Brandon, planned to join them shortly after their arrival at Cleveland. Marianne, having spent so few comfortable hours in London and longing so desperately to escape it, nonetheless felt profound pain bidding farewell to the house where she had last harbored her hopes and trust in Willoughby—hopes and trust now extinguished forever. She also struggled to leave the city where Willoughby remained, busy with new engagements and schemes from which she was excluded, without weeping. Conversely, Elinor's relief at leaving was absolute. She held no lingering attachments that caused regret, parted from no one she loved, and welcomed freedom from the suffocating pressure of Lucy's friendship. Grateful for the chance to depart unseen by Willoughby since his marriage, she looked forward with hope to the tranquility of Barton, confident it would restore Marianne's peace and solidify her own.

Their journey proceeded smoothly. On the second day, they entered Somerset, a county alternately cherished and dreaded in Marianne's imagination. By the morning of the third, they reached Cleveland. It was a spacious, modern estate set upon a sloping lawn, lacking a park but boasting extensive pleasure grounds. A smooth gravel road wound around a plantation, leading to the front door, while the lawn was scattered with timber. The house itself was shielded by a dense screen of firs, mountain-ashes, and acacias, interspersed with towering Lombardy poplars that hid the service buildings.

Marianne entered the house with a heart swelling with emotion, knowing she was barely eighty miles from Barton and less than thirty from Combe Magna. Yet, within five minutes of stepping inside, while the others assisted Charlotte in settling her child with the housekeeper, she slipped away. She wandered through the winding shrubberies, which were just beginning to display their spring beauty, seeking a distant hilltop. From a nearby Grecian temple, her gaze swept across the vast landscape to the southeast, resting fondly on the farthest ridges of the horizon, imagining that from those peaks, Combe Magna might be visible. In these moments of exquisite misery, she rejoiced in tears to be at Cleveland. Returning via a different path, savoring the luxury of solitary country freedom, she resolved to spend nearly every hour of her stay exploring the grounds alone.

She returned just in time to join the group for an excursion through the immediate grounds. The rest of the morning passed leisurely as they toured the kitchen garden, admiring the blooms on the walls, listening to the gardener's complaints about blight, dawdled through the greenhouse (where Charlotte laughed at the loss of favorite plants damaged by lingering frost), and visited the poultry yard. Here, the dairy-maid's disappointments—hens abandoning nests, thefts by foxes, or dwindling broods—provided fresh amusement.

The morning had been fine and dry, and Marianne had not anticipated bad weather. Consequently, she was surprised to find herself confined indoors after dinner due to a persistent downpour. She had hoped for a twilight stroll to the temple and throughout the grounds; a merely cold or damp evening might not have deterred her, but a heavy, settled rain was unbearable to her. With a small party and quiet hours passing, Mrs. Palmer attended to her child, and Mrs. Jennings worked on her carpet. They discussed their departed friends, arranged Lady Middleton's schedule, and speculated whether Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon would reach Reading that night. Elinor, though largely unconcerned, joined the conversation. Meanwhile, Marianne, possessing an uncanny ability to locate the library regardless of the family's efforts to avoid it, quickly secured a book.

Mrs. Palmer displayed constant, friendly goodwill, making her guests feel welcome. Her lack of refinement and occasional lapses in polite formality were more than compensated for by her open-heartedness and engaging kindness, highlighted by her lovely face. While her folly was evident, it was not repulsive because it lacked arrogance. Elinor could forgive everything about her save her laugh.

The following day, the two gentlemen arrived for a late dinner, delightfully expanding the party and enriching the conversation, which had grown dull from the prolonged morning rain. Elinor, having observed so little of Mr. Palmer previously, wondered what to expect in his domestic setting. She found him perfectly gentlemanly with visitors, though occasionally rough with his wife and mother-in-law. He was a pleasant companion, hindered only by a tendency to believe himself superior to everyone else, particularly to Mrs. Jennings and Charlotte. Otherwise, his character showed nothing unusual for his age and station: fastidious about food, irregular with his hours, affectionate toward his child despite pretending otherwise, and wasting mornings playing billiards instead of working. Overall, she liked him far better than expected, and inwardly she was glad she could not love him further. Observing his epicureanism, selfishness, and conceit only strengthened her contentment in remembering Edward's generosity, simple tastes, and modest nature.

Colonel Brandon, who had recently visited Dorsetshire, brought news regarding Edward. Treating Elinor both as Mr. Ferrars' impartial friend and his own trusted confidante, he spoke extensively about the rectory at Delaford, detailing its shortcomings and outlining his plans to remedy them. His behavior—his evident joy in seeing her after only ten days apart, his eagerness to converse, and his deference to her opinions—seemed to validate Mrs. Jennings's belief in his attachment. Indeed, it would have been sufficient proof had Elinor not already suspected Marianne was his true favorite. Nevertheless, such a notion rarely occurred to her unless prompted by Mrs. Jennings. Confident in her own keen observation, she scrutinized his eyes while Mrs. Jennings focused solely on his actions. When Mrs. Jennings failed to notice Colonel Brandon's look of anxious concern for Marianne's unspoken headache and sore throat—the onset of a severe cold—Elinor saw clearly the quick, alarmed feelings of a lover.

Two delightful twilight walks on the third and fourth evenings, traversing not just the dry gravel paths but the entire grounds, especially the wilder sections with ancient trees and long, wet grass, combined with the imprudence of sitting in wet shoes and stockings, inflicted a violent cold upon Marianne. Though initially dismissed or denied, the illness inevitably worsened, drawing the attention of everyone present and Marianne herself. Prescriptions flooded in from all sides, as is customary, and were uniformly refused. Despite being heavy, feverish, plagued by limb pains, a cough, and a sore throat, she insisted that a single good night's sleep would cure her completely. Only with difficulty did Elinor persuade her to try one or two of the simplest remedies before retiring.

The next morning, Marianne rose at her accustomed hour. When asked how she felt, she insisted she was recovering, attempting to prove it by resuming her usual activities. Yet, a day spent shivering beside the fire, clutching a book she could not read, or lying listlessly upon a sofa, offered little evidence of improvement. By the time she finally retreated to bed, increasingly indisposed, Colonel Brandon was merely astonished by her sister's composure. Elinor, despite tending to Marianne all day against her wishes and forcing medicine upon her at night, shared Marianne's faith in the healing power of sleep and felt no genuine alarm.

However, a restless, feverish night dashed their expectations. When Marianne, after stubbornly insisting on rising, eventually confessed her inability to sit up and returned willingly to bed, Elinor readily accepted Mrs. Jennings's suggestion to summon the Palmers' apothecary. Mr. Harris arrived, examined the patient, and while encouraging Miss Dashwood to anticipate a swift recovery within a few days, his diagnosis carried a darker tone. He described the disorder as having a "putrid tendency" and casually uttered the word "infection." This single phrase sent Mrs. Palmer into an instant panic regarding her infant. Mrs. Jennings, who had suspected from the outset that Marianne's condition was more serious than Elinor realized, grew grave upon hearing Mr. Harris's report. Confirming Charlotte's fears, she urgently advocated for the immediate removal of the child and her mother. Although Mr. Palmer dismissed their anxieties as unfounded, he found his wife's anxiety and persistence insurmountable. Consequently, her departure was arranged. Within an hour of Mr. Harris's arrival, she departed with her son and nurse for the home of a relative living several miles outside Bath. Mr. Palmer promised to follow shortly, as urged by his wife, and she pressed her mother just as fervently to accompany them.

Mrs. Jennings, however, declared with heartfelt kindness that she would not stir from Cleveland so long as Marianne remained ill. Determined to replace the absent mother through her own attentive care, she proved to be a most willing and active helper to Elinor, eager to share the burden and offering valuable nursing experience. Poor Marianne, drained by the severity of her malady and feeling universally unwell, could no longer hope for recovery by the next day. The thought of what that day would have brought without this illness—the start of their journey home, accompanied by a servant, surprising their mother the following morning—made every ailment seem more acute. Her few words were filled with lamentation over this unavoidable delay, though Elinor strove to lift her spirits, genuinely believing at the time that the postponement would be brief.

The following day brought little change; Marianne was neither better nor seemingly worse. The household was further diminished when Mr. Palmer, though reluctant due to his natural goodness and dislike of appearing cowardly, was finally persuaded by Colonel Brandon to fulfill his promise. As he prepared to depart, Colonel Brandon himself began to speak of leaving as well. Here, Mrs. Jennings intervened with perfect tact. She argued that sending the Colonel away while his heart was so troubled by concern for his sister would deprive them both of comfort. Urging him to stay, citing her need for his company in playing piquet while Miss Dashwood cared for Marianne, she won him over. Gratified by the opportunity to remain, Colonel Brandon could not long feign reluctance, especially as Mr. Palmer also expressed relief at having someone capable of advising Elinor in any emergency. Naturally, Marianne was kept ignorant of these arrangements. She did not realize she had inadvertently caused the owners of Cleveland to depart within a week of their arrival. Seeing nothing of Mrs. Palmer caused her no surprise, nor did it trouble her enough to mention her name.

Two days passed since Mr. Palmer's departure, and Marianne's condition remained largely unchanged. Mr. Harris, attending daily, still spoke confidently of a speedy recovery, and Elinor matched his optimism, even writing lighter accounts to her mother than her friend had suggested. However, the day ended less auspiciously than it began. Toward evening, Marianne grew heavier, more restless, and uncomfortable. Elinor, still hopeful, attributed the shift to the fatigue of sitting up for her bed to be made. Administering the prescribed cordials, she watched with satisfaction as Marianne sank into a slumber, expecting beneficial results. Though not perfectly quiet, the sleep lasted a considerable time. Anxious to witness the outcome, Elinor resolved to stay by her side throughout.

Mrs. Jennings, unaware of the change, retired unusually early. One of the principal nurses was resting in the housekeeper's room, leaving Elinor alone with Marianne. The patient's repose became increasingly disturbed. Watching her constant shifting and hearing the frequent, inarticulate murmurs of distress, Elinor almost wished to wake her from such painful dreams until Marianne was suddenly startled awake by a noise in the house. Rising hastily with feverish wildness, she cried out, "Is Mama coming?"

"Not yet," Elinor answered, concealing her own terror and helping Marianne lie down again. "But she will be here, I hope, before long. It is a great distance from here to Barton."

"But she must not go round by London!" Marianne exclaimed in the same hurried tone. "I shall never see her if she goes by London."

Elinor perceived with alarm that Marianne was not herself. While attempting to soothe her, she eagerly checked her pulse. It was lower and quicker than ever. Marianne's frantic talk of her mother caused Elinor's alarm to escalate so rapidly that she decided instantly to send for Mr. Harris and dispatch a messenger to Barton for her mother. Consulting Colonel Brandon on the best method to achieve this was the immediate next step. Having rung for the maid to replace her, Elinor hastened downstairs to the drawing-room, where she knew he usually stayed later. There was no time for hesitation. Her fears and difficulties were laid bare before him. Lacking the courage or confidence to dispel her fears, he listened in silent despondence; yet her practical difficulties were instantly solved. With a readiness that seemed pre-arranged, he offered himself as the messenger to fetch Mrs. Dashwood.

Elinor made no resistance that was not easily overcome. Thanking him with brief, fervent gratitude, she watched as he hurried off his servant with orders for post-horses. Meanwhile, she wrote a few lines to her mother. How gratefully she felt the comfort of such a friend at that critical moment! A companion whose judgment would guide, whose presence would relieve, and whose friendship would soothe. Whatever he felt internally, Colonel Brandon acted with the firmness of a collected mind, arranging everything with despatch and calculating exactly when she might expect his return. No moment was wasted. The horses arrived sooner than anticipated. After pressing her hand with a look of solemnity and speaking words too low for her to hear, he hurried into the carriage.

It was nearly twelve o'clock. Elinor returned to Marianne's room to await the apothecary and watch through the remaining hours. It was a night of nearly equal suffering for both: sleepless pain and delirium for Marianne, and cruel anxiety for Elinor, before Mr. Harris appeared. Once her apprehensions were raised, they demanded payment for all her earlier complacency. The servant who sat with her, refusing to disturb Mrs. Jennings, only tormented her more by hinting at what her mistress had always feared. Marianne's thoughts remained fixated incoherently on her mother; every mention of her name pierced Elinor's heart. Reproaching herself for trifling with days of illness and desperate for relief, Elinor feared that all efforts might be in vain, that everything had been delayed too long, and imagined her suffering mother arriving too late to see her daughter alive or sane.

On the verge of calling Mr. Harris again—or seeking other counsel if he could not come—he finally arrived, albeit not until after five o'clock. His opinion offered some solace for his tardiness. Acknowledging an unexpected and unpleasant alteration in his patient, he refused to deem the danger material, speaking confidently of the relief a new treatment would bring. He promised to return in three or four hours, leaving both patient and attendant somewhat more composed than he had found them.

In the morning, Mrs. Jennings heard the account with deep concern. Her former apprehensions, now justified, left her no doubt of the gravity of the situation. Though she tried to offer comfort to Elinor, her conviction of Marianne's peril prevented her from expressing false hope. Her heart truly grieved at the prospect of such a young, lovely girl fading away quickly. Beyond professional duty, she felt personal sorrow; Marianne had been her companion for three months, under her care, and known to have suffered greatly. Furthermore, considering that Marianne might be to Mrs. Dashwood what Charlotte was to herself, her sympathy for Elinor's distress was profound.

Mr. Harris was punctual for his second visit, only to disappoint his own hopes. His medicines had failed; the fever persisted unabated. Marianne remained in a heavy stupor, quieter but no more herself. Elinor, seizing every sign of fear in his voice, proposed calling in further advice. He deemed it unnecessary, claiming he had one final remedy to try, of which he was as confident as before. His visit concluded with reassuring assurances that reached Elinor's ears but could not penetrate her heart. Calm except when thinking of her mother, she was almost hopeless, continuing in this state until noon, barely moving from her sister's bed. Her thoughts

Elinor recoiled, her face pale with horror, and turned instinctively to flee the room. Her hand was already upon the latch when it halted, frozen by his sudden advance. In a voice that brooked no refusal, bordering on command rather than plea, he demanded, "Miss Dashwood, for half an hour—for ten minutes—I beg you to stay."

"No, sir," she replied, her resolve unshaken. "I shall not stay. Your business cannot concern me. The servants, I presume, failed to inform you that Mr. Palmer is not at home."

"Suppose they had told me," he shouted with intense fervor, "that Mr. Palmer and every relative of his were consigned to hell, it would not have driven me from this threshold. My business is solely with you, and only with you."

"With *me*!" she exclaimed, stunned. "Very well, sir—hasten your speech, and pray, try to be less violent."

"Sit down, and I shall be both prompt and composed."

She hesitated, unsure of her next move. The terrifying possibility that Colonel Brandon might arrive and find her trapped in this confrontation crossed her mind. Yet, having pledged to listen, her curiosity and her sense of duty compelled her to remain. Concluding that prudence demanded speed and that her compliance would facilitate it, she moved silently toward the table and seated herself. He took the chair opposite, and for thirty seconds, neither spoke.

"Hurry, sir," Elinor said impatiently. "I have no time to waste."

He sat in a posture of profound contemplation, seemingly deaf to her urgency. Finally, with abruptness, he addressed her: "Your sister... is out of danger. I learned it from a servant. Praise God!—But is it true? Is it actually true?"

Elinor refused to answer. He pressed the question with renewed desperation. "For heaven's sake, tell me—is she safe, or is she not?"

"We hope she is."

He rose and paced the length of the room. "If I had known this truth half an hour ago—" he continued, returning to his seat with a forced liveliness—"but since I am here, what difference does it make? Once, Miss Dashwood, perhaps the last time, let us share a moment of cheerfulness. I am in a remarkably festive mood. Be honest with me: do you consider me a knave or a fool?"

Elinor stared at him with mounting astonishment. She began to fear he must be intoxicated; the strangeness of his visit and his erratic behavior seemed intelligible only through that lens. Seizing upon this impression, she stood abruptly. "Mr. Willoughby, I advise you to return to Combe immediately. I am not at leisure to remain with you. Whatever your business may be, it will be better recalled and explained tomorrow."

"I understand you," he replied, smiling expressively with a voice perfectly calm. "Yes, I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was sufficient to unsettle me."

"At Marlborough!" cried Elinor, utterly bewildered.

"Yes. I left London this morning at eight. The only ten minutes I have spent outside my carriage since then secured me a luncheon at Marlborough."

The steadiness of his manner and the clarity in his eyes convinced Elinor that, however foolish his presence here might be, it was not caused by intoxication. After a brief pause, she said, "Mr. Willoughby, you *ought* to feel, and I certainly *do*, that after everything that has transpired, your arrival here in this manner, forcing yourself upon my attention, demands a very specific excuse. What is it you intend to convey?"

"I mean," he said with grave intensity, "to reduce your hatred of me by even a single degree. I intend to offer an explanation, an apology for the past; to open my entire heart to you, and by proving that though I have often been a blockhead, I have never been a rascal, to secure some semblance of forgiveness from your sister."

"Is this the true reason for your coming?"

"Upon my soul, it is," he answered, with a warmth that evoked the former Willoughby, compelling her, despite herself, to believe his sincerity.

"If that is all, you may be satisfied already; for Marianne *has*—she has *long* forgiven you."

"Has she?" he cried, in the same eager tone. "Then she forgave me before she ought to have. But she shall forgive me again, and on more reasonable grounds. *Now* will you listen to me?"

Elor nodded her consent.

"I do not know," he said after a pause filled with her anticipation and his reflection, "how *you* may have interpreted my behavior toward your sister, or what diabolical motives you may have attributed to me. Perhaps you will hardly think better of me—but the trial is worth it, and you shall hear everything. When I first became acquainted with your family, I harbored no other intention than to pass my time pleasantly while detained in Devonshire, more enjoyably than ever before. Your sister's lovely appearance and engaging manners pleased me; indeed, her behavior toward me, almost from the start, was of a nature that makes me marvel, reflecting now on what she was, that my heart remained so insensible. Initially, I must confess, my vanity was merely elevated by it. Indifferent to her happiness, focused solely on my own amusement, yielding to habits I had long indulged, I strove by every means to please her without any design of reciprocating her affection."

At this point, Miss Dashwood turned her gaze upon him with angry contempt and interrupted, "It is hardly worth while, Mr. Willoughby, for you to recount, or for me to endure further. Such a beginning cannot be followed by anything acceptable. Do not pain me by hearing any more on this subject."

"I insist you hear the whole of it," he replied. "My fortune was never substantial, and I had always been extravagant, accustomed to associating with those of higher income. Every year since my majority, or perhaps before, had increased my debts. Though the death of my old cousin, Mrs. Smith, promised freedom, that event being uncertain and potentially distant, it had long been my intention to restore my circumstances by marrying a woman of fortune. To attach myself to your sister, therefore, was unthinkable. With a meanness, selfishness, and cruelty that no indignant or contemptuous look from you, Miss Dashwood, can condemn too harshly, I acted thus, striving to engage her regard without a thought of returning it. However, one thing may be said in my defense: even in that horrid state of selfish vanity, I did not comprehend the extent of the injury I meditated, because I did not *then* know what it was to love. But have I ever known it? Well may it be doubted; for had I truly loved, could I have sacrificed my feelings to vanity, to greed? Or, more importantly, could I have sacrificed hers? Yet, I have done it. To avoid a comparative poverty that her affection and society would have rendered unbearable, I raised myself to affluence only to lose everything that could make it a blessing."

"You did then," Elinor said, slightly softened, "believe yourself attached to her at one time?"

"To resist such attractions, to withstand such tenderness! Is there a man on earth capable of it? Yes, I found myself, gradually, sincerely fond of her; and the happiest hours of my life were those spent with her when I felt my intentions were strictly honorable and my feelings blameless. Even *then*, however, when fully determined to pay my addresses, I allowed myself most improperly to delay, day by day, the moment of declaration, unwilling to commit while my circumstances were so embarrased. I will not reason here—nor will I stop for *you* to expound on the absurdity, and worse-than-absurdity, of scrupling to pledge my faith where my honor was already bound. Events have proven I was a cunning fool, meticulously preparing for an opportunity to render myself contemptible and wretched forever. Eventually, however, my resolution was taken. I had decided that, as soon as I could engage her alone, I would justify the attentions I had invariably shown and openly assure her of an affection I had already displayed with such care. But in the interim—in the few hours before I could speak privately—a circumstance occurred, an unlucky one, to ruin all my resolution and my peace. A discovery was made," he hesitated, looking down. "Mrs. Smith had somehow, perhaps via a distant relation interested in depriving me of her favor, been informed of an affair, a connection—but I need not elaborate further," he added, gazing at her with flushed cheeks and an inquiring eye—"your particular intimacy. You have probably heard the whole story long ago."

"I have," returned Elinor, coloring and hardening her heart anew against any compassion for him. "I have heard it all. How you will explain away any part of your guilt in that dreadful business, I confess, is beyond my comprehension."

"Remember," cried Willoughby, "from whom you received the account. Could it be impartial? I acknowledge that her situation and character ought to have been respected by me. I do not mean to justify myself, but I cannot leave you to suppose that because she was injured she was irreproachable, and because *I* was a libertine, *she* must be a saint. If the violence of her passions, the

For some time after Willoughby departed, and indeed long after the rumble of his carriage faded into the distance, Elinor remained overwhelmed by a tumult of conflicting thoughts. While these ideas varied greatly in nature, they all converged upon a single, heavy emotion: sorrow. In this state, she found it impossible to turn her mind to her sister's welfare.

It was strange how quickly her perspective had shifted. Less than an hour prior, she had despised Willoughby as the most despicable of men. Now, despite his glaring faults, she felt a profound pity for the suffering they had caused him. This compassion led her to view him as forever severed from her family, evoking a tenderness and regret that she soon admitted to herself were driven more by his own desperate wishes than by any redeeming qualities he possessed. She recognized that his hold on her mind was strengthened by factors that reason dictated should carry no weight: his striking appearance, his open, affectionate, and vivacious demeanor—which were hardly virtues—and the lingering, passionate love he bore for Marianne, a sentiment that was scarcely innocent to harbor. Yet, she knew this influence would linger long before she could truly diminish it.

When she finally returned upstairs to find Marianne, she discovered her sister just awakening, revitalized by a deep and peaceful slumber that matched the height of her hopes. Elinor's heart swelled with emotion. The convergence of the past, the present, and the future—the memory of Willoughby's visit, Marianne's safe return, and her mother's imminent arrival—threw her into such an agitated state of spirits that she felt no fatigue, only a fierce determination not to reveal her turmoil to her sister.

However, this fear of exposure lasted barely half an hour. Soon, the sound of another carriage announced its approach downstairs. Driven by a desperate need to spare her mother any further moments of agonizing uncertainty, Elinor rushed into the hall and reached the outer door just in time to catch and steady her mother as she stumbled inside. Mrs. Dashwood, whose terror during the journey had nearly convinced her that Marianne was dead, possessed no voice for questions—not for Elinor, nor for anyone else. Instead, Elinor, offering neither greeting nor inquiry, immediately delivered the joyful news. Her mother seized this tidbit with her characteristic warmth, and in an instant, she was as overcome by happiness as she had been previously by fear. Supported between her daughter and her friend, Mrs. Dashwood shed tears of joy, though still unable to speak. She embraced Elinor repeatedly, then turned to press Colonel Brandon's hand, her gaze speaking volumes of gratitude and the certainty that he shared in their bliss. Brandon shared it, albeit in a silence even deeper than her own.

Once Mrs. Dashwood regained her composure, her first impulse was to see Marianne. Within two minutes, she was beside her beloved child, who had become dearer to her than ever through the trials of absence, unhappiness, and danger. Elinor's delight at witnessing the reunion was tempered only by the fear that it might rob Marianne of further rest. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood, capable of prudence when a child's life hung in the balance, remained calm. Satisfied by her mother's presence and aware of her own weakness, Marianne readily submitted to the enforced silence and quiet prescribed by the nurses surrounding her. Mrs. Dashwood insisted on staying awake with her throughout the night; consequently, Elinor, complying with her mother's plea, retired to her room.

But the rest that one sleepless night and hours of exhausting anxiety demanded was denied her by irritability of spirit. "Poor Willoughby" became a constant refrain in her thoughts. She found herself torn between wanting to hear his defense and blaming herself for judging him so harshly beforehand. Every thought oscillated between condemnation and acquittal. However, the promise she had made to relate his story to her sister remained a source of pain. She dreaded telling Marianne, fearing the impact it might have on her sister's heart. For a fleeting moment, she even wished Willoughby were a widower, hoping to spare him further grief. Then, recalling Colonel Brandon, she chided herself, realizing that the reward of her sister's affection belonged far more to Brandon's sufferings and constancy than to his rival's. She wished for nothing more than Mrs. Willoughby's survival.

The shock of Colonel Brandon's mission at Barton had been significantly softened for Mrs. Dashwood by her own preceding alarm. So great was her unease regarding Marianne that she had already resolved to travel to Cleveland that very day, without awaiting further news. She had arranged her journey so thoroughly that the Careys were expected to fetch Margaret away at any moment, as her mother refused to take her anywhere where infection might lurk.

Marianne improved daily, and the radiant cheerfulness of Mrs. Dashwood's countenance and spirits testified to her claim of being one of the happiest women in the world. Elinor could not listen to such declarations or witness their proof without occasionally wondering if her mother ever recalled Edward. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood, trusting in the temperate account Elinor had sent, was swept away by the exuberance of her joy, focusing solely on what would amplify it further. Marianne had been rescued from a peril in which, as she now realized, her own misguided judgment in fostering the attachment to Willoughby had played a part. Furthermore, in her recovery, Mrs. Dashwood found another source of joy entirely unexpected by Elinor.
