As soon as privacy allowed, the mother confided in her daughter. "At last we are alone, my Elinor. You do not yet know all my happiness. Colonel Brandon loves Marianne. He has told me so himself."

Her daughter, feeling a mixture of pleasure and pain, surprise and lack thereof, listened in total silence. "You are never like me, dear Elinor," her mother continued, "or I should marvel at your composure now. If I had sat down to wish for any possible good for my family, I would have chosen Colonel Brandon marrying one of you as the most desirable outcome. And I believe Marianne will be the happier of the two."

Elinor was half-inclined to ask why she thought so, knowing that no argument based on an impartial assessment of their ages, characters, or feelings could support such a notion. But her mother was always carried away by her imagination on matters of interest. Thus, Elinor merely smiled and let the question pass.

"He opened his whole heart to me yesterday while we traveled," Mrs. Dashwood explained. "It happened quite unexpectedly, quite unintentionally. I, you may believe, could talk of nothing but my child; he could not conceal his distress. I saw that his pain equaled my own. Perhaps, thinking that mere friendship—as the world currently defines it—would not justify such warm sympathy, or perhaps simply giving way to irresistible feelings, he made me acquainted with his earnest, tender, and constant affection for Marianne. He has loved her, my Elinor, since the very first moment he saw her."

Here, Elinor perceived that she was hearing not the actual words or professions of Colonel Brandon, but the natural embellishments of her mother's active fancy, fashioning everything to suit her delight. "His regard for her," Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed, "infinitely surpasses anything Willoughby ever felt or feigned. It is far warmer, more sincere, and more constant—however we choose to label it. It has endured through all knowledge of dear Marianne's unhappy infaturation with that worthless young man! Without selfishness, without encouraging false hope! Could he have seen her happy with another? Such a noble mind! Such openness, such sincerity! No one can be deceived by him."

"Colonel Brandon's character," Elinor interjected gently, "as an excellent man, is well established."

"I know it is," replied her mother seriously. "Or, after such a warning, I should be the last to encourage such affection, or even to be pleased by it. But his coming for me as he did, with such active and ready friendship, is sufficient proof that he is one of the worthiest of men."

"His character, however," answered Elinor, "does not rest on a single act of kindness, to which his affection for Marianne, were humanity absent, would have prompted him. To Mrs. Jennings, to the Middletons, he has been long and intimately known; they equally love and respect him. Even my own knowledge of him, though recently acquired, is considerable. I value and esteem him so highly that if Marianne can be happy with him, I shall be as ready as yourself to consider our connection the greatest blessing in the world. What answer did you give him? Did you allow him to hope?"

"Oh, my love!" cried her mother. "I could not then speak of hope to him or to myself. Marianne might have been dying at that moment. But he did not ask for hope or encouragement. His was an involuntary confidence, an irrepressible effusion to a soothing friend, not an application to a parent. Yet, after a time, I *did* say—for at first I was quite overcome—that if she lived, as I trusted she might, my greatest happiness would lie in promoting their marriage. Since our arrival, since our delightful security, I have repeated this to him more fully, giving him every encouragement in my power. 'Time,' I tell him, 'will do everything; Marianne's heart is not to be wasted forever on such a man as Willoughby. His own merits must soon secure it.'"

"To judge from the Colonel's

Marianne's illness, though severe in its nature, had not persisted long enough to hinder her recovery. Armed with youthful vigor, innate strength, and the devoted care of her mother, her convalescence progressed swiftly. Within four days of her mother's arrival, she was strong enough to transfer to Mrs. Palmer's dressing room. There, at Marianne's specific and eager request, Colonel Brandon was invited to pay her a visit. She was impatient to thank him personally for having fetched her mother.

Upon entering the room, the Colonel's reaction was profound. Seeing Marianne's altered appearance and receiving the pale hand she immediately extended to him, his emotion was palpable. Elinor observed that this distress stemmed from something deeper than mere affection or the knowledge that his love was known to others. Through the Colonel's melancholy gaze and shifting countenance as he regarded his sister-in-law, Elinor discerned the resurgence of past miseries. The resemblance between Marianne and the late Miss Grey, previously noted, was now intensified by Marianne's hollow eyes, sickly complexion, and posture of fragile weakness. These physical traits, combined with Marianne's warm expression of deep gratitude, stirred memories of sorrow that weighed heavily upon the Colonel's mind.

Mrs. Dashwood watched the scene with equal vigilance, yet her perspective differed greatly. While she perceived only the simplest, most natural emotions in the Colonel's behavior, she interpreted Marianne's actions and words as signaling the dawn of a sentiment greater than simple gratitude.

A day or two later, as Marianne showed visible signs of strengthening hourly, Mrs. Dashwood, driven by the shared desires of herself and her daughter, proposed moving to Barton Cottage. This decision would dictate the plans of her two friends: Mrs. Jennings could not leave Cleveland while the Dashwoods remained, and consequently, Colonel Brandon was soon persuaded by their united request to settle his residence there as firmly as theirs. In return, at the joint insistence of Mrs. Dashwood and Mrs. Jennings—who possessed an active goodness that made her hospitable to others as readily as to herself—the Colonel agreed to provide his carriage for Mrs. Dashwood's journey, ensuring better comfort for her sick child. Furthermore, he pledged to repay this kindness with a visit to the cottage within a few weeks.

The day of departure finally arrived. Marianne took a particularly long and heartfelt leave of Mrs. Jennings, expressing a gratitude so earnest, respectful, and filled with kind wishes that it seemed born of a secret acknowledgment of her own past neglects. She bid farewell to Colonel Brandon with the warmth of a true friend. With his careful assistance, he helped her into the carriage, ensuring she occupied at least half of the space. Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed, leaving the others to converse about the travelers and bask in their own quiet dullness until Mrs. Jennings was summoned to her carriage to enjoy the chatter of her maid regarding the loss of her young companions. Shortly thereafter, the Colonel departed alone toward Delaford.

The Dashwoods spent two days traveling, and Marianne endured the journey on both occasions without significant fatigue. Every effort made by their zealous companions to ensure her comfort was met with her physical ease and a remarkable calmness of spirit. This tranquility was especially gratifying to Elinor. Having witnessed her sister week after week suffering from anguished thoughts she lacked the courage to voice or the fortitude to hide, Elinor now rejoiced in Marianne's apparent composure. Trusting that this state resulted from serious reflection, she believed it would inevitably lead to contentment.

As they neared Barton and entered landscapes where every field and tree held painful memories, Marianne fell silent and thoughtful. Turning her face away from their gaze, she stared intently through the window. Elinor felt neither surprise nor blame; rather, when she saw Marianne cry as she was helped from the carriage, she viewed it merely as a natural emotion, too gentle to warrant anything less than pity, and praiseworthy for its restraint.

Throughout the remainder of her demeanor, Elinor detected a mind awakening to reasonable exertion. Hardly had they entered the sitting-room than Marianne surveyed the surroundings with a look of resolute determination, as if vowing to accustom herself to objects associated with the memory of Willoughby. She spoke little, yet every sentence aimed at cheerfulness. Though a sigh occasionally escaped her, it was invariably followed by a smile.

After dinner, she attempted to play the pianoforte. However, the sheet music resting on the stand was an opera procured by Willoughby, containing their favorite duets and inscribed on the outer leaf with his handwriting. "That will not do," she murmured. Shaking her head, she pushed the music aside. After pressing the keys briefly, she feigned weakness in her fingers and closed the instrument, firmly declaring her intention to practice diligently in the future.

The following morning brought no diminution of these hopeful signs. Strengthened by rest, she appeared and spoke with genuine spirit, looking forward to Margaret's return. She spoke of the reunited family circle, their shared pursuits, and cheerful society as the sole happiness worthy of desire.

"When the weather settles and I have regained my strength," she declared, "we shall take long walks daily. We will stroll to the farm at the edge of the downs to see how the children fare; we will visit Sir John's new plantations at Barton Cross and the Abbeylands; and we will frequently go to the ancient ruins of the Priory, attempting to trace its foundations as far as history tells us they once reached. I know we shall be happy. I know the summer will pass joyously. I intend never to rise later than six o'clock, dividing every moment thereafter between music and reading. I have formulated a plan and am determined to embark on a course of serious study. Our own library is too familiar to offer anything beyond amusement, but there are many valuable works at the Park, and others of modern origin which I know I can borrow from Colonel Brandon. By reading six hours a day, I shall acquire in a year a vast amount of instruction that I currently lack."

Elinor honored the nobility of such a plan, though she smiled to see the same eager imagination that had once led her sister to languid idleness and selfish complaining now working to introduce excess into a scheme of rational employment and virtuous self-discipline. Her smile faded into a sigh, however, when she recalled the unfulfilled promise to Willoughby. She feared communicating this news might unsettle Marianne's mind and destroy this fair prospect of tranquil industry. Resolving to postpone the inevitable confrontation until her sister's health was more secure, Elinor decided to wait. Yet, this resolution proved fleeting.

Marianne had been home for only a few days before the weather cleared sufficiently for an invalid to venture outdoors. Finally, a soft, genial morning broke, tempting both the daughter's wishes and the mother's confidence. Leaning on Elinor's arm, Marianne was permitted to walk as long as she could without fatigue along the lane before the house. Moving at a slow pace, dictated by her recent frailty, the sisters had barely advanced far enough to command a full view of the hill behind the house when Marianne paused. Gazing toward it, she calmly pointed with one hand.

"There, exactly there," she said, indicating a projecting mound. "On that spot I fell; and there I first saw Willoughby."

Her voice faltered on the name, but quickly recovering, she added hesitantly, "I am thankful to find I can look upon the place with so little pain! Shall we ever discuss that subject again, Elinor?"

Elinor gently encouraged her openness. "As for regret," Marianne continued, "I have done with that concerning *him*. I do not wish to recount what my feelings *were*, but what they *are* now. If I could be satisfied on one point—if I could believe he was not always acting a part, not always deceiving me; above all, if I could be assured he was never quite as wicked as my fears imagined, especially after the story of that unfortunate girl—" she stopped abruptly.

Elinor joyfully seized upon her words, answering, "If you could be assured of that, you would find peace."

"Yes. My peace of mind depends doubly on it. Not only is it horrifying to suspect someone, who has treated me as he did, of such designs, but what does it make me appear to myself? In a situation like mine, only a shamelessly unguarded affection could expose me to such consequences."

"How then," Elinor asked, "would you account for his behavior?"

"I would suppose him..." Marianne sighed, "Oh, how gladly would I suppose him... merely fickle. Very, very fickle."

Elinor said nothing. She debated internally whether to begin her narrative immediately or wait until Marianne was stronger. They walked on in silence for a few moments.

"I am not wishing him too much good," Marianne said finally, with a sigh. "May his secret reflections be no more unpleasant than my own. He will suffer enough in them."

"Do you compare your conduct with his?"

"No. I compare it with what it *ought* to have been; I compare it with yours."

"Our situations have borne little resemblance."

"They have borne more than our conduct. Do not, my dearest Elinor, let your kindness defend what your judgment must condemn. My illness has made me think. It has granted me leisure and calmness for serious recollection. Long before I was recovered enough to speak, I was fully capable of reflection. I considered the past; I saw in my own behavior, since the beginning of our acquaintance

Mrs. Dashwood listened to Willoughby's defense with a heavy heart. While she rejoiced that he was absolved of the specific charge laid against him, and felt genuine sorrow for his plight, she could not pretend to forgive him. The past remained an immutable barrier; no amount of current pleading could restore the unblemished faith Marianne once held, nor erase the pain she had endured through his actions. Nor could it wash away the stain of his treatment toward Eliza. Consequently, Willoughby could never regain his place in Marianne's esteem, nor could Colonel Brandon's reputation suffer further injury from his presence.

Had Mrs. Dashwood heard Willoughby's tale firsthand, witnessing his distress and believing the sincerity of his manner, her pity might have been deeper. Yet, unlike her daughter, she was not swayed by emotion alone. Elinor, guided by reflection and a sober judgment, refused to embellish the truth with tender fictions that might mislead. She sought only to state the facts clearly, allowing her mother to form her own conclusions based on reality rather than sentiment.

That evening, when the three women sat together, Marianne broke her silence regarding Willoughby. It required visible effort; her restlessness, flushed cheeks, and trembling voice betrayed the turmoil within. "I wish to assure you both," she declared, "that I see everything exactly as you desire."

Elinor, wishing to hear her sister's unbiased thoughts, signaled her mother to remain silent. Marianne continued slowly, her voice gaining a strange calmness. "What Elinor told me this morning has been a great relief. I have heard precisely what I needed to hear. I am now perfectly satisfied and wish for no change. I never could have been happy with him, even if I had known sooner what I must inevitably discover. There would have been no confidence, no esteem. Nothing could ever have bridged that gap."

"My dear Marianne!" cried her mother, tears in her eyes. "To find happiness with a man of libertine practices! With one who destroyed the peace of our dearest friend and the finest soul we know! No, my child, your sensitive conscience would never allow you to marry such a man."

Marianne sighed deeply. "I wish for no change."

"You view this matter," Elinor interjected gently, "with the clarity of a sound mind. You must realize, as I do, that your marriage would have guaranteed you endless trouble and disappointment, unsupported by any reliable affection. Had you wed him, poverty would have been your constant companion. His extravagance is admitted even by himself; his life proves he understands little of self-denial. Your limited income, combined with his demands and your own inexperience, would have plunged you into distresses unknown until then. Your strong sense of honor would have driven you to extreme economy, retrenching on your own comfort. Perhaps he would have tolerated this for a time, but eventually, faced with ruin, he would have resented your interference. Instead of winning his love, you would have diminished your influence, making him regret the very bond that trapped him in difficulty."

Marianne's lips quivered as she whispered, "Selfish?" implying, *Do you truly believe him selfish?*

"The entire course of his behavior," Elinor replied firmly, "was rooted in selfishness. From the moment he toyed with your affections, to the delay in confessing his engagement to another, to his sudden departure from Barton—it was all dictated by his own pleasure or convenience."

"It is true," Marianne agreed. "My happiness was never his objective."

"At present," Elinor continued, "he regrets his actions solely because they have failed to bring him personal satisfaction. Now that his financial burdens are lifted, he merely complains that he married a woman of a less agreeable temperament than yours. But does this mean he would have been happier with you? The hardships would differ. He would have suffered under the crushing weight of poverty, which he currently ignores. He would have had a wife of perfect temper, yet he would have been perpetually destitute. Soon, he would likely learn to value the comforts of wealth far more highly than the kindness of a partner."

"I have no doubt of it," Marianne said. "I regret nothing but my own foolishness."

"Perhaps you should blame your mother's imprudence, my child," Mrs. Dashwood offered softly. "She bears the responsibility."

Marianne stopped her mother before she could elaborate. Elinor, sensing that dwelling on the past would only weaken her sister's spirit, steered the conversation elsewhere. "There is one lesson we can draw from this tragedy," she said. "All of Willoughby's troubles stem from his initial violation of virtue in his dealings with Eliza Williams. That original sin spawned every subsequent failure and his current misery."

Marianne nodded in solemn agreement. This prompted Mrs. Dashwood to list Colonel Brandon's virtues and the wrongs done to him, speaking with warmth born of friendship and justice. However, Elinor noticed that Marianne barely seemed to listen. Over the next few days, Marianne's recovery stalled. Though she maintained a brave front, appearing cheerful and composed, Elinor trusted that time itself would heal her.

Margaret returned, and the family resettled quietly at the cottage. While their studies lacked the vigor of their earlier days at Barton, they planned to resume them with renewed energy. Meanwhile, Elinor grew increasingly anxious about Edward. Since leaving London, she had received no news of his plans or whereabouts. Letters from her brother John had confirmed only that they knew nothing of their "unfortunate" Edward and assumed he remained at Oxford—a fact never explicitly stated in subsequent correspondence.

Then, one morning, the household servant returned from Exeter. After answering questions about his errand, he volunteered a startling piece of news. "I suppose you know, ma'am, that Mr. Ferrars is married."

Marianne jumped violently, locking eyes with Elinor, who turned deathly pale. Before Elinor could react, Marianne collapsed into hysterics. Mrs. Dashwood, seeing the horror on her daughter's face, realized the full impact of the news. Distressed by both Marianne's breakdown and Elinor's shock, she struggled momentarily to decide whom to aid first. The servant, noticing Marianne's collapse, wisely summoned a maid. Together, they helped the hysterical girl to another room.

By the time Marianne had calmed slightly, her mother left her in Margaret's care and returned to Elinor. Though shaken, Elinor had regained her composure and began questioning the servant. Mrs. Dashwood took over the interrogation, sparing Elinor the strain.

"Who told you Mr. Ferrars was married, Thomas?"

"I saw Mr. Ferrars myself this morning in Exeter, along with his lady, Miss Steele," the servant explained. "They were waiting in a carriage at the New London Inn when I arrived with a message for her brother, a post-boy. As I passed, I recognized Miss Steele and tipped my hat. She greeted me warmly, asking after you and the young ladies, particularly Miss Marianne. She conveyed her and Mr. Ferrars' best wishes and expressed regret that they were in too much of a hurry to visit, noting they were traveling further west for a short while but promised to return and call."

"But did she mention the marriage?"

"Yes, ma'am. She smiled and said she had changed her name since visiting these parts. She is always so affable and straightforward. I congratulated her accordingly."

"Was Mr. Ferrars in the carriage?"

"Yes, ma'am. He leaned back, looking away. He is rarely talkative."

"Were there others?"

"No, just the two of them."

"Do you know where they came from?"

"They came straight from town, as Mrs. Ferrars told me."

"And are they heading west?"

"Yes, ma'am, but not to stay long. They'll be back soon, and then they'll surely call here."

Mrs. Dashwood glanced at Elinor, expecting her to ask for details, but Elinor knew better. She recognized the manipulative nature of Lucy's message; she was confident Edward would never approach them again. Speaking in a low voice to her mother, she noted they were likely heading to Mr. Pratt's near Plymouth.

Thomas's report ended there. Elinor appeared to want more, but the servant shook his head. "Did you see them off?"

"No, ma'am. The horses were just starting, but I couldn't wait longer; I feared being late."

"Did Mrs. Ferrars look well?"

"Yes, ma'am. She said she was very well. To me, she has always been a handsome young lady, and she seemed immensely content."

With no further questions arising, Thomas and the tablecloth were dismissed. Marianne had already informed them she could eat nothing. Both Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor lost their appetites. Margaret, however, felt fortunate; despite the emotional turmoil and the sisters' frequent neglect of proper meals, she had never gone hungry.

Once dessert and wine were served, and the servants withdrew, Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor sat in a profound silence, sharing a similar heaviness of thought. Mrs. Dashwood dared not speak, fearing any words might cause harm. She realized she had erred in trusting Elinor's earlier assurances. Everything had been softened then to protect Elinor from additional grief, especially since she was already suffering greatly for Marianne's sake. Mrs. Dashwood felt she had been misled by her daughter's careful demeanor into believing Elinor's attachment

Elinor discovered anew the profound distinction between anticipating a misfortune, however certain reason dictates it must be, and the crushing weight of absolute certainty. Until this moment, despite her better judgment, she had secretly harbored a hope whenever Edward remained unmarried—that fate might intervene to spare him the union with Lucy. She had convinced herself that some personal resolve, the intervention of friends, or a more advantageous prospect for the lady would inevitably emerge to safeguard everyone's happiness. But now, the marriage was done. And she bitterly reproached her own heart for the lingering flattery that had so acerbated the sting of this news.

It startled her initially that he had wed so swiftly, seemingly before he could take holy orders and thus possess the living. Yet, upon reflection, she realized how easily Lucy, driven by her own resourcefulness and eagerness to secure him, might have overlooked everything save the danger of delay. They were married in London and were now hastening toward her uncle's estate. Elinor tried to imagine Edward's feelings as he approached Barton, spotted her mother's servant, and received Lucy's message. Soon, she presumed, they would be settled at Delaford. The name sent a shiver through her; it was a place bound to her by deep ties, one she longed to know intimately yet desperately wished to avoid.

In her mind's eye, she pictured them in their new parsonage. She saw Lucy as the bustling, scheming manager, balancing a desire for fashionable appearance with extreme frugality, yet mortified if suspected of her penny-pinching ways. She envisioned Lucy pursuing her own interests in every thought, courting the favor of Colonel Brandon, Mrs. Jennings, and every wealthy acquaintance. As for Edward, she could discern nothing clearly, nor did she wish to. Whether he appeared happy or wretched mattered not; she turned her gaze away from every image of him.

She consoled herself with the belief that someone among their London connections would soon write to announce the event and provide details. Day after day passed, bringing no letter, no news. Though she knew no specific party was at fault, she found fault with every absent friend, labeling them thoughtless or indolent. "When do you write to Colonel Brandon, ma'am?" the question sprang from her own impatience, needing something tangible to occupy her thoughts. "I wrote to him last week, my dear, and I expect to see him rather than hear from him," her mother replied warmly. "I urged him to come here; I shall not be surprised if he walks in today or tomorrow." This offered a sliver of comfort—a person to anticipate. Colonel Brandon *must* possess information.

Scarcely had she resolved this when a figure on horseback caught her eye at the window. He halted at the gate. It was a gentleman, unmistakably Colonel Brandon. Her heart leaped; now she could learn the truth. But it was not him; neither his bearing nor his stature matched. Could it possibly be Edward? She glanced again. He had just dismounted. There was no mistaking it—it was Edward. She recoiled and sat down heavily. "He has come from Mr. Pratt's specifically to visit us," she whispered to herself. "I will remain calm; I will master myself."

Instantly, she sensed the others shared her confusion. She watched her mother and Marianne flush, exchange glances, and murmur softly. Oh, how she wished to speak, to assure them that no coldness or slight would mar their welcome to him—but her tongue failed her, leaving the matter entirely to their discretion. Silence hung heavy in the room as they awaited his arrival. Footsteps crunched on the gravel path; moments later, he stood before them.

His expression was not joyful, certainly not for Elinor. His face was pale with agitation, his demeanor suggesting fear of rejection and a consciousness that he deserved none. Mrs. Dashwood, trusting implicitly in her daughter's guidance, greeted him with a smile of forced cheerfulness, offering her hand and wishing him joy. Edward blushed deeply and stammered an unintelligible response. Elinor's lips had moved in unison with her mother's, and once the gesture was complete, she regretted not shaking his hand herself. Instead, she sat down, attempting to maintain an open countenance while discussing the weather. Marianne had withdrawn as far as possible to hide her distress, while Margaret, sensing partial knowledge but not the full picture, chose dignity, seating herself as distant as possible and maintaining a rigid silence.

When Elinor finally stopped praising the dryness of the season, an oppressive silence descended. Mrs. Dashwood broke it, hoping he had left Mrs. Ferrars well. In a hurried tone, he affirmed this. Another pause stretched out. Determined to act, though dreading the sound of her own voice, Elinor asked, "Is Mrs. Ferrars at Longstaple?"

"At Longstaple!" he exclaimed, surprised. "No, my mother is in town."

"I meant," Elinor continued, picking up some embroidery from the table, "to inquire for Mrs. *Edward* Ferrars." She dared not lift her eyes, yet both her mother and Marianne turned their gazes sharply upon him. He flushed, looking perplexed and doubtful. After a hesitant moment, he said, "Perhaps you mean... my brother? You mean Mrs.—Mrs. *Robert* Ferrars."

"Mrs. Robert Ferrars!" cried Marianne and her mother in tones of sheer astonishment. Even Elinor, unable to speak, stared at him with impatient wonder. Rising abruptly, he walked to the window, unsure of his next move. Picking up a pair of scissors lying nearby, he began snipping the sheath apart as he spoke in a frantic voice, "Perhaps you do not know; you may not have heard that my brother is lately married—to the youngest—to Miss Lucy Steele."

The words were echoed by everyone except Elinor. While the others reacted with shock, she sat with her head bowed over her work, trembling in an agony of emotion that left her unsure of her surroundings. "Yes," he added, "they were married last week and are now at Dawlish."

Elinor could endure no more. She nearly fled the room. Once the door clicked shut behind her, she collapsed into tears of pure joy, believing they would never cease. Edward, who had been looking anywhere but at her, noticed her rush away. Perhaps he saw, or even heard, her emotion; for immediately afterward, he fell into a deep reverie. No remark, no inquiry, no affectionate plea from Mrs. Dashwood could pierce his trance. Finally, without a word, he left the room and walked out toward the village, leaving the others in a state of bewildered astonishment at the sudden, miraculous turn of events—a confusion they could only attempt to soothe with wild conjecture.

Yet, however inexplicable the circumstances of his release might seem to the entire family, it was undeniable that Edward was free. The purpose of this newfound liberty was quickly decided upon by all: having endured the miseries of one imprudent engagement, contracted without his mother's consent and lasting over four years, nothing short of an immediate renewal of such a union could be expected of him upon its failure. His mission to Barton was straightforward: to ask Elinor to marry him. Given that he was not entirely inexperienced in making such a proposal, it was somewhat surprising that he felt so uneasy in this instance, requiring so much encouragement and fresh air to gather his courage. However, how swiftly he walked himself into the right resolve, how quickly an opportunity arose to act upon it, and how he was received, need not be detailed in detail. Suffice it to say that when the family sat down to dinner at four o'clock, roughly three hours after his arrival, he had secured his lady's hand, obtained his mother's consent, and was not only expressing himself as a rapturous lover but, in the reality of reason and truth, one of the happiest men alive.

His situation was indeed more than commonly joyful. He possessed more than the ordinary triumph of accepted love to swell his heart and lift his spirits. He was freed without reproach from an entanglement that had long been his misery, from a woman he had long ceased to love; and he was instantly elevated to a security with another that he would have deemed almost hopeless until recently, and which he now cherished with desperate desire. He was brought not from doubt or suspense, but directly from misery to happiness—a change openly expressed in a genuine, flowing, and grateful cheerfulness his friends had never witnessed before. His heart was now fully open to Elinor; all its weaknesses and errors were confessed, and his youthful infatuation with Lucy was treated with the philosophical dignity of a twenty-four-year-old reflecting on his past.

"It was a foolish, idle inclination on my part," he admitted. "It stemmed from ignorance of the world and a lack of occupation. Had my mother provided me with an active profession when I was removed at eighteen from Mr. Pratt's care, I am certain it would never have happened. Although I left Longstaple with what I then considered an unconquerable preference for his niece, had I possessed any pursuit, any object to engage my time and keep me at a distance from her for a few months, I should very soon have outgrown that fancied attachment, especially by mixing more with the world as I would have been forced to do. Instead, having nothing to do, no profession chosen for me, or allowed to choose one, I returned home to complete idleness. For the first twelve months thereafter, I lacked even the nominal employment that university membership would have provided, for I was not entered at Oxford until I was nineteen. Thus, I had nothing in the world to occupy me but to imagine myself in love. Since my mother did not make my home entirely comfortable, I had no friend in my brother, and I disliked new acquaintances, it was natural for me to spend much of my time at Longstaple, where I always felt at home and was sure of a welcome. Consequently, from eighteen to nineteen, I spent the majority of my time there. Lucy appeared to possess every amiable quality; she was pretty—at least, I thought so *then*—and having seen so little of other women, I could make no comparisons and discern no defects. Considering everything, I hope that although our engagement was foolish and has since been proven foolish in every way, it was not at the time an unnatural or inexcusable piece of folly."

The transformation wrought in a few hours upon the minds and hearts of the Dashwoods was so profound that it promised them all a sleepless night of joy. Mrs. Dashwood, too happy to be merely comfortable, struggled to express her love for Edward, to praise Elinor sufficiently, to thank him for his release without wounding his delicacy, and to balance granting them leisure for private conversation with enjoying the sight and society of both. Marianne could articulate her happiness only through tears. Comparisons inevitably arose, regrets surfaced, and her joy, though sincere in its love for her sister, was of a nature that gave her neither spirit nor words. But Elinor—how were her feelings to be described? From the moment she learned of Lucy's marriage and Edward's freedom until the moment he justified the hopes that had instantly followed, she was everything but tranquil. Yet, once the initial shock passed, finding every doubt and solicitude removed, comparing her current situation with what it had been so recently, seeing him honorably released from his former bond, and witnessing him instantly profit from that release to declare an affection as tender and constant as she had ever imagined possible, she was overwhelmed by her own felicity. Happily disposed as the human mind is to become easily accustomed to any change for the better, it took several hours to bring sedateness to her spirits or tranquility to her heart.

Edward was now settled at the cottage for at least a week; for regardless of other claims, it was impossible to dedicate less than a week to the enjoyment of Elinor's company or to discuss half of what needed saying regarding the past, present, and future. While a few hours of incessant talk might exhaust many topics between rational individuals, with lovers, no subject is ever truly finished, nor is any communication complete until it has been exchanged at least twenty times over. Lucy's marriage, an unceasing and reasonable wonder to everyone involved, naturally became one of their earliest discussions. Elinor's intimate knowledge of both parties made it appear to her, in every aspect, as one of the most extraordinary and unaccountable circumstances she had ever encountered. How they could have been thrown together, and by what attraction Robert could be drawn to marry a girl whose beauty she had heard him describe without admiration—a girl already engaged to his brother, on whose account that brother had been rejected by his family—it was beyond her comprehension. To her own heart, it was a delightful affair; to her imagination, even a ridiculous one; but to her reason and judgment, it was a complete puzzle.

Edward could only attempt an explanation by suggesting that, perhaps, an accidental meeting initially allowed the vanity of one to be worked upon by the flattery of the other, leading gradually to all subsequent events. Elinor recalled what Robert had told her in Harley Street regarding his opinion of how his own mediation in his brother's affairs might have helped if applied in time. She repeated this to Edward. "That was exactly like Robert," he observed immediately. "And that," he added shortly after, "might perhaps have been in his mind when the acquaintance between them first began. And Lucy, perhaps, at first, might have thought only of securing his good offices in my favor. Other designs might have arisen later."

How long the secret had been carried on between them, however, he was just as lost as she was in determining; for at Oxford, where he had remained voluntarily since leaving London, he had had no means of hearing of her except through her own letters, which to the very last had been neither less frequent nor less affectionate than usual. Therefore, not the slightest suspicion had ever prepared him for what followed. When it finally burst upon him via a letter from Lucy herself, he had been, for some time, he believed, half stupefied between wonder, horror, and the joy of such a deliverance. He handed the letter to Elinor.

>
> Being very sure I have long lost your affections, I have thought myself at liberty to bestow my own on another, and have no doubt of being as happy with him as I once used to think I might be with you; but I scorn to accept a hand while the heart belongs to another. I sincerely wish you happy in your choice, and it shall not be my fault if we are not always good friends, as our near relationship now makes proper. I can safely say I owe you no ill-will, and am sure you will be too generous to do us any ill offices. Your brother has gained my affections entirely, and as we could not live without one another, we are just returned from the altar, and are now on our way to Dawlish for a few weeks, which place your dear brother has great curiosity to see, but thought I would first trouble you with these few lines, and shall always remain,
>
> Your sincere well-wisher, friend, and sister,
>
> I have burnt all your letters, and will return your picture the first opportunity. Please to destroy my scrawls—but the ring with my hair you are very welcome to keep.

Elinor read it and returned it without comment. "I will not ask your opinion of it as a composition," said Edward. "For worlds, I would not have had a letter of hers seen by *you* in former days. In a sister, it is bad enough, but in a wife! How I have blushed over the pages of her writing! And I believe I may say that since the first half-year of our foolish business, this is the only letter I ever received from her of which the substance made me any amends for the defect of the style."

"However it may have come about," said Elinor after a pause, "they are certainly married. And your mother has brought upon herself a most appropriate punishment. The independence she settled on Robert, through resentment against you, has placed it in his power to make his own choice; and she has actually been bribing one son with a thousand-a-year to do the very deed which she disinherited the other for intending to do

After a spirited and steadfast refusal on Mrs. Ferrars's part—a resistance fierce enough to shield her from the dreaded accusation of being "too amiable"—Edward was finally granted an audience and reinstated as her son. Of late, the Ferrars household had undergone a series of dramatic fluctuations. For many years, Mrs. Ferrars had boasted of two sons; yet the recent social death of Edward had deprived her of one, and the earlier demise of Robert had left her bereft of both for a fortnight. Now, with Edward miraculously resurrected, she possessed a son once more.

Despite this restoration, Edward did not feel his existence truly secure until he disclosed his current engagement. He feared that revealing his intention to marry Miss Dashwood might shock his constitution so violently that he would vanish as quickly as he had returned. Thus, with cautious trepidation, he unveiled the news, only to find Mrs. Ferrars listening with an unexpected calm. Initially, she attempted to dissuade him, deploying every argument at her disposal. She urged him toward Miss Morton, describing her as a lady of superior rank and substantial fortune. She emphasized that Miss Morton was the daughter of a nobleman holding thirty thousand pounds, whereas Miss Dashwood was merely the offspring of a private gentleman possessing a mere three thousand. However, upon realizing that Edward, while acknowledging the factual disparity, showed no inclination to be swayed by it, she deemed further opposition futile. Drawing wisdom from past experiences, she chose submission. Following a delay calculated to preserve her own dignity and avoid appearing overly eager, she formally decreed her consent to the union of Edward and Elinor.

The next matter to address was the augmentation of their income. Here, the hierarchy of the family became clear: although Edward was now her sole surviving son, he was not her eldest. While Robert was destined for a yearly allowance of one thousand pounds, no objection was raised against Edward accepting holy orders for a modest stipend of two hundred and fifty pounds. Beyond the ten thousand pounds already gifted with Fanny, no further promises were made, present or future. Yet, this sum proved ample for Edward and Elinor's desires, exceeding their expectations. Even Mrs. Ferrars, through her evasive excuses, appeared the only one surprised that she offered no more.

With a sufficient income secured pending Edward's installation in his living, the couple awaited only the completion of the parsonage at Barton. Colonel Brandon, eager to accommodate Elinor, was diligently overseeing significant renovations. After enduring the customary delays and disappointments typical of such projects, Elinor, breaking her firm resolve to postpone marriage until everything was perfect, proceeded with the wedding in Barton Church early in the autumn.

Their first month of wedded bliss was spent at the Mansion-House with their friend, allowing them to supervise the parsonage's progress and design its layout—from selecting wallpaper to planning shrubberies and fountains. By Michaelmas, Mrs. Jennings visited the newlyweds in their parsonage, declaring, with genuine belief, that they were one of the happiest couples in the world. Indeed, they lacked for nothing save the marriage of Colonel Brandon and Marianne, and perhaps slightly better grazing for their cows.

They were showered with visits from nearly all their relations and friends. Mrs. Ferrars arrived to inspect the happiness she had reluctantly authorized, and even the Dashwoods undertook a journey from Sussex to pay their respects. As Mr. John Dashwood walked with his sister-in-law near Delaford House, he remarked, "I shall not claim to be disappointed, my dear sister—that would be saying too much, for you have undoubtedly been one of the most fortunate young women in the world. Nevertheless, I confess it would bring me great pleasure to call Colonel Brandon my brother. His estate, his residence, and his grounds are in such impeccable condition! His woods alone surpass anything I have seen in Dorsetshire. Though Marianne may not appear the ideal match for him, I believe it would be wise to invite them to stay frequently. Since Colonel Brandon seems so comfortable here, who knows what might happen? You can easily highlight her virtues, and so on. Simply put, you should give her a chance; you understand."

Mrs. Ferrars did visit, treating them with a facade of decent affection, yet she never bestowed upon them the genuine favor and preference reserved for others. Such treatment was the exclusive domain of Robert and his wife, whose fortunes rose swiftly. Lucy's sharp self-interest, which initially drew Robert into trouble, ultimately facilitated his escape from it. Her humble demeanor, attentive service, and ceaseless flattery, once given the slightest opportunity, won back Mrs. Ferrars's heart and fully restored Robert to her grace. Consequently, Lucy's entire conduct during the affair, culminating in its success, stands as a testament to how relentless pursuit of self-interest, despite apparent obstacles, secures every material advantage, requiring only the sacrifices of time and conscience.

When Robert first sought Lucy's acquaintance in Bartlett's Buildings, his motive, as perceived by his brother, was merely to persuade her to break her engagement. Expecting that a few conversations would suffice to overcome their mutual affection, he miscalculated. Lucy, however, played along, offering hope that his eloquence would eventually win her over. Each subsequent meeting required additional persuasion to dispel lingering doubts. This prolonged courtship secured his attendance, and the rest followed naturally. Their conversations shifted from Edward to Robert, a topic on which he spoke endlessly and in which she displayed equal enthusiasm. Soon, it became evident that he had entirely supplanted his brother. He boasted of his triumph over Edward, of deceiving him, and of marrying privately without maternal consent.

What ensued is well known. They enjoyed several months of great happiness at Dawlish, where she entertained numerous relatives and acquaintances, while he drafted plans for magnificent cottages. Upon returning to London, they secured Mrs. Ferrars's forgiveness simply by asking for it—an act initiated by Lucy. Initially, this pardon extended only to Robert. Lucy, owing no filial duty to her mother and thus committing no breach thereof, remained unpardoned for some weeks. However, her persistent humility, self-reproach regarding Robert's offense, and feigned gratitude for her mother's harshness eventually won her the haughty attention that softened into affection and influence. Lucy became indispensable to Mrs. Ferrars, ranking alongside Robert and Fanny. While Edward was never warmly forgiven for his past intentions, and Elinor, despite her superior fortune and birth, was often dismissed as an interloper, Lucy was regarded as a favorite child in all things.

Settling in London, they received generous aid from Mrs. Ferrars and maintained excellent relations with the Dashwoods. Aside from the petty jealousies between Fanny and Lucy (which their husbands naturally exacerbated) and occasional domestic squabbles between Robert and Lucy, their lives were marked by remarkable harmony. How Edward forfeited his rights as the eldest son, and how Robert succeeded to them, remains a puzzle; yet the arrangement was justified by its results. Robert showed no sign of regretting his wealth or feeling guilty about his brother's reduced share. Similarly, Edward, judging by his diligent performance of duties, deepening love for his wife and home, and cheerful disposition, appeared thoroughly content with his lot, harboring no desire for exchange.

Elinor's marriage kept her closely connected to her family without rendering the cottage at Barton obsolete, as her mother and sisters spent much of their time there. Mrs. Dashwood acted on both principle and pleasure in visiting Delaford so frequently; her desire to unite Marianne and Colonel Brandon was even more fervent than John's had been. It became her cherished goal. Though precious to her was the company of her daughter, she yearned to see Marianne settled at the mansion-house, a wish shared equally by Edward and Elinor. They understood each other's sorrows and obligations, and by universal agreement, Marianne was to be the reward for all their efforts.

Faced with such a united front, coupled with intimate knowledge of Colonel Brandon's goodness and a realization of his profound, albeit belated, attachment to herself, what choice did Marianne have? Marianne Dashwood was destined for an extraordinary fate. She was born to disprove her own beliefs and contradict her most cherished principles. Born to overcome an affection formed late in life—at seventeen, based solely on strong esteem and lively friendship—and voluntarily offer her hand to another! Moreover, this other man was one who had suffered deeply from a previous attachment, whom she had deemed too old to marry just two years prior, and who still clung to the comforting habit of wearing a flannel waistcoat.

Yet, so it happened. Instead of succumbing to an irresistible passion, or retiring forever to her mother's side to find joy in solitude and study—as she had later resolved in calmer moments—she found herself at nineteen submitting to new bonds, embracing new responsibilities, establishing a new home, becoming a wife, mistress of a family, and patroness of a village.

Colonel Brandon was now as happy as those who loved him best believed he deserved. In Marianne, he found solace for every past sorrow; her regard and companionship revived his spirit and brought cheerfulness back to his life. Equally, Marianne discovered her own happiness in contributing to his. Every observing friend agreed. Marianne could never love partially; in time, her whole heart became as devoted to her husband as it once had been to Willoughby.

Willoughby could not hear of her marriage without pain, and his punishment was soon completed by the voluntary forgiveness of Mrs. Smith. By attributing her clemency to Willoughby's marriage to a woman of character, she gave him reason to believe that had he acted honorably toward Marianne, he might have achieved both happiness and
